# Practical English — A Working Reference for Portuguese Speakers

> **What this document is:** a reference for *using* English — reading, writing, speaking, and being understood.
> **What this document is not:** a grammar course, a CEFR syllabus, or a list of every rule that exists.

---

## How to Use This Document

This is **not meant to be read once from start to finish**. It is meant to be searched, skimmed, and returned to.

Three ways to use it:

1. **Look something up.** Use the table of contents. Sections 16 (confusing words), 26 (Portuguese-speaker mistakes), 27 (false friends), and 35 (tables) are the ones you will return to most.
2. **Fix a specific weakness.** If your listening is weak, read sections 18 and 19. If your writing sounds "translated," read sections 20, 26, and 29.
3. **Read section 36 first** if you want the highest-value 10 minutes. It is a summary of everything.

### Conventions used here

| Symbol | Meaning |
|---|---|
| ❌ | Wrong, or so unnatural that it marks you as a non-native speaker |
| ⚠️ | Grammatically possible but unnatural, or correct but risky |
| ✅ | Natural, what people actually say |
| 🗣️ | Spoken English only — don't write this in formal contexts |
| ✍️ | Written/formal English |
| 🇧🇷 | Specific interference from Portuguese |
| 🇺🇸 / 🇬🇧 | American vs British difference that actually matters |

### The three-tier priority system

Throughout the document, grammar and vocabulary are marked:

- **ESSENTIAL** — you cannot communicate well without this. Learn it properly.
- **USEFUL** — you will need it regularly. Learn it after the essentials.
- **LATER** — real English, but low frequency. Recognize it; don't drill it.

If you are short on time, do the **ESSENTIAL** items until they are automatic. A person with perfect Present Simple, Past Simple, Present Perfect, articles, and 500 collocations communicates better than a person who knows the Third Conditional and hesitates for five seconds per sentence.

# 1. How English Actually Works

Before any grammar rule, understand the **shape** of English. Five facts explain most of the difficulty a Portuguese speaker has.

## 1.1 The five facts

### Fact 1 — Word order carries the meaning

Portuguese has rich verb endings and flexible order. English has almost no endings, so **position = meaning**.

```text
The dog bit the man.     ≠     The man bit the dog.
```

In Portuguese you can say *"Comprei um carro ontem"*, *"Ontem comprei um carro"*, *"Um carro, comprei ontem"* — all fine. In English, moving the object around usually breaks the sentence.

❌ A car I bought yesterday. (only in poetry or strong contrast)
✅ I bought a car yesterday.

**The default order is almost sacred:**

```text
SUBJECT + VERB + OBJECT + (MANNER) + (PLACE) + (TIME)
```

- I sent the report quickly to the client this morning.
- She fixed the bug carefully on the server last night.

### Fact 2 — The subject is almost always required

Portuguese drops the subject constantly (*"Está chovendo"*, *"Fui ao mercado"*). English cannot.

| Portuguese | ❌ Literal | ✅ English |
|---|---|---|
| Está chovendo. | Is raining. | **It** is raining. |
| Fui ao mercado. | Went to the market. | **I** went to the market. |
| É importante. | Is important. | **It**'s important. |
| Tem muita gente aqui. | Have a lot of people here. | **There are** a lot of people here. |
| Dizem que... | Say that... | **They** say that... / **It is said** that... |

English even invents subjects when there is no real one: **it** and **there**. These are called "dummy subjects," and you need them constantly.

- **It**'s cold. / **It**'s 5 o'clock. / **It**'s two hours by car. / **It** seems fine.
- **There**'s a problem. / **There** were three people. / **There** used to be a bakery here.

> 🇧🇷 **The #1 structural mistake:** confusing *ter* (to have) with *haver/existir* (there is). Portuguese uses *tem* for both.
>
> ```text
> Tem um problema no servidor.
>         ↓
> ❌ Have a problem in the server.
>         ↓
> ✅ There's a problem with the server.
> ```

### Fact 3 — English is built on auxiliary verbs

To make a question or a negative, English does not change word order the way Portuguese does with intonation. It **adds an auxiliary verb** — a helper verb with little meaning of its own.

The helpers: **do / does / did**, **be**, **have**, **will**, and the modals (**can, should, would**...).

```text
Statement:  You    work    here.
Question:   Do     you     work here?
Negative:   You    don't   work here.
```

Once there is already an auxiliary in the sentence, you **don't add `do`** — you reuse the one that is there.

| Statement | Question | Negative |
|---|---|---|
| She **is** working. | **Is** she working? | She **isn't** working. |
| They **have** finished. | **Have** they finished? | They **haven't** finished. |
| He **will** call. | **Will** he call? | He **won't** call. |
| You **can** help. | **Can** you help? | You **can't** help. |
| It **works**. *(no auxiliary)* | **Does** it work? | It **doesn't** work. |
| It **worked**. *(no auxiliary)* | **Did** it work? | It **didn't** work. |

This one table explains questions, negatives, short answers, and tag questions. It is the single most profitable table in English grammar.

### Fact 4 — The auxiliary carries the tense, not the main verb

When you add `do/does/did`, the **main verb goes back to base form**. Portuguese speakers routinely double-mark the tense.

❌ Did you went to the meeting?
✅ **Did** you **go** to the meeting?

❌ She doesn't works here.
✅ She **doesn't work** here.

❌ Where did he bought it?
✅ Where **did** he **buy** it?

> Think of it as: **the tense is used up by the auxiliary.** Only one verb in the sentence gets to be marked.

### Fact 5 — English separates *what you say* from *how you say it*

The same idea has a neutral form, a polite form, and a casual form. Choosing wrong doesn't break grammar — it breaks the social situation.

| Situation | Version |
|---|---|
| Direct/neutral | Send me the file. |
| Polite request | Could you send me the file? |
| Very polite | Would you mind sending me the file? |
| Casual 🗣️ | Can you shoot me that file? |
| Formal ✍️ | Please find the file attached. / I would appreciate it if you could send the file. |

Portuguese uses tone and *por favor* for this. English uses **grammatical structures** (modals, questions, past forms) to signal politeness. Using the imperative in an email sounds much harsher in English than *"Me manda o arquivo"* does in Portuguese.

## 1.2 Basic sentence structure

### The core patterns

**Pattern 1 — Subject + Verb**

```text
S + V
```
- The server crashed.
- She left.
- It works.

**Pattern 2 — Subject + Verb + Object** *(the most common pattern in English)*

```text
S + V + O
```
- I use Linux.
- We finished the sprint.
- They hired two developers.

**Pattern 3 — Subject + `be` + Complement**

```text
S + be + adjective / noun
```
- The API is slow.
- She is a designer.
- This is important.

**Pattern 4 — Subject + Verb + Indirect Object + Direct Object**

```text
S + V + person + thing
S + V + thing + to/for + person
```
- I sent **him the report**. = I sent **the report to him**.
- She bought **me a coffee**. = She bought **a coffee for me**.

> ⚠️ When the direct object is a pronoun, use the `to/for` version:
> ❌ Send me it. → ✅ **Send it to me.**

**Pattern 5 — Subject + Verb + Object + Complement**

```text
S + V + O + adjective/noun
```
- That makes **it easier**.
- We call **this approach "lazy loading"**.
- Keep **the door open**.

### There is / There are — ESSENTIAL

```text
There is  + singular / uncountable
There are + plural
```

- There's a bug in production.
- There are three open tickets.
- There isn't enough time.
- Is there any coffee left?
- There used to be a meeting on Fridays.
- There's going to be a delay.

🗣️ In fast speech, people often say *"There's three people here"* — technically wrong, extremely common. Recognize it; write `there are`.

## 1.3 Questions — the mechanism

Three shapes cover ~95% of questions.

**A. Yes/No question — invert or add `do`**

```text
(Auxiliary) + Subject + Verb ... ?
```
- **Are** you free tomorrow?
- **Do** you have a minute?
- **Have** you seen this error before?
- **Can** we move the meeting?

**B. WH question — question word first, then the same structure**

```text
WH-word + Auxiliary + Subject + Verb ... ?
```
- **Where do** you work?
- **When did** they release it?
- **Why is** he calling?
- **How long have** you been here?

**C. Subject questions — no auxiliary at all**

When the question word **is** the subject, don't add `do`.

- **Who** called you? *(not: Who did call you?)*
- **What** happened? *(not: What did happen?)*
- **Which team** won?

> 🇧🇷 The most common Portuguese-speaker error in questions is **keeping statement order**:
> ```text
> Onde você mora?
>         ↓
> ❌ Where you live?
>         ↓
> ✅ Where do you live?
> ```

## 1.4 Negative sentences — the mechanism

**Rule:** put `not` after the first auxiliary. If there is no auxiliary, bring in `do/does/did`.

| Tense | Negative |
|---|---|
| Present Simple | I **don't** know. / He **doesn't** know. |
| Past Simple | We **didn't** finish. |
| `be` | It **isn't** ready. / They **weren't** there. |
| Present Perfect | She **hasn't** replied. |
| Future | I **won't** be late. |
| Modal | You **shouldn't** do that. |

**One negative per clause.** Portuguese doubles negatives (*"Não vi ninguém"*). English does not.

```text
Não vi ninguém.
        ↓
❌ I didn't see nobody.
        ↓
✅ I didn't see anyone.  /  ✅ I saw no one.
```

Pattern to memorize: **negative verb + any-word**, or **positive verb + no-word**. Never both negatives.

## 1.5 Auxiliary verbs — the four workhorses

### `do / does / did` — ESSENTIAL

Used for questions and negatives with **all normal verbs** (any verb except `be` and modals).

- Do you **like** it? / He doesn't **like** it. / Did they **like** it?

Also used for **emphasis** (very useful, underused by learners):

- I **do** understand your point. *(= I really do)*
- She **did** send the email — check your spam.
- 🗣️ "Do come in!" / "I did tell you."

And for **short answers and echoes**:

- "Do you work here?" — "Yes, I **do**."
- "I love this tool." — "**Do** you?" *(= really?)*
- "I don't like it." — "Neither **do** I." / "Me neither."

### `be` — ESSENTIAL

Three jobs:

1. **Linking verb:** She **is** a lawyer. It **is** cold.
2. **Continuous tenses:** They **are** working. He **was** sleeping.
3. **Passive voice:** The file **was** deleted.

Forms: *am / is / are / was / were / been / being*.

> 🇧🇷 Portuguese has *ser* and *estar*; English has only **be**. That is easier. But the reverse causes a problem: Portuguese uses *estar com* + noun for states where English uses **be** + adjective.
>
> | Portuguese | ❌ | ✅ |
> |---|---|---|
> | Estou com fome | I have hunger / I'm with hunger | I'**m hungry** |
> | Estou com frio | I have cold | I'**m cold** |
> | Estou com medo | I have fear | I'**m scared / afraid** |
> | Tenho 30 anos | I have 30 years | I'**m 30 (years old)** |
> | Estou com sono | I have sleep | I'**m sleepy / tired** |
> | Você tem razão | You have reason | You'**re right** |
> | Estou com pressa | I'm with hurry | I'**m in a hurry** |

### `have` — ESSENTIAL

Two different jobs — do not confuse them:

1. **Main verb (possess):** I **have** a car. → question: *Do you have a car?*
2. **Auxiliary (perfect tenses):** I **have** finished. → question: *Have you finished?*

```text
Do you have time?      ← "have" = possess → needs "do"
Have you had lunch?    ← first "have" = auxiliary, second = main verb
```

### `will` — ESSENTIAL

Marks the future and decisions made at the moment of speaking. Never changes form; the verb after it is always base form.

- I **will call** you. / I'**ll call** you.
- ❌ I will to call you. ❌ He will calls.

## 1.6 Modal verbs — the quick view

`can, could, may, might, must, should, would, will, shall`

Three rules that never change:

1. **No `to` after them:** ✅ I can swim. ❌ I can to swim.
2. **No `-s` in third person:** ✅ She can. ❌ She cans.
3. **They are their own auxiliary:** Can you? / He can't.

Full treatment in [Section 5](#5-modal-verbs).

## 1.7 Contractions

Contractions are **normal, neutral English** — not slang. Native speakers use them in speech constantly and in most writing (including business email). Avoiding them makes you sound stiff or angry.

| Full | Contraction | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| I am | I'm | |
| you are / we are / they are | you're / we're / they're | |
| he is / she is / it is | he's / she's / it's | also = *he has* etc. |
| I have / you have | I've / you've | |
| I had / I would | I'd | context decides |
| I will | I'll | |
| do not | don't | |
| does not | doesn't | |
| did not | didn't | |
| cannot | can't | |
| will not | **won't** | irregular — memorize |
| would not | wouldn't | |
| should not | shouldn't | |
| is not | isn't | |
| are not | aren't | |
| was not / were not | wasn't / weren't | |
| have not / has not | haven't / hasn't | |
| there is | there's | |
| let us | let's | |

**Two contraction traps:**

- `it's` = *it is / it has*. `its` = possessive. **The apostrophe never means possession for `its`.**
  - ✅ It's raining. / The company lost its data.
- `'s` can be *is*, *has*, or possessive: *He's late* (is), *He's left* (has), *John's car* (possessive). Context tells you.

**When NOT to contract:** very formal documents, legal text, and — importantly — **at the end of a sentence in a positive short answer**:

- ❌ "Are you ready?" — "Yes, I'm."
- ✅ "Yes, I **am**."
- ✅ "No, I'**m not**." *(negative contraction at the end is fine)*

## 1.8 Short answers — ESSENTIAL

English does **not** answer questions by repeating the verb the way Portuguese does (*"Você vai?" — "Vou."*). It repeats the **auxiliary**.

```text
Question auxiliary → same auxiliary in the answer
```

| Question | Yes | No |
|---|---|---|
| Do you work here? | Yes, I do. | No, I don't. |
| Did she call? | Yes, she did. | No, she didn't. |
| Are you coming? | Yes, I am. | No, I'm not. |
| Is it ready? | Yes, it is. | No, it isn't. |
| Have you eaten? | Yes, I have. | No, I haven't. |
| Can you help? | Yes, I can. | No, I can't. |
| Will you be there? | Yes, I will. | No, I won't. |
| Would you like coffee? | Yes, please. | No, thanks. |

> 🇧🇷 ❌ "Do you like it?" — "Yes, I like." → ✅ "Yes, I **do**." (or just "Yes." / "Yeah, I love it.")

**Related high-frequency structures:**

- **Agreeing (positive):** "I'm tired." — "**So am I.**" / 🗣️ "**Me too.**"
- **Agreeing (negative):** "I don't like it." — "**Neither do I.**" / 🗣️ "**Me neither.**"
- **Tag questions:** You're coming, **aren't you**? / He didn't call, **did he**? *(positive statement → negative tag, and vice versa)*

## 1.9 Formal vs informal English — a practical map

Most learners overestimate how formal English is. Business English in the US and UK is **much more casual** than business Portuguese.

| Idea | Formal ✍️ | Neutral (safe everywhere) | Informal 🗣️ |
|---|---|---|---|
| Ask | I would like to request... | Could you...? | Can you...? / Mind doing X? |
| Say sorry | I apologize for the inconvenience. | Sorry about that. | My bad. |
| Say thanks | Thank you for your assistance. | Thanks a lot. | Thanks! / Cheers 🇬🇧 |
| Get info | Please advise. | Let me know. | Lemme know. |
| Start email | Dear Mr. Silva, | Hi John, | Hey! |
| End email | Kind regards, | Best, / Thanks, | Cheers, / — |
| Say no | I'm afraid that won't be possible. | Sorry, I can't. | No way. |
| Explain | Due to the fact that... | Because... | 'Cause... |
| Big words | commence, utilize, purchase, assist | start, use, buy, help | — |

> **Practical rule:** in professional English, prefer the **neutral** column. Formal English written by a non-native speaker often sounds like a 1950s letter. "Please find attached herewith" is not better English than "Here's the file."

**Signals of informal English:** contractions, phrasal verbs, `get`, short sentences, `gonna/wanna`, sentence-initial *And/But/So*.
**Signals of formal English:** full forms, Latin-origin verbs (*receive, obtain, require*), passive voice, no phrasal verbs, `whom`, longer sentences.

---

# 2. Essential Parts of Speech

Only what changes what you say. Terminology is kept to the minimum needed to talk about the rules.

## 2.1 Nouns

**What they do:** name things, people, ideas — *server, meeting, Ana, freedom*.

### The four things that actually matter

**1. Countable or uncountable** (see [Section 9](#9-countable-and-uncountable-nouns)) — this decides `a/an`, `some`, `many/much`, and plural `-s`.

**2. Plural formation**

| Rule | Examples |
|---|---|
| + `-s` | file → files, meeting → meetings |
| ends in *s, sh, ch, x, z* → + `-es` | box → boxes, watch → watches, bus → buses |
| consonant + `y` → `-ies` | company → companies, city → cities |
| vowel + `y` → just `-s` | day → days, key → keys |
| some `-f/-fe` → `-ves` | life → lives, knife → knives, shelf → shelves |
| irregulars (memorize) | man→men, woman→women, child→children, person→**people**, foot→feet, tooth→teeth, mouse→mice |
| no change | sheep, fish, series, species, aircraft |
| always plural | people, police, clothes, glasses, scissors, jeans, headphones |

**3. Possessive `'s`**

```text
person/animal + 's + thing        → my boss's laptop, the dog's name
plural ending in s + '            → the students' results
thing (non-living) → use "of"     → the name of the file / the file name
```

- ✅ Ana's report / the team's decision / today's meeting
- ⚠️ the report of Ana → unnatural. Use **Ana's report**.
- ✅ the end of the file *(things prefer `of`)*

**4. Noun + noun (compound nouns)** — extremely common and very useful

English stacks nouns where Portuguese uses *de*:

| Portuguese | ❌ Literal | ✅ Natural |
|---|---|---|
| relatório de vendas | report of sales | **sales report** |
| reunião de equipe | meeting of team | **team meeting** |
| banco de dados | bank of data | **database** |
| curso de inglês | course of English | **English course** |
| carteira de motorista | license of driver | **driver's license** |
| taxa de conversão | rate of conversion | **conversion rate** |

**Order:** the **last** noun is the real thing; earlier nouns describe it. *A car key* is a key. *A key car* would be a car.
**The first noun stays singular:** ❌ a shoes shop → ✅ a **shoe** shop; ❌ two hours meeting → ✅ a **two-hour** meeting.

> 🇧🇷 **Common mistake:** using an adjective as a noun. English usually needs a noun.
> ❌ The importants are here. → ✅ The **important ones** are here.
> ❌ I need a professional advice. → ✅ I need **professional advice** *(uncountable)* / **some advice**.

## 2.2 Pronouns

Words that replace nouns — *I, me, my, mine, myself, this, someone*. Full treatment in [Section 3](#3-pronouns). The one thing to note here: **English keeps the pronoun; Portuguese drops it.**

❌ Is a good idea. → ✅ **It**'s a good idea.
❌ When I finished, sent it. → ✅ When I finished, **I** sent it.

## 2.3 Verbs

**What they do:** express actions (*run, build*), processes (*become*), and states (*be, know, own*).

### The forms you need for every verb

| Form | Regular (*work*) | Irregular (*go*) | Used for |
|---|---|---|---|
| base | work | go | after *to*, after modals, in `do` questions, imperatives |
| 3rd person `-s` | works | goes | Present Simple with he/she/it |
| past | worked | **went** | Past Simple |
| past participle | worked | **gone** | perfect tenses, passive |
| `-ing` | working | going | continuous tenses, gerund |

> **The 3rd-person `-s` is the most-forgotten rule by every learner.** It carries almost no meaning, so people drop it. Native speakers notice immediately. `He work here` sounds clearly wrong; `He works here` is correct.

### Irregular verbs that actually matter

You don't need all 200. These ~50 cover the vast majority of real usage:

| Base | Past | Participle | | Base | Past | Participle |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| be | was/were | been | | leave | left | left |
| become | became | become | | let | let | let |
| begin | began | begun | | lose | lost | lost |
| break | broke | broken | | make | made | made |
| bring | brought | brought | | mean | meant | meant |
| build | built | built | | meet | met | met |
| buy | bought | bought | | pay | paid | paid |
| catch | caught | caught | | put | put | put |
| choose | chose | chosen | | read | read *(red)* | read *(red)* |
| come | came | come | | run | ran | run |
| cost | cost | cost | | say | said | said |
| do | did | done | | see | saw | seen |
| drive | drove | driven | | sell | sold | sold |
| eat | ate | eaten | | send | sent | sent |
| fall | fell | fallen | | set | set | set |
| feel | felt | felt | | sit | sat | sat |
| find | found | found | | speak | spoke | spoken |
| forget | forgot | forgotten | | spend | spent | spent |
| get | got | got/gotten 🇺🇸 | | stand | stood | stood |
| give | gave | given | | take | took | taken |
| go | went | gone | | teach | taught | taught |
| grow | grew | grown | | tell | told | told |
| have | had | had | | think | thought | thought |
| hear | heard | heard | | understand | understood | understood |
| keep | kept | kept | | wear | wore | worn |
| know | knew | known | | win | won | won |
| lead | led | led | | write | wrote | written |

### State verbs — verbs that resist `-ing` — USEFUL

Some verbs describe states, not actions. They are normally **not** used in continuous tenses:

*know, understand, believe, remember, forget, mean, need, want, like, love, hate, prefer, own, belong, seem, cost, contain, depend, matter, agree, hear, see (= perceive)*

- ❌ I am knowing the answer. → ✅ I **know** the answer.
- ❌ I am wanting a coffee. → ✅ I **want** a coffee.
- ❌ Are you understanding? → ✅ **Do you understand?**

Exceptions you will hear (they change meaning to an *action*):
- I'**m thinking** about it. *(mental process — OK)* vs. I **think** it's fine. *(opinion — never `-ing`)*
- I'**m having** lunch. *(= eating — OK)* vs. I **have** a car. *(possession — never `-ing`)*
- 🗣️ "I'm loving it." — marketing/casual, technically an exception.

## 2.4 Auxiliary Verbs

Covered in [1.5](#15-auxiliary-verbs--the-four-workhorses). The mental model:

```text
Auxiliary = the part of the verb that handles grammar
Main verb = the part that handles meaning
```

Questions, negatives, short answers, tags, and emphasis all attach to the **auxiliary**.

## 2.5 Modal Verbs

Cover ability, permission, obligation, possibility, and politeness. See [Section 5](#5-modal-verbs).

## 2.6 Adjectives

**What they do:** describe nouns — *fast, expensive, useful*.

### Three practical rules

1. **They go BEFORE the noun** (opposite of Portuguese default).
   ```text
   um carro vermelho → ❌ a car red → ✅ a red car
   uma ideia interessante → ✅ an interesting idea
   ```
2. **They never take a plural `-s`.**
   ❌ two importants meetings → ✅ two **important** meetings
3. **They also go after `be`, `seem`, `look`, `feel`, `sound`, `become`, `get`.**
   - The API is **slow**. / It looks **expensive**. / She seems **busy**. / It's getting **late**.

Full treatment: [Section 10](#10-adjectives).

## 2.7 Adverbs

**What they do:** describe verbs, adjectives, or whole sentences — *quickly, always, very, unfortunately*.

Most are formed with `-ly`: *quick → quickly, easy → easily, careful → carefully*.

**Irregular but essential:** *good → **well***, *fast → **fast***, *hard → **hard***, *late → **late**, early → early*.

- ❌ She speaks English good. → ✅ She speaks English **well**.
- ❌ He drives very fastly. → ✅ He drives very **fast**.

Full treatment (including position, which is where mistakes happen): [Section 12](#12-adverbs).

## 2.8 Articles

`a`, `an`, `the`, or nothing. Small words, huge error rate for Portuguese speakers, because Portuguese uses articles far more often than English.

```text
Eu gosto de café.    → ❌ I like the coffee.  → ✅ I like coffee.
O Brasil é grande.   → ❌ The Brazil is big.  → ✅ Brazil is big.
A vida é curta.      → ❌ The life is short.  → ✅ Life is short.
```

Full treatment: [Section 8](#8-articles).

## 2.9 Determiners

Words that come before a noun and specify it: *the, a, this, my, some, any, each, every, both, all, no, another, other*.

**Key rule: only one determiner slot.** You cannot stack them the way Portuguese does.

```text
o meu carro → ❌ the my car → ✅ my car
todos os meus amigos → ❌ all the my friends → ✅ all my friends
```

**Order when combining:** `all/both/half` + `the/my/this` + number + adjective + noun
- **all my** old files / **both the** teams / **half the** budget / **all three** options

## 2.10 Prepositions

Small words showing relationships in time, space, and abstraction: *in, on, at, to, for, with, about, from, by, of*.

**Preposition choice is mostly memorized, not derived.** They almost never map 1:1 to Portuguese.

| Portuguese | ❌ Literal | ✅ English |
|---|---|---|
| depende de | depend of | depend **on** |
| casar com | marry with | marry ∅ *(marry someone)* |
| chegar em | arrive in | arrive **at/in** |
| pensar em | think in | think **about/of** |
| sonhar com | dream with | dream **about/of** |
| na TV | in the TV | **on** TV |
| de manhã | in the morning ✅ | in the morning |
| responsável por | responsible for ✅ | responsible **for** |

Full treatment: [Section 13](#13-prepositions).

## 2.11 Conjunctions

Words that join: *and, but, or, so, because, although, while, if, when*.

Two types worth distinguishing:

- **Coordinating** (join equals): *and, but, or, so, yet, for, nor* — often preceded by a comma when joining two full sentences.
  - It's cheap**, but** it works.
- **Subordinating** (make one clause depend on another): *because, although, if, when, while, since, unless, after, before*.
  - **Although** it's cheap, it works. / It works **although** it's cheap.

> 🇧🇷 **Never use both halves.** Portuguese allows *"Embora seja barato, mas funciona"* in speech. English does not.
> ❌ Although it's cheap, but it works. → ✅ **Although** it's cheap, it works. / It's cheap, **but** it works.

Full treatment: [Section 14](#14-the-most-important-english-connectors).

## 2.12 Quantifiers

How much / how many: *some, any, much, many, a lot of, few, little, several, plenty of, enough, no*.

The key split is **countable vs uncountable**:

| | Countable | Uncountable |
|---|---|---|
| Question/negative | many | much |
| Positive | a lot of / lots of / plenty of | a lot of / lots of / plenty of |
| Small amount (positive) | a few | a little |
| Small amount (negative) | few | little |

Full treatment: [Section 9](#9-countable-and-uncountable-nouns).

## 2.13 Expressions and fixed phrases

The single highest-value "part of speech" for a learner. English is full of fixed chunks that you must store whole — changing one word makes them wrong.

| Fixed phrase | Meaning | Note |
|---|---|---|
| by the way | mudando de assunto | 🗣️ + ✍️ |
| as far as I know | pelo que eu sei | hedging |
| to be honest | pra ser sincero | softener |
| in my opinion | na minha opinião | ✍️ |
| at the moment | no momento | |
| in the meantime | enquanto isso | |
| on the other hand | por outro lado | |
| as soon as possible (ASAP) | o quanto antes | |
| it depends | depende | never "it depends of" |
| never mind | deixa pra lá | 🗣️ |
| no worries / no problem | sem problema | 🗣️ |
| makes sense | faz sentido | often without subject 🗣️ |
| fair enough | justo / entendi | 🗣️ |
| let me know | me avisa | |
| I'll get back to you | te retorno | professional |
| that said | dito isso | |
| in that case | nesse caso | |
| just in case | por via das dúvidas | |
| on purpose | de propósito | |
| at least | pelo menos | |
| at the same time | ao mesmo tempo | |
| in advance | com antecedência | Thanks in advance. |

Learn these as **single units**. Do not translate them word by word.

---

# 3. Pronouns

Small set, high frequency, and a very common source of errors that sound bad even when the meaning survives.

## 3.1 The full map

| Subject | Object | Possessive adjective | Possessive pronoun | Reflexive |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| I | me | my | mine | myself |
| you | you | your | yours | yourself |
| he | him | his | his | himself |
| she | her | her | hers | herself |
| it | it | its | — | itself |
| we | us | our | ours | ourselves |
| you (pl.) | you | your | yours | yourselves |
| they | them | their | theirs | themselves |

Learn this table by **rows**, not columns: *I – me – my – mine – myself*.

## 3.2 Subject vs object pronouns — `I` × `me`

```text
Before the verb → subject (I, he, she, we, they)
After the verb or after a preposition → object (me, him, her, us, them)
```

- **I** called **her**. / **She** helped **us**. / **They** work with **him**.
- Give it to **me**. / It's for **them**. / Between you and **me**.

**The two situations where learners get it wrong:**

**1. After a preposition — always object form.**
- ❌ between you and I → ✅ between you and **me**
- ❌ for she and I → ✅ for **her** and **me**
- ✅ This is a message from **him** and **me**.
> Native speakers also make the "you and I" mistake, so you'll *hear* it. It's still wrong in writing.

**2. In compound subjects — always subject form.**
- ❌ Me and him went to the meeting. 🗣️ *(very common in speech, wrong in writing)*
- ✅ **He and I** went to the meeting.
> **Test:** remove the other person. *"Me went"* is obviously wrong → so it's *"I went"* → *"He and I went."*

**Short answers 🗣️:** "Who wants coffee?" — "**Me!**" (not "I!"). "I'm tired." — "**Me too**." These are fixed conversational forms; they're fine.

## 3.3 `it` — the pronoun Portuguese doesn't have

`it` = things, animals (unknown/unimportant sex), situations, weather, time, distance, and **dummy subject**.

- The server is down. **It** crashed at 3 a.m.
- **It**'s raining. / **It**'s Monday. / **It**'s 10 km from here.
- **It**'s hard to explain. / **It** seems like a bug. / **It** doesn't matter.

> 🇧🇷 **Two mistakes here:**
> 1. **Dropping it:** ❌ Is very late. → ✅ **It**'s very late.
> 2. **Using `he/she` for objects:** Portuguese has grammatical gender (*a mesa → ela*), English does not.
>    ❌ I bought a new laptop. She is fast. → ✅ **It**'s fast.
>    *(Exception: ships, cars, and pets sometimes get `she`/`he` affectionately. Not required.)*

**Also:** `they/them/their` is now standard for a single person of unknown or unspecified gender.
- If **someone** calls, tell **them** I'll call **them** back.
- Each user must reset **their** password.

## 3.4 Possessive adjective vs possessive pronoun — `my` × `mine`

```text
Possessive adjective + NOUN     → my car, your idea, their team
Possessive pronoun (NO noun)    → It's mine. Is this yours? Theirs is better.
```

| ✅ | ❌ |
|---|---|
| This is **my** laptop. | This is my. |
| This laptop is **mine**. | This laptop is my. / This is the mine. |
| **Your** point is valid. | Yours point is valid. |
| That desk is **yours**. | That desk is your. |
| **Her** name is Ana. | Hers name is Ana. |
| The idea was **hers**. | The idea was her. |

**Three specific traps:**

1. **`his` is both.** *His car is here. / That car is his.*
2. **`its` (possessive) vs `it's` (it is).** *The app lost **its** data.* / *It's slow.*
3. **No article before possessives:** ❌ the my car → ✅ **my** car. To combine, use `of mine`:
   - ✅ a friend **of mine** (= um amigo meu)
   - ✅ that colleague **of yours**

> 🇧🇷 Portuguese uses possessives for body parts and family more than English needs. English also uses possessives where Portuguese uses articles:
> ```text
> Ele quebrou o braço.
>         ↓
> ❌ He broke the arm.
>         ↓
> ✅ He broke his arm.
> ```

## 3.5 Reflexive pronouns

Used when the subject and object are the same person, or for emphasis.

- I hurt **myself**. / She taught **herself** Python. / Help **yourselves**.
- I did it **myself**. *(emphasis = eu mesmo)*
- **by myself** = alone / without help: *I live by myself. I fixed it by myself.*

> 🇧🇷 **Portuguese over-uses reflexives.** Many Portuguese reflexive verbs are NOT reflexive in English:
>
> | Portuguese | ❌ | ✅ |
> |---|---|---|
> | Eu me chamo Ana | I call myself Ana | My name **is** Ana / I'**m** Ana |
> | Ele se levantou | He got up himself | He **got up** |
> | Eu me sinto bem | I feel myself well | I **feel** good/fine |
> | Sente-se | Sit yourself | **Sit down** |
> | Eles se casaram | They married themselves | They **got married** |
> | Ela se lembrou | She remembered herself | She **remembered** |
> | Concentre-se | Concentrate yourself | **Focus** / Concentrate |
> | Nós nos vemos amanhã | We see ourselves tomorrow | **See you** tomorrow |
>
> For "each other" use **each other**, not a reflexive: *They help **each other*** (not *themselves*).

## 3.6 Demonstratives — `this` × `that` × `these` × `those`

| | Near (here / now / just mentioned) | Far (there / then / earlier) |
|---|---|---|
| Singular | **this** | **that** |
| Plural | **these** | **those** |

- **This** is my desk. / **That** building over there is ours.
- **These** files are new. / **Those** were the old requirements.

**Practical uses beyond physical distance:**

- **Time:** *this week* (current), *that week* (past/other). *this morning* vs *that morning*.
- **Referring back:** *"The API is down." — "**That**'s a problem."* → `that` refers to something already said. `this` introduces something new.
- **On the phone 🇺🇸:** "Hi, **this is** Ana." (never *"I am Ana"* or *"Here is Ana"*)
- 🗣️ **that** as an intensifier: *"It's not that hard."* (= não é tão difícil assim)
- 🗣️ **those** = "the ones": *Those who arrive late will wait.* ✍️

**Pronunciation warning:** `this` /ðɪs/ vs `these` /ðiːz/ — short vs long vowel + s/z sound. Many learners pronounce both the same, causing real confusion between singular and plural.

## 3.7 Indefinite pronouns — `some-` / `any-` / `no-` / `every-`

|  | people | things | places |
|---|---|---|---|
| **some-** (positive) | someone, somebody | something | somewhere |
| **any-** (question/negative) | anyone, anybody | anything | anywhere |
| **no-** (negative meaning, positive verb) | no one, nobody | nothing | nowhere |
| **every-** (all) | everyone, everybody | everything | everywhere |

### The core rule

```text
Positive statement  → some-
Negative & question → any-
Negative verb + any-  =  positive verb + no-
```

- There's **someone** at the door.
- Is there **anyone** here? / I don't see **anyone**.
- I saw **no one**. = I didn't see **anyone**.
- ❌ I didn't see nobody. *(double negative)*

### The exceptions that matter

**1. `any` in a positive sentence = "it doesn't matter which."**
- Ask **anyone** — they'll all say the same thing.
- You can call me **anytime**.
- **Anything** is better than this.

**2. `some` in questions = offers and requests (when you expect "yes").**
- Would you like **something** to drink?
- Could you give me **some** help?

**3. They are grammatically SINGULAR.**
- ❌ Everyone are here. → ✅ **Everyone is** here.
- ❌ Nobody know. → ✅ **Nobody knows**.
- ✅ Everything **is** ready. / Someone **has** left a message.

But the follow-up pronoun is usually **they/them/their**:
- ✅ Everyone brought **their** laptop.
- ✅ If anyone calls, tell **them** I'm busy.

**4. Adjectives go AFTER them** (the opposite of normal adjective order).
- ✅ something **new**, anything **else**, someone **important**, nothing **special**, somewhere **quiet**
- ❌ new something / else anything
- *This is one of the few places where an English adjective follows the word it describes.*

> 🇧🇷 `anyone/anything` do not mean *"qualquer um"* in questions and negatives — there they simply mean *"alguém/algo"*. The "qualquer" meaning only appears in positive sentences.
> - Do you need anything? = *Você precisa de alguma coisa?* (not "qualquer coisa")
> - Take anything you want. = *Pegue qualquer coisa que quiser.*

## 3.8 Quick self-test

Fix these:

1. ❌ Me and my colleague finished the report.
2. ❌ This is a friend of me.
3. ❌ Is raining, so we cancelled.
4. ❌ Everyone are ready?
5. ❌ I didn't tell nothing to nobody.
6. ❌ She hurt herself the hand.
7. ❌ The book is very good. She has 300 pages.
8. ❌ Where is the my phone?

**Answers:** 1. My colleague and I... 2. ...a friend **of mine**. 3. **It's** raining... 4. **Is everyone** ready? 5. I didn't tell **anything to anyone**. 6. She hurt **her hand**. 7. **It** has 300 pages. 8. Where is **my** phone?

---

# 4. Verbs — The Essential System

## 4.0 Priority: not all tenses are equal

English "has 12 tenses." In real usage, a small subset does most of the work.

| Tense | Priority | Rough share of real usage |
|---|---|---|
| Present Simple | **ESSENTIAL** | very high |
| Past Simple | **ESSENTIAL** | very high |
| Present Continuous | **ESSENTIAL** | high |
| `will` future | **ESSENTIAL** | high |
| `be going to` | **ESSENTIAL** | high |
| Present Perfect | **ESSENTIAL** | high — and the hardest for PT speakers |
| Modals (can/should/would/could) | **ESSENTIAL** | very high |
| Past Continuous | **USEFUL** | medium |
| Present Perfect Continuous | **USEFUL** | medium |
| Past Perfect | **USEFUL** | low-medium |
| 1st/2nd Conditional | **USEFUL** | medium |
| Future Continuous | **LATER** | low |
| Future Perfect | **LATER** | very low |
| Past Perfect Continuous | **LATER** | very low |
| 3rd/Mixed Conditional | **LATER** | low |

> **Practical consequence:** if you speak with confident Present Simple, Past Simple, Present Continuous, `will`/`going to`, Present Perfect and modals, you sound competent. Adding Future Perfect Continuous adds almost nothing.

---

## 4.1 Present Simple — ESSENTIAL

**Meaning:** facts, habits, routines, permanent situations, schedules, and — very importantly — **opinions and states right now**.

**Pattern:**

```text
+   I/you/we/they + VERB          He/she/it + VERB-s
–   I don't + VERB                He doesn't + VERB
?   Do I/you/we/they + VERB?      Does he/she/it + VERB?
```

**Examples:**

- I **work** for a fintech.
- She **manages** three teams.
- The office **opens** at 8.
- Water **boils** at 100°C.
- I **don't drink** coffee.
- **Does** this **work** on mobile?
- The train **leaves** at 6:15. *(schedule = future meaning)*

**Signal words:** always, usually, often, sometimes, never, every day/week, on Mondays, twice a month.

**Spelling of the `-s` form:** *go → goes, do → does, watch → watches, study → studies, have → **has***.

**Common mistakes:**

❌ He work here. → ✅ He **works** here.
❌ She doesn't works here. → ✅ She doesn't **work** here.
❌ Do he like it? → ✅ **Does** he like it?
❌ I am agree. → ✅ I **agree**. *(agree is a verb, not an adjective — huge PT mistake)*
❌ I am needing help. → ✅ I **need** help.

> 🇧🇷 **The state-verb trap.** Portuguese happily says *"Estou precisando"*, *"Estou querendo"*, *"Estou achando"*. English uses Present Simple:
> ```text
> Estou achando que não vai dar.
>         ↓
> ❌ I'm thinking that it won't work.
>         ↓
> ✅ I don't think it'll work. / I think it won't work.
> ```

---

## 4.2 Present Continuous — ESSENTIAL

**Meaning:** (1) happening right now, (2) temporary situations around now, (3) **planned future arrangements**, (4) changing trends, (5) complaints with *always*.

**Pattern:**

```text
+   Subject + am/is/are + VERB-ing
–   Subject + am/is/are + not + VERB-ing
?   Am/Is/Are + Subject + VERB-ing?
```

**Examples:**

- I'**m working** from home today. *(temporary)*
- He'**s presenting** right now. *(this moment)*
- We'**re meeting** the client on Thursday. *(future arrangement — very common)*
- Prices **are rising**. *(trend)*
- She'**s always interrupting** me. 🗣️ *(complaint)*
- **Are** you **coming** to the retro?

**Signal words:** now, right now, at the moment, today, this week, currently, these days.

**Spelling of `-ing`:** *make → making* (drop e), *run → running* (double consonant), *lie → lying*.

### Present Simple vs Present Continuous — the key distinction

| Present Simple | Present Continuous |
|---|---|
| always / generally true | temporary / right now |
| I **work** in São Paulo. *(my job)* | I'**m working** in São Paulo. *(this month)* |
| He **speaks** three languages. | He'**s speaking** to a client. |
| What **do** you **do**? *(job)* | What **are** you **doing**? *(right now)* |
| The app **crashes** on startup. *(always)* | The app **is crashing** a lot lately. *(recent pattern)* |
| I **live** in Lisbon. | I'**m living** with my parents until June. |

> **The most useful pair in the language:** *What do you do?* = "Qual é sua profissão?" / *What are you doing?* = "O que você está fazendo?"

---

## 4.3 Past Simple — ESSENTIAL

**Meaning:** finished action at a finished time. If the time is over, use Past Simple.

**Pattern:**

```text
+   Subject + VERB-ed / irregular past
–   Subject + didn't + BASE VERB
?   Did + Subject + BASE VERB?
```

**Examples:**

- We **deployed** the fix yesterday.
- She **went** to Berlin last year.
- I **didn't see** your message.
- **Did** you **talk** to him?
- The meeting **started** late and **ended** at 7.

**Signal words:** yesterday, last week/month/year, ago, in 2019, when I was a kid, then, this morning *(if the morning is over)*.

**`-ed` pronunciation (3 ways — matters for listening):**

| Ending sound of the verb | `-ed` sounds like | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| t / d | **/ɪd/** — extra syllable | wanted, needed, started, decided |
| voiceless (p, k, f, s, sh, ch) | **/t/** | worked, stopped, watched, fixed |
| everything else (voiced) | **/d/** | called, played, opened, tried |

> Learners often add an extra syllable everywhere (*"work-ed"*), which sounds odd. Only after **t/d** is `-ed` a separate syllable.

**Common mistakes:**

❌ I didn't went. → ✅ I **didn't go**.
❌ Did you saw it? → ✅ **Did you see** it?
❌ Yesterday I have finished. → ✅ Yesterday I **finished**.
❌ I was work there. → ✅ I **worked** there.

---

## 4.4 Past Continuous — USEFUL

**Meaning:** an action in progress at a past moment; background for another event; two simultaneous actions.

**Pattern:**

```text
Subject + was/were + VERB-ing
```

**Examples:**

- I **was working** when the power went out.
- What **were** you **doing** at 10 p.m.?
- They **were arguing** while I **was trying** to concentrate.
- Sorry, I **wasn't paying** attention.

**The classic combination:** long action (Past Continuous) interrupted by short action (Past Simple), joined by *when/while*.

```text
While I was driving,   my phone rang.
      [long action]    [short action]
```

- **When** + Past Simple (short) / **While** + Past Continuous (long)

---

## 4.5 Present Perfect — ESSENTIAL and hardest

This is the biggest source of error for Portuguese speakers, because Portuguese *pretérito perfeito composto* ("tenho feito") works differently.

**Meaning:** a past action **connected to now**. Three main uses:

1. **Experience** (when doesn't matter): *I'**ve been** to Japan.*
2. **Result now**: *I'**ve lost** my keys.* (→ I don't have them now)
3. **Unfinished time period**: *I'**ve had** three meetings today.* (today isn't over)

**Pattern:**

```text
+   Subject + have/has + PAST PARTICIPLE
–   Subject + haven't/hasn't + PAST PARTICIPLE
?   Have/Has + Subject + PAST PARTICIPLE?
```

**Examples:**

- **Have** you **finished** the report?
- She'**s worked** here for six years. *(and still does)*
- We'**ve just deployed** the new version.
- I'**ve never used** Kubernetes.
- They **haven't replied** yet.
- This is the best coffee I'**ve ever had**.

**Signal words:** ever, never, already, yet, just, so far, recently, lately, since, for, this week/year, still (in negatives).

### Past Simple vs Present Perfect — the decisive rule

```text
Is the TIME finished?  → Past Simple
Is the TIME still open, or is the time irrelevant?  → Present Perfect
```

| Past Simple | Present Perfect |
|---|---|
| I **saw** him yesterday. | I'**ve seen** him. *(sometime, recently)* |
| She **worked** here for 5 years. *(she left)* | She'**s worked** here for 5 years. *(still here)* |
| **Did** you **eat**? *(at lunch, which is over)* | **Have** you **eaten**? *(today / are you hungry now)* |
| We **launched** it in March. | We'**ve launched** the beta. *(news, result now)* |
| I **lost** my phone last week. | I'**ve lost** my phone. *(right now I can't find it)* |

**Absolute rule:** a specific finished time expression (**yesterday, last week, in 2020, two days ago, when I was young**) forces **Past Simple**.

❌ I have finished it yesterday. → ✅ I **finished** it yesterday.
❌ He has called me last night. → ✅ He **called** me last night.
❌ When have you arrived? → ✅ **When did you arrive?**

> 🇧🇷 **The `for/since` trap.** Portuguese uses the *present* for ongoing duration; English uses the Present Perfect.
> ```text
> Trabalho aqui há cinco anos.
>         ↓
> ❌ I work here since five years.  /  ❌ I am working here for 5 years.
>         ↓
> ✅ I've worked here for five years.
> ✅ I've been working here for five years.
> ```
>
> Same for: *Moro aqui há...* → *I've lived here for...* / *Conheço ele há...* → *I've known him for...*

**`for` vs `since`:**

```text
for   + a period of duration   → for two hours, for a week, for ages, for a long time
since + a starting point        → since 2020, since Monday, since I was 20, since this morning
```

**🇺🇸 vs 🇬🇧 note:** Americans often use Past Simple where the British use Present Perfect: *"Did you eat yet?"* 🇺🇸 vs *"Have you eaten yet?"* 🇬🇧. Both are understood everywhere. Use the Present Perfect rules above and you will be correct in both varieties.

---

## 4.6 Present Perfect Continuous — USEFUL

**Meaning:** an activity that started in the past and is **still going on** (or just stopped), with focus on the **duration or the activity itself**, not the result.

**Pattern:**

```text
Subject + have/has + been + VERB-ing
```

**Examples:**

- I'**ve been working** on this bug all morning.
- **How long have** you **been learning** English?
- She'**s been trying** to reach you.
- Sorry I'm sweaty — I'**ve been running**.
- We'**ve been getting** a lot of complaints lately.

### Present Perfect vs Present Perfect Continuous

| Present Perfect (result) | Present Perfect Continuous (activity/duration) |
|---|---|
| I'**ve written** three emails. *(finished, countable)* | I'**ve been writing** emails all day. *(activity)* |
| She'**s read** the report. *(done)* | She'**s been reading** it. *(maybe not finished)* |
| We'**ve fixed** it. *(result)* | We'**ve been fixing** bugs since Monday. *(ongoing)* |

**Practical shortcut:**
- Focus on **how many / what's done** → Present Perfect.
- Focus on **how long / what I've been busy with** → Present Perfect Continuous.
- With **live, work, study, feel** the two are basically interchangeable: *I've lived here for 5 years* = *I've been living here for 5 years*.
- **State verbs never take the continuous:** ❌ I've been knowing him for years. → ✅ I'**ve known** him for years.

---

## 4.7 Past Perfect — USEFUL

**Meaning:** the past before the past. Use it only when you need to make the **order** clear.

**Pattern:**

```text
Subject + had + PAST PARTICIPLE
```

**Examples:**

- When I arrived, the meeting **had** already **started**.
- She told me she **had never used** Docker.
- The server crashed because we **hadn't tested** the release.
- By 2020, they **had opened** ten offices.

**Reality check:** in everyday speech, people often just use Past Simple with *before/after*, because the order is already clear:
- ✅ I **had eaten** before he arrived.
- ✅ 🗣️ I **ate** before he arrived. *(equally natural)*

Use Past Perfect when there is no other clue about the order, in storytelling, and in reported speech.

---

## 4.8 Future — `will` vs `be going to` — ESSENTIAL

Both talk about the future. Choosing between them is about **when you decided** and **what evidence you have**.

**Patterns:**

```text
will:        Subject + will + BASE VERB          (I'll, he'll, won't)
going to:    Subject + am/is/are + going to + BASE VERB
```

| Use `will` | Use `be going to` |
|---|---|
| Decision made **now**, while speaking | Plan/intention decided **before** |
| Prediction based on opinion/belief | Prediction based on **present evidence** |
| Promises, offers, refusals | Something already arranged |
| "OK, I'**ll** send it right now." | "I'**m going to** send it after lunch." *(already planned)* |
| "I think it'**ll** rain tomorrow." | "Look at those clouds — it'**s going to** rain." |
| "I'**ll** help you." *(offer)* | "We'**re going to** hire two people." *(decided)* |
| "That'**ll** be $20." | "He'**s going to** quit." *(I know his plan)* |

**Other future forms you actually need:**

- **Present Continuous for fixed arrangements** (very common, often better than both):
  *I'**m meeting** the client at 3.* / *We'**re flying** to Madrid on Sunday.*
- **Present Simple for timetables:** *The flight **leaves** at 7:40.*
- 🗣️ **gonna** = *going to* in speech. *"I'm gonna check."* Understand it always; write `going to`.

**Common mistakes:**

❌ I will to call you. → ✅ I **will call** you.
❌ He wills come. → ✅ He **will** come.
❌ I go to send it tomorrow. → ✅ I'**m going to send** it tomorrow.
❌ When I will arrive, I'll call. → ✅ **When I arrive**, I'll call. *(no `will` after `when/if/as soon as/before/after/until`)*

> 🇧🇷 That last one is a top-5 mistake. Portuguese uses the future subjunctive (*"Quando eu chegar"*), which feels like a future. English uses the **present**:
> ```text
> Quando eu chegar, eu te ligo.
>         ↓
> ❌ When I will arrive, I will call you.
>         ↓
> ✅ When I arrive, I'll call you.
> ```
> Same with: *If it rains...*, *As soon as he calls...*, *Before you leave...*, *Until I finish...*

---

## 4.9 Future Continuous and Future Perfect — LATER

**Future Continuous** — `will be + -ing`. Two real uses:
- In progress at a future time: *This time tomorrow I'**ll be flying** to Chicago.*
- **Polite, neutral inquiry** (useful in business): *Will you **be joining** the call?* — softer than *"Will you join?"*

**Future Perfect** — `will have + participle`. Low frequency; mostly with *by*:
- *By December, we'**ll have finished** the migration.*

You can survive for years without producing these. Just recognize them.

---

## 4.10 Conditional structures — quick view

Full treatment in [Section 23](#23-conditionals). The essentials:

```text
If + present, ... will ...        → real future:  If it rains, we'll cancel.
If + past, ... would ...          → hypothetical: If I had more time, I'd rewrite it.
```

---

## 4.11 `used to` vs `be used to` vs `get used to` — high-value distinction

These look similar and mean completely different things.

| Form | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **used to + BASE VERB** | past habit that stopped (*costumava*) | I **used to smoke**. / We **used to work** together. |
| **be used to + NOUN/-ING** | be accustomed to (*estar acostumado com*) | I'**m used to working** late. / She's used to the noise. |
| **get used to + NOUN/-ING** | become accustomed (*se acostumar*) | You'll **get used to it**. / I'm getting used to the new system. |

- ✅ I **used to live** in Porto. *(I don't anymore)*
- ✅ I'**m used to living** alone. *(it's normal for me now)*
- ❌ I'm used to live alone. *(mixes both — very common error)*

**Negative/question of `used to`:** *I **didn't use to** like it* / *Did you **use to** work there?* — note: no `-d` after `did`.

**Also useful — `would` for past habits** (only for repeated actions, not states):
- When I was a kid, we **would spend** every summer at the beach. ✍️
- ❌ I would live in Porto. *(state → must use `used to`)*

---

## 4.12 `have` vs `have got`

| | Form | Register |
|---|---|---|
| Possession 🇺🇸 | I **have** a car. / Do you **have** a minute? | neutral, standard everywhere |
| Possession 🇬🇧 🗣️ | I'**ve got** a car. / **Have** you **got** a minute? | very common in speech, esp. British |

- Both are correct. `have got` is present only — the past is always **had**: *I had a car* (never *I had got*).
- Do NOT mix the question forms: ❌ Do you have got...? ❌ Have you a car? *(archaic)*
- 🗣️ Americans say *"I got a car"* (dropping 've) in casual speech — recognize it, don't write it.
- **`have to`** is different: obligation. *I **have to** go.* / 🗣️ *I'**ve got to** go* → *"I gotta go."*

**`have` for actions (not possession)** — very common collocations:
*have breakfast/lunch/dinner, have a shower, have a break, have a look, have a problem, have fun, have a meeting, have a baby, have an idea*.
These take `do` in questions: *Did you **have** lunch?* — and they CAN be continuous: *I'm having lunch.*

---

## 4.13 `do` as auxiliary vs `do` as a normal verb

`do` has two lives. Both can appear in one sentence:

```text
What do you do?        → 1st "do" = auxiliary, 2nd "do" = main verb
He doesn't do much.    → doesn't = auxiliary, do = main verb
Did you do it?         → did = auxiliary, do = main verb
I do my homework.      → main verb only
I do agree.            → emphatic auxiliary
```

**Main verb `do` = perform an activity:** *do the dishes, do homework, do business, do research, do exercise, do your job, do a favor*.
Contrast with `make` = create/produce: see [Section 16](#16-high-value-expressions-and-distinctions).

---

## 4.14 The imperative

Base verb, no subject. Used for instructions, directions, signs, and — carefully — commands.

- **Click** the button. / **Don't touch** that. / **Let's start**. / **Please sit down**.

⚠️ In requests to people, the bare imperative can sound rude in English. Soften it:
- ⚠️ Send me the file. → ✅ **Could you send me the file?** / **Please send me the file when you get a chance.**
- In documentation and instructions, the imperative is perfect and expected: *Install the package. Run the script.*

---

## 4.15 Verb summary table

| Tense | Structure | One-line use | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| Present Simple | V / V-s | habits, facts, opinions | I **work** here. |
| Present Continuous | am/is/are + V-ing | now, temporary, arrangements | I'**m working** on it. |
| Past Simple | V-ed / irregular | finished past time | I **worked** yesterday. |
| Past Continuous | was/were + V-ing | in progress in the past | I **was working** when he called. |
| Present Perfect | have/has + PP | past connected to now | I'**ve worked** here for years. |
| Pres. Perfect Cont. | have/has been + V-ing | duration of ongoing activity | I'**ve been working** all day. |
| Past Perfect | had + PP | the past before the past | I'**d worked** there before. |
| Future `will` | will + V | decision now, prediction | I'**ll call** you. |
| Future `going to` | am/is/are going to + V | plan, evidence | I'**m going to call** him. |
| Future Continuous | will be + V-ing | in progress in the future | I'**ll be working** at 9. |

---

# 5. Modal Verbs

Modals do not describe actions — they describe your **attitude** toward the action: is it possible? necessary? a good idea? polite?

## 5.0 The three rules that never change

1. **Base verb after the modal** — no `to`, no `-ing`, no `-ed`.
   ✅ You should **go**. ❌ You should to go. ❌ You should going.
2. **No `-s` for he/she/it.**
   ✅ She **can** help. ❌ She cans help.
3. **They're their own auxiliary** — no `do`.
   ✅ **Can** you...? / He **can't**. ❌ Do you can...? ❌ He doesn't can.

**Exceptions to the "no to" rule:** `have to`, `need to`, `ought to`, `be able to`, `be going to` — these are "semi-modals" and DO take `to`.

---

## 5.1 The complete practical map

| Modal | Main meanings | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| **can** | ability, permission, possibility, requests, offers | I **can** drive. / **Can** I leave early? / It **can** be slow. / **Can** you help? |
| **could** | past ability, polite request, possibility, suggestion | I **could** swim at 5. / **Could** you repeat that? / It **could** work. |
| **may** | formal permission, possibility | **May** I come in? ✍️ / It **may** take a while. |
| **might** | possibility (a bit less certain) | I **might** be late. |
| **must** | strong obligation (internal/rule), logical deduction | You **must** wear a helmet. / He **must** be tired. |
| **have to** | external obligation | I **have to** work Saturday. |
| **should** | advice, expectation, mild obligation | You **should** rest. / It **should** arrive today. |
| **would** | hypothetical, polite requests, past habit | I **would** do it differently. / **Would** you mind...? |
| **will** | future, promise, willingness, instant decision | I'**ll** do it. / She **won't** answer. |
| **shall** | 🇬🇧 formal offers/suggestions | **Shall** we begin? *(rare in 🇺🇸)* |
| **need to** | necessity (softer than must) | You **need to** restart it. |
| **be able to** | ability where `can` can't be used | I'**ll be able to** finish tomorrow. |

---

## 5.2 By communicative function (the way you actually use them)

### Ability

```text
can (present)  →  could (past)  →  will be able to (future)
```
- I **can** speak Spanish. / I **could** run 10 km when I was 20. / I'**ll be able to** join at 4.
- **One past exception:** for a single successful action in the past, use **was able to / managed to**, not `could`.
  - ❌ Yesterday I could fix the bug. → ✅ I **was able to** fix it. / I **managed to** fix it.
  - `could` works in the negative for single events: ✅ I **couldn't** fix it.

### Permission

- Asking: **Can** I...? *(neutral)* / **Could** I...? *(polite)* / **May** I...? *(formal)*
- Giving: You **can** use my desk. / You **may** leave. ✍️
- Refusing: You **can't** park here. / I'm afraid you **can't**.

### Possibility & probability — a scale

```text
100%  will / must (deduction)     It must be broken.
 90%  should                       It should work now.
 50%  may / might / could          It might rain.
 10%  probably won't               It probably won't happen.
  0%  can't (deduction)            He can't be serious.
```

- **must** = I'm sure it's true: *The lights are on — she **must be** home.*
- **can't** = I'm sure it's false: *He **can't be** at the office; it's 2 a.m.*
- ❌ *He mustn't be home* is NOT the opposite of *must be*. Use **can't be**.

### Obligation

| Strength | Form | Example |
|---|---|---|
| Strong, rule-based | **must** ✍️ | Passengers **must** show ID. |
| Strong, external | **have to** | I **have to** be there at 9. |
| Medium | **need to** | We **need to** talk. |
| Advice | **should / ought to** | You **should** apply. |
| No obligation | **don't have to / don't need to** | You **don't have to** come. *(it's optional)* |
| Prohibition | **must not / can't** | You **mustn't** share the password. |

> ⚠️ **The most dangerous modal confusion in English:**
> - **mustn't** = prohibited. *You **mustn't** tell anyone.* (= é proibido)
> - **don't have to** = not necessary. *You **don't have to** tell anyone.* (= não precisa)
>
> These are opposites. Getting this wrong changes the meaning completely.

### Advice

- You **should** take a break. / You **shouldn't** work weekends.
- **Should I** call him? *(asking for advice)*
- Stronger: *You **really should**...* / *You'd **better** call him.* 🗣️ *(warning tone: "you'd better" implies bad consequences)*
- Softer: *You **could** try restarting it.* / *It **might be worth** checking the logs.*

### Requests, offers, invitations — the politeness ladder

| Level | Request | Offer / Invitation |
|---|---|---|
| Direct | Send me the file. | Want a coffee? 🗣️ |
| Neutral | **Can** you send me the file? | **Do you want** to join? |
| Polite | **Could** you send me the file? | **Would you like** to join? |
| Very polite | **Would you mind** sending the file? | **Would you care** to join? ✍️ (rare) |
| Formal ✍️ | I **would appreciate it if you could** send the file. | We **would be delighted** if you could attend. |

**Key mechanism:** the **past forms** (`could`, `would`) are more polite than the present forms (`can`, `will`). They create distance, which sounds less demanding. This is why *"Could you..."* is safer than *"Can you..."* with strangers or clients.

**Careful with `Would you mind`:** it means "is it a problem for you?", so **"No"** is the positive answer.
- "Would you mind opening the window?" — "**No, not at all.**" *(= sure, I'll do it)*
- Grammar: `Would you mind + -ing`: ❌ Would you mind to open... → ✅ Would you mind **opening**...

---

## 5.3 The distinctions that matter

### `must` vs `have to`

| | must | have to |
|---|---|---|
| Source | speaker's own authority / rules in writing | external circumstances |
| Register | more formal, more written | everyday, all situations |
| Example | *You **must** submit by Friday.* (rule) | *I **have to** submit by Friday.* (they require it) |
| Past | ❌ no past → use **had to** | I **had to** rewrite it. |
| Future | ❌ → use **will have to** | We'**ll have to** wait. |

> **Practical advice:** in speech, use **have to** almost always. `must` in speech can sound bossy or dramatic. Keep `must` for written rules and for deduction (*it must be broken*).

### `should` vs `must`

- **should** = advice, my recommendation. You can ignore it.
- **must / have to** = obligation. Ignoring it has consequences.
- *You **should** update the docs.* (good idea) vs *You **have to** update the docs before merging.* (required)

### `can` vs `could`

- **Ability now** vs **ability in the past**: *I can drive / I could drive at 18.*
- **Requests:** `could` = more polite. Both are fine among colleagues.
- **Possibility:** `can` = general possibility (*It can get cold here in June*), `could` = specific possibility now (*It could be a network issue*).
- ⚠️ **Don't use `could` for a specific past success:** see the `was able to` note above.

### `may` vs `might`

- Practically almost interchangeable for possibility. `might` is a bit less certain and much more common in speech.
- **may** additionally means formal permission (*May I?*) — `might` does not.
- ✍️ In formal/legal writing, `may` = permission or possibility: *Users may cancel at any time.*
- **Rule of thumb:** speaking → `might`. Formal writing → `may`.

### `will` vs `would`

| will | would |
|---|---|
| real future | hypothetical / unreal |
| *I'**ll** help you.* | *I'**d** help you if I had time.* |
| *He **won't** answer.* (refuses / won't in future) | *He **wouldn't** answer.* (refused, past) |
| *That **will** be $30.* | *That **would** be great.* |
| direct request: *Will you...?* | polite request: *Would you...?* |

**High-frequency `would` uses to memorize:**
- **I'd like** = a polite "I want": *I'd like a coffee. / I'd like to speak to Ana.*
- **Would you like...?** = polite offer.
- **I'd rather** = I prefer: *I'd rather work from home. / I'd rather not talk about it.*
- **Opinions, softened:** *I'd say it's about 20%.* / *That would be a problem.*
- **Past habit:** *We'd meet every Friday.*

### `shall` — LATER

Rare in American English. Two surviving uses:
- Suggestions/offers 🇬🇧: *Shall we go? / Shall I open the window?*
- Legal/contract writing: *The Supplier shall deliver...*
Elsewhere, use `will` or `should I`.

---

## 5.4 Modals in the past — quick reference

| Present | Past | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| can | could / was able to | ability |
| can't | couldn't | inability |
| must (obligation) | **had to** | obligation |
| must (deduction) | **must have + PP** | *She **must have forgotten**.* |
| might/may | **might have + PP** | *He **might have missed** the train.* |
| should | **should have + PP** | *I **should have** tested it.* (regret) |
| shouldn't | **shouldn't have + PP** | *You **shouldn't have said** that.* |
| can't (deduction) | **can't have + PP** | *She **can't have known**.* |

**`should have` is extremely useful** for regret and criticism — worth drilling:
- I **should have** left earlier.
- We **shouldn't have** merged that PR.
- 🗣️ Pronounced *"should've"* → sounds like *"shoulda"*. Never write *"should of"* (a common native mistake).

## 5.5 Common modal mistakes

❌ I must to go. → ✅ I **must go**. / I **have to** go.
❌ She cans help. → ✅ She **can** help.
❌ Do you can come? → ✅ **Can** you come?
❌ I will can do it. → ✅ I'**ll be able to** do it.
❌ You don't must smoke here. → ✅ You **mustn't** smoke here. / You **can't** smoke here.
❌ Would you mind to help? → ✅ Would you mind **helping**?
❌ I'd like drink something. → ✅ I'd like **to drink** something. / I'd like **a drink**.
❌ Yesterday I could finish the task. → ✅ Yesterday I **was able to** finish the task.

---

# 6. Questions

## 6.1 The universal formula

```text
(WH-word) + AUXILIARY + SUBJECT + MAIN VERB + (rest) ?
```

Everything else is a variation of this.

| WH | AUX | SUBJ | VERB | rest |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| — | Do | you | speak | English? |
| Where | do | you | live | ? |
| What | are | you | working | on? |
| When | did | they | release | it? |
| How long | have | you | been | here? |
| Why | should | we | change | it? |
| — | Is | the file | — | ready? |

## 6.2 Questions with `be` — invert, don't add anything

`be` moves in front of the subject. **No `do`.**

- **Are** you ready? / **Is** it working? / **Was** he there? / **Were** they late?
- **Why is** the build failing?
- ❌ Do you are ready? ❌ Is you ready?

## 6.3 Questions with `do / does / did`

Used with **all other main verbs** in the simple tenses.

| Tense | Auxiliary | Example |
|---|---|---|
| Present, I/you/we/they | do | **Do** you use Slack? |
| Present, he/she/it | does | **Does** she know? |
| Past (all persons) | did | **Did** it work? |

**The main verb stays in base form.**
❌ Does she knows? → ✅ Does she **know**?
❌ Did you went? → ✅ Did you **go**?

## 6.4 Questions with modals and other auxiliaries

Just move the auxiliary to the front:

- **Can** you hear me? / **Should** we wait? / **Would** you like some? / **Will** he come?
- **Have** you seen it? / **Has** it started? / **Had** they left?
- **Are** you coming? / **Is** he working?

## 6.5 Questions in every tense — one table

| Tense | Question |
|---|---|
| Present Simple | **Do** you **work** here? / **Does** it **work**? |
| Present Continuous | **Are** you **working**? |
| Past Simple | **Did** you **work** yesterday? |
| Past Continuous | **Were** you **working**? |
| Present Perfect | **Have** you **worked** here long? |
| Pres. Perf. Continuous | **Have** you **been working** all day? |
| Past Perfect | **Had** you **worked** there before? |
| `will` future | **Will** you **work** on Saturday? |
| `going to` | **Are** you **going to work** on it? |
| Modal | **Can/Should/Would** you **work** on it? |

## 6.6 The question words

| Word | Asks about | Example | Typical answer |
|---|---|---|---|
| **what** | thing, action | What do you do? | I'm an engineer. |
| **where** | place | Where is the meeting? | In room 3. |
| **when** | time | When did it start? | At 9. |
| **who** | person (subject) | Who called? | Ana did. |
| **whom** ✍️ | person (object) — rare | To whom did you send it? | *(use "Who did you send it to?")* |
| **whose** | possession | Whose laptop is this? | It's mine. |
| **which** | choice from a limited set | Which option do you prefer? | The second one. |
| **why** | reason | Why is it slow? | Because of the query. |
| **how** | manner, method | How does it work? | It uses a queue. |
| **how much** | uncountable / price | How much does it cost? | $50. |
| **how many** | countable | How many users do we have? | About 2,000. |
| **how long** | duration | How long does it take? | Ten minutes. |
| **how often** | frequency | How often do you deploy? | Twice a week. |
| **how far** | distance | How far is the airport? | 20 km. |
| **how old** | age | How old is the system? | Five years. |
| **what kind of** | type | What kind of database is it? | Postgres. |
| **what time** | clock time | What time is the call? | 3 p.m. |
| **how come** 🗣️ | why (informal) | How come you're late? | Traffic. |

**Note on `how come`:** it takes **statement word order**, not question order.
- ✅ How come **you didn't call**? *(not: How come didn't you call?)*

**`what` vs `which`:** `which` = a limited, known set. `what` = open.
- **Which** browser do you use, Chrome or Firefox? / **What** browser do you use?

## 6.7 Subject questions — no auxiliary

When *who/what/which* **is** the subject, don't invert and don't add `do`.

| Subject question (no `do`) | Object question (with `do`) |
|---|---|
| **Who called** you? | **Who did** you **call**? |
| **What happened**? | **What did** you **do**? |
| **Who wants** coffee? | **Who do** you **work** with? |
| **What caused** the outage? | **What did** it **cause**? |

## 6.8 Prepositions in questions

In natural English, the preposition usually goes **at the end**.

- **Who** are you talking **to**? *(not: To whom are you talking? ✍️/archaic)*
- **What** are you looking **for**?
- **Where** are you **from**?
- **Which** project are you working **on**?
- **What** did you do that **for**? *(= why)*

> 🇧🇷 Portuguese puts the preposition first (*Com quem você falou?*). English speakers put it last in speech.
> ```text
> Com quem você está falando?
>         ↓
> ⚠️ With whom are you speaking?  (correct but very formal)
>         ↓
> ✅ Who are you talking to?
> ```

## 6.9 Indirect and polite questions — USEFUL and underused

Indirect questions are **softer**. They are used constantly in professional English. The critical rule:

```text
Inside an indirect question, use STATEMENT word order — no inversion, no do/does/did.
```

| Direct | Indirect / polite |
|---|---|
| Where is the office? | Could you tell me **where the office is**? |
| What time does it start? | Do you know **what time it starts**? |
| Did he call? | I was wondering **if he called**. |
| How much is it? | Can you tell me **how much it is**? |
| Why did they cancel? | I'd like to know **why they cancelled**. |
| Is she available? | Do you know **whether she's available**? |

❌ Could you tell me where **is the office**?
✅ Could you tell me where **the office is**.

**Useful openers:**
```text
Could you tell me ...          Do you know ...
I was wondering if ...         Would you mind telling me ...
I'd like to know ...           Any idea ... 🗣️
Do you happen to know ...      Can I ask ...
```

**Note:** if the indirect question is inside a statement (*I don't know where he is.*), it ends with a period. If it starts with *Do you know / Could you tell me*, it ends with a question mark.

**For yes/no questions, use `if` or `whether`:**
- Do you know **if** the meeting is still on?
- I'm not sure **whether** he received it.

## 6.10 Question tags — USEFUL

Short questions added at the end to confirm or invite agreement.

```text
Positive statement → negative tag:  You're coming, aren't you?
Negative statement → positive tag:  He didn't call, did he?
```

- It's ready, **isn't it**?
- You work with Ana, **don't you**?
- They haven't replied, **have they**?
- I'm late, **aren't I**? *(irregular)*
- Let's start, **shall we**?
- Close the door, **will you**?

🗣️ In speech, many people just use **"right?"** — much easier and always acceptable in informal contexts: *"You sent it, right?"*

## 6.11 Common Portuguese-speaker mistakes in questions

| ❌ Wrong | ✅ Right | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Where you work? | Where **do** you work? | missing auxiliary |
| What means this? | What **does this mean**? | word order + auxiliary |
| How is called this? | What **is this called**? | wrong question word |
| You are coming? ⚠️ | **Are** you coming? | inversion (though the ⚠️ form exists in casual speech) |
| Did he came? | Did he **come**? | base verb after `did` |
| Do you know where is he? | Do you know where **he is**? | indirect question order |
| How much people? | How **many** people? | countable → many |
| What hour is it? | **What time** is it? | literal translation |
| Who is the responsible? | **Who's responsible?** / Who's in charge? | adjective ≠ noun |
| Why you didn't call? | Why **didn't you** call? | inversion |

---

# 7. Negation

## 7.1 The universal rule

```text
Put "not" after the first auxiliary.
No auxiliary?  → bring in do / does / did.
```

| Sentence | Negative |
|---|---|
| She is here. | She **is not / isn't** here. |
| They have finished. | They **haven't** finished. |
| He can swim. | He **can't** swim. |
| It will work. | It **won't** work. |
| I work here. | I **don't** work here. |
| He works here. | He **doesn't** work here. |
| We finished. | We **didn't** finish. |

## 7.2 The full contraction list

| Full form | Contraction | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| do not | don't | |
| does not | doesn't | |
| did not | didn't | |
| is not | isn't | also *he's not / she's not* |
| are not | aren't | also *you're not / they're not* |
| was not | wasn't | |
| were not | weren't | |
| have not | haven't | |
| has not | hasn't | |
| had not | hadn't | |
| will not | **won't** | irregular spelling |
| would not | wouldn't | |
| should not | shouldn't | |
| could not | couldn't | |
| cannot | **can't** | written as one word: *cannot* |
| must not | mustn't | rare in 🇺🇸 speech |
| am not | — | no contraction; use *I'm not* |

**Two forms, same meaning:** *She isn't ready* = *She's not ready*. The second form puts more emphasis on the negative. Both are common.

## 7.3 Negatives with the main verb `be` and `have`

- `be` never needs `do`: ❌ I don't be ready → ✅ I'**m not** ready.
- `have` (possession) usually does: ✅ I **don't have** a car. / 🇬🇧 I **haven't got** a car.

## 7.4 Never, no, nothing — the "hidden" negatives

These words are negative by themselves. **Don't add another negative.**

- I **never** work weekends. *(not: I don't never work)*
- There's **no** solution. = There **isn't a** solution.
- I have **nothing** to add. = I **don't have anything** to add.
- **Nobody** knows. = **Not anybody** knows ❌ → the natural form is *Nobody knows.*

```text
Não tenho nada para dizer.
        ↓
❌ I don't have nothing to say.
        ↓
✅ I have nothing to say.  /  ✅ I don't have anything to say.
```

**Which to choose?** `no/nothing/nobody` is a bit stronger and more emphatic; `not...any` is more neutral and more common in speech.

## 7.5 Double negatives

Standard English allows only one negative per clause. Double negatives exist in songs and some dialects (*"I don't need no help"*) — recognize them, don't use them.

**Legitimate double negatives** (a negative + a negative prefix = a soft positive) are formal and useful in professional English:
- It's **not un**common. *(= it's fairly common, said cautiously)*
- I **don't dis**agree. *(= I partly agree)*
- That's **not impossible**.

## 7.6 Negative questions

Used for surprise, confirmation, or complaints.

- **Didn't** you get my email? *(surprise)*
- **Isn't** it obvious?
- **Can't** we do this tomorrow?
- **Why don't** we take a break? *(= suggestion, very common)*
- **Don't** you think it's too expensive?

> ⚠️ **The answer trap.** English answers based on the *facts*, not on the question's polarity — the opposite of Portuguese.
>
> "Didn't you send it?"
> - If you sent it → "**Yes**, I did." *(Portuguese speakers often say "No, I sent it" — confusing)*
> - If you didn't → "**No**, I didn't."
>
> ```text
> "Você não vai?"  — "Não, eu vou."  ← works in Portuguese
> "Aren't you going?" — ❌ "No, I'm going."  → ✅ "Yes, I am."
> ```
> **Safety strategy:** don't answer with only yes/no. Add the verb: *"Yes, I sent it this morning."* This removes all ambiguity.

## 7.7 Negative answers and softeners

| Direct | Softer (professional) |
|---|---|
| No. | I'm afraid not. |
| That's wrong. | I'm not sure that's quite right. |
| I can't. | I'd love to, but I can't. / I won't be able to. |
| I don't agree. | I see it a bit differently. / I'm not sure I agree. |
| We can't do that. | That might be tricky. / That would be difficult. |
| No, it doesn't work. | Actually, it's not working on my end. |

## 7.8 Common negative expressions

- **not at all** — "Thanks!" — "Not at all." / "Would you mind?" — "Not at all."
- **not really** 🗣️ — soft "no": *"Do you like it?" — "Not really."*
- **not yet** — *"Have you finished?" — "Not yet."*
- **not anymore** — *I don't work there anymore.*
- **neither / nor** — *Neither option works.* / *I don't like it, and neither does she.*
- **hardly / barely** — almost negative: *I **hardly** ever go out.* / *I could **barely** hear you.* (see [Section 16](#16-high-value-expressions-and-distinctions))
- **no longer** ✍️ — *This feature is **no longer** supported.*
- 🗣️ **nope / nah** — very casual "no". **yep / yeah** — very casual "yes".

## 7.9 Spoken negative forms to recognize

| Written | Spoken sounds like |
|---|---|
| don't know | *dunno* /dəˈnoʊ/ |
| doesn't | *duhznt* |
| didn't | *didn* — the final t often disappears |
| can't 🇺🇸 | /kænt/ — the `t` is often silent, so **can** and **can't** sound similar; the vowel and stress tell you which |
| won't | /woʊnt/ — contrast with *want* /wɑnt/ |
| isn't it | *innit* 🇬🇧 🗣️ |
| I'm not gonna | *I'm not gonna* → often *"I ain't gonna"* (non-standard) |

> **Listening tip:** `can` vs `can't` is a real comprehension problem. In American English, *can* is unstressed and reduced (/kən/), while *can't* is stressed with a full vowel (/kænt/). If you hear a **strong, clear** "can," it's probably *can't*.

---

# 8. Articles

Articles are tiny words that native speakers process automatically and non-natives get wrong for decades. The good news: **article mistakes almost never destroy meaning.** They just sound foreign. Fix the high-frequency cases and ignore the rest.

## 8.1 The decision tree

```text
Is the noun specific — does the listener know WHICH one?
├── YES → the
└── NO  → Is it singular and countable?
          ├── YES → a / an
          └── NO (plural or uncountable) → no article (∅)
```

That single tree covers most real cases.

## 8.2 `a` vs `an`

Based on **sound**, not spelling.

| `a` (consonant sound) | `an` (vowel sound) |
|---|---|
| a user | an update |
| a **u**niversity /juː/ | an **u**mbrella |
| a **E**uropean country | an **h**our /aʊər/ |
| a **o**ne-hour meeting /w/ | an **h**onest answer |
| a URL /juː/ | an **M**BA /em/ |
| a CV /siː/ | an **F**AQ /ef/, an **S**QL query /es/ |

**Test:** say the word out loud. If it starts with a vowel *sound*, use `an`.

## 8.3 `the` — when the listener knows which one

Use `the` when:

1. **Second mention** — *I bought **a** laptop. **The** laptop is really fast.*
2. **Only one exists in the context** — *Close **the** door. / **The** CEO called. / **The** sun.*
3. **Made specific by what follows** — ***the** file I sent you*, ***the** meeting on Friday*, ***the** best option*
4. **Superlatives and ordinals** — ***the** biggest*, ***the** first*, ***the** last*, ***the** only*, ***the** same*
5. **Groups/systems seen as one** — ***the** internet, **the** government, **the** environment, **the** economy, **the** police*
6. **Musical instruments (playing)** — *She plays **the** guitar.*

## 8.4 Zero article (∅) — the hardest part for Portuguese speakers

Portuguese uses definite articles far more than English. **This is the #1 article error.**

### Rule: general/abstract statements take NO article

```text
Eu gosto de música.       → ❌ I like the music.      → ✅ I like music.
A vida é curta.           → ❌ The life is short.     → ✅ Life is short.
Os cachorros são leais.   → ❌ The dogs are loyal.    → ✅ Dogs are loyal.
O tempo é dinheiro.       → ❌ The time is money.     → ✅ Time is money.
A tecnologia mudou tudo.  → ❌ The technology...      → ✅ Technology changed everything.
Eu odeio reuniões.        → ❌ I hate the meetings.   → ✅ I hate meetings.
```

But add `the` the moment it becomes specific:
- ✅ I like **the** music **in this bar**. / ✅ **The** meetings **on Mondays** are useless.

### No article with these categories

| Category | Examples |
|---|---|
