| Languages | I speak **∅ Portuguese**. *(but: **the** Portuguese language)* |
| Meals | We had **∅ lunch**. *(but: **the** lunch we had yesterday)* |
| Most countries/cities | ∅ Brazil, ∅ Portugal, ∅ France, ∅ Lisbon, ∅ Japan |
| Sports & academic subjects | I play **∅ football**. She studies **∅ economics**. |
| Days, months, holidays | on ∅ Monday, in ∅ July, at ∅ Christmas |
| `go to` + institution (for its purpose) | go to ∅ work, ∅ school, ∅ church, ∅ bed, ∅ university 🇬🇧, ∅ prison |
| Transport with `by` | by ∅ car, by ∅ plane, by ∅ bus, by ∅ email |
| Company/product names | ∅ Google, ∅ Microsoft, ∅ Docker |
| Plural professions in general | ∅ Engineers earn well. |

### `the` with these categories — memorize as a list

| Category | Examples |
|---|---|
| Countries with plural/political names | **the** United States, **the** UK, **the** Netherlands, **the** Philippines, **the** Czech Republic, **the** UAE |
| Oceans, seas, rivers | **the** Atlantic, **the** Mediterranean, **the** Amazon, **the** Thames |
| Mountain ranges, island groups | **the** Andes, **the** Alps, **the** Azores, **the** Canaries |
| Regions | **the** north, **the** Middle East, **the** south of France |
| Newspapers | **the** Guardian, **the** New York Times |
| Hotels, cinemas, museums | **the** Hilton, **the** Louvre |
| Superlatives/uniques | **the** best, **the** only one, **the** same |
| Decades / centuries | in **the** 90s, in **the** 21st century |

> 🇧🇷 **Portugal, Brazil, and country names.** In Portuguese, most countries take an article (*o Brasil, a França*). In English, almost none do.
> ```text
> O Brasil é grande.
>         ↓
> ❌ The Brazil is big.
>         ↓
> ✅ Brazil is big.
> ```
> Exception: *the United States*, *the UK*, *the Netherlands* — because they are descriptions, not names.

### `school` / `hospital` / `work` — with or without `the`

```text
No article  → you're there for its purpose
"the"       → you're there as a visitor / it's a specific building
```

| Purpose | Building |
|---|---|
| He's in **∅ hospital** 🇬🇧 / in **the** hospital 🇺🇸 *(as a patient)* | I'm at **the** hospital *(visiting)* |
| She goes to **∅ school**. *(as a student)* | I'm at **the** school *(the building)* |
| He's in **∅ prison**. *(a prisoner)* | She works at **the** prison. |
| I'm at **∅ work**. *(always no article)* | — |
| I'm going **∅ home**. *(never "to the home")* | — |

**Very common mistakes:**
❌ I'm going to the work. → ✅ I'm going **to work**.
❌ I'm going to the home. → ✅ I'm going **home**. *(no preposition either!)*
❌ I go to the bed at 11. → ✅ I go **to bed** at 11.

## 8.5 Professions and roles

```text
Singular profession → a / an        →  She's a designer. He's an engineer.
Plural / general    → ∅             →  Designers are expensive.
Unique position     → the / ∅        →  She's the CTO. / She was elected president.
```

> 🇧🇷 Portuguese drops the article here (*"Ele é engenheiro"*). English requires it.
> ```text
> Ela é médica.
>         ↓
> ❌ She is doctor.
>         ↓
> ✅ She is a doctor.
> ```

## 8.6 A quick decision table

| Situation | Article | Example |
|---|---|---|
| First mention, countable singular | a/an | I found **a** bug. |
| Second mention | the | **The** bug is in the login flow. |
| Only one in context | the | **The** manager wants to talk. |
| General plural | ∅ | **Bugs** are inevitable. |
| General uncountable | ∅ | **Software** is expensive. |
| Specific uncountable | the | **The software** we bought is expensive. |
| Profession (singular) | a/an | He's **an** accountant. |
| Country (normal) | ∅ | She lives in **Spain**. |
| Superlative | the | It's **the** fastest option. |
| Abstract idea | ∅ | **Trust** takes time. |

## 8.7 Article mistakes ranked by importance

**Fix these first (they sound worst):**
1. `the` before general nouns — *"I like the music"*
2. `the` before countries — *"the Brazil"*
3. Missing `a` before professions — *"I am engineer"*
4. *"go to the work / the home"*
5. Missing article with singular countable nouns — *"I have car"*

**These barely matter:** subtle cases of *the* with institutions, seasons (*in ∅ summer* / *in the summer* are both fine), or *hospital* 🇺🇸/🇬🇧.

---

# 9. Countable and Uncountable Nouns

## 9.1 The core idea

```text
Countable   → can be counted: one file, two files, three files
Uncountable → measured, not counted: water, information, work
```

Uncountable nouns:
- have **no plural** form,
- take **no `a/an`**,
- take a **singular verb**.

- ✅ **Information is** useful. ❌ ~~informations are~~
- ✅ I need **some advice**. ❌ ~~an advice~~ ❌ ~~advices~~

## 9.2 The list that fixes most errors

These are **uncountable in English** but countable (or pluralized) in Portuguese. This is the highest-value list in this section.

| ❌ Wrong | ✅ Right | Portuguese |
|---|---|---|
| informations | **information** | informações |
| advices | **advice** / a piece of advice | conselhos |
| news are | **news is** | notícias |
| a furniture / furnitures | **furniture** / a piece of furniture | móveis |
| equipments | **equipment** | equipamentos |
| softwares | **software** | softwares |
| feedbacks | **feedback** | feedbacks |
| knowledges | **knowledge** | conhecimentos |
| researches | **research** | pesquisas |
| trainings ⚠️ | **training** *(some business use: "trainings" 🇺🇸)* | treinamentos |
| works ⚠️ | **work** *(job/effort)* | trabalhos |
| moneys | **money** | dinheiro |
| a homework | **homework** | tarefa |
| progresses | **progress** | progressos |
| evidences | **evidence** | evidências |
| accommodations 🇬🇧 | **accommodation** | acomodações |
| luggages / baggages | **luggage / baggage** | bagagens |
| staffs | **staff** *(the people)* | equipe |
| traffics | **traffic** | trânsito |
| damages ⚠️ | **damage** *(harm)* — "damages" = legal compensation | danos |
| a bread | **bread** / a loaf of bread | pão |
| a water ⚠️ | **water** *(but "a water" = a bottle 🗣️)* | água |

Others commonly uncountable: *time, money, music, love, help, patience, weather, air, electricity, data ⚠️, content, feedback, support, access, transportation, permission, experience ⚠*.

> **⚠️ `data`:** technically plural of *datum*. In modern usage, treat it as **uncountable**: *The data **is** incomplete.* 🇺🇸/tech. Academic writing may use *the data are*. Both are defensible; don't say *"datas"*.

> **⚠️ Nouns with two lives** — countable AND uncountable, with different meanings:
>
> | Uncountable | Countable |
> |---|---|
> | I don't have **time**. | I've been there three **times**. |
> | She has **experience**. | It was a great **experience**. |
> | I need **space**. | There are two **spaces** left. |
> | Would you like **chicken**? *(meat)* | There's **a chicken** in the yard. |
> | **Paper** is cheap. | I wrote **a paper** on it. *(an article)* |
> | **Business** is slow. | I started **a business**. |
> | **Work** is hard. | It's **a work** of art. ✍️ |
> | **Glass** breaks. | Pass me **a glass**. |

## 9.3 How to count uncountable nouns

Use a **measure word** + `of`:

```text
a piece of advice / information / news / furniture / equipment / cake
a bit of help / trouble / luck
a cup of coffee, a glass of water, a bottle of wine, a slice of bread
two kilos of rice, three liters of fuel
a lot of / plenty of / a couple of
an item of clothing, an article of furniture ✍️
```

- ✅ Can I give you **a piece of advice**?
- ✅ I have **some good news**. / ✅ That's **a piece of good news**. *(rarer)*

## 9.4 Quantifiers — the full picture

| Quantifier | Countable | Uncountable | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| some | ✅ some files | ✅ some water | positive statements, offers, requests |
| any | ✅ any files | ✅ any water | questions, negatives; also "whichever" |
| many | ✅ many files | ❌ | mostly questions/negatives |
| much | ❌ | ✅ much time | mostly questions/negatives |
| a lot of / lots of | ✅ | ✅ | **positive statements** — most common |
| plenty of | ✅ | ✅ | more than enough |
| a few | ✅ a few files | ❌ | some, a small number (positive) |
| few | ✅ few files | ❌ | almost none (negative) |
| a little | ❌ | ✅ a little time | some, a small amount (positive) |
| little | ❌ | ✅ little time | almost none (negative) |
| several | ✅ | ❌ | more than a few |
| a couple of | ✅ | ❌ | two, or 🗣️ "a few" |
| enough | ✅ | ✅ | |
| no / none | ✅ | ✅ | |
| all / most | ✅ | ✅ | |
| a great deal of ✍️ | ❌ | ✅ | formal |
| a number of | ✅ | ❌ | formal-ish |
| an amount of | ❌ | ✅ | |

### `much` / `many` vs `a lot of`

```text
Positive statement  → a lot of / lots of   (much/many sound formal or odd)
Question / negative → many / much           (a lot of also works)
```

- ⚠️ I have much work. → ✅ I have **a lot of** work.
- ⚠️ She has many friends. → ✅ She has **a lot of** friends. *(both correct; "a lot of" is more natural in speech)*
- ✅ **How much** does it cost? / **How many** people came?
- ✅ I don't have **much** time. / There aren't **many** options.
- ✍️ In formal writing, *much/many* in positive statements is fine: *Many companies have adopted this approach.*

### `few` vs `a few` — the difference is attitude

```text
a few / a little  →  some — positive, "enough"
few / little      →  almost none — negative, "not enough"
```

| Positive | Negative |
|---|---|
| I have **a few** questions. *(a small number — fine)* | I have **few** questions. *(almost none)* |
| We have **a little** time. *(some — we can do it)* | We have **little** time. *(hurry!)* |
| **A few** people showed up. *(some did)* | **Few** people showed up. *(disappointing)* |

🗣️ In speech, people avoid bare *few/little* and say **"not many" / "not much" / "hardly any"** instead:
- ✅ Not many people showed up. / ✅ We have hardly any time.

> 🇧🇷 **`a few`** ≈ *alguns / uns poucos* (positivo). **`few`** ≈ *poucos* (com sentido de falta).

### `less` vs `fewer`

```text
fewer + countable plural   → fewer bugs, fewer people, fewer meetings
less  + uncountable        → less time, less money, less traffic
```

- ✅ We have **fewer** bugs and **less** technical debt.
- ⚠️ Native speakers constantly say *"less bugs"* and supermarkets write *"10 items or less."* It's extremely common but considered incorrect in careful writing.
- **Always `less`** with money, time, and distance treated as amounts: *less than $50, less than two hours, less than 5 km*.

## 9.5 Common Portuguese-speaker mistakes

| ❌ | ✅ | Why |
|---|---|---|
| I need some informations. | I need **some information**. | uncountable |
| He gave me a good advice. | He gave me **some good advice** / **a piece of advice**. | uncountable |
| The news are bad. | The news **is** bad. | singular |
| I have many works to do. | I have **a lot of work** to do. | uncountable |
| We bought new furnitures. | We bought new **furniture**. | uncountable |
| How much people came? | How **many** people came? | countable |
| I have much friends. | I have **a lot of** friends. | quantifier choice |
| There is few time. | There's **little** time. / We don't have **much** time. | uncountable |
| Do you want some helps? | Do you want **some help**? | uncountable |
| Three coffees ⚠️ | ✅ acceptable 🗣️ (= three cups of coffee) | container shortcut |
| The peoples of the office | **The people** in the office | *people* is already plural |

---

# 10. Adjectives

## 10.1 Position — two places only

**1. Before the noun**

```text
adjective + noun
```
- a **fast** connection / an **urgent** request / **useful** feedback

**2. After a linking verb** (`be, seem, look, feel, sound, taste, smell, become, get, stay, remain`)

```text
subject + linking verb + adjective
```
- The connection is **fast**. / It seems **urgent**. / That sounds **great**. / It's getting **late**.

> 🇧🇷 **Portuguese puts most adjectives after the noun.** This is the most audible learner mistake.
> ```text
> um problema sério
>         ↓
> ❌ a problem serious
>         ↓
> ✅ a serious problem
> ```

## 10.2 Adjectives never take `-s`

```text
duas reuniões importantes
        ↓
❌ two importants meetings
        ↓
✅ two important meetings
```

## 10.3 Adjective order — USEFUL, not essential

When you stack adjectives, English follows a fixed order. Native speakers feel it; you can memorize the sequence:

```text
Opinion → Size → Age → Shape → Color → Origin → Material → Purpose → NOUN
```

- a **nice big old** wooden desk
- a **cheap Chinese plastic** case
- a **beautiful little red Italian sports** car

**Practical advice:** you will rarely use more than two. With two, this is the reliable rule: **opinion before fact**, and **size before color/origin**.
- ✅ a **great new** feature *(opinion + age)*
- ✅ a **small black** laptop *(size + color)*
- ❌ a black small laptop

**Comma rule:** use commas between adjectives of the same type (*a cold, wet, miserable day*), not between different types (*a big red car*).

## 10.4 `-ed` vs `-ing` adjectives — HIGH VALUE

This causes real, embarrassing confusion.

```text
-ing → describes the THING that causes the feeling
-ed  → describes the PERSON who feels it
```

| Cause (-ing) | Feeling (-ed) |
|---|---|
| The meeting was **boring**. | I was **bored**. |
| This project is **interesting**. | I'm **interested** in this project. |
| The trip was **tiring**. | I'm **tired**. |
| The news is **exciting**. | We're **excited**. |
| That's **confusing**. | I'm **confused**. |
| The result was **surprising**. | I was **surprised**. |
| It's **frustrating**. | I'm **frustrated**. |
| The movie was **depressing**. | He felt **depressed**. |
| It's **annoying**. | I'm **annoyed**. |
| The deadline is **stressful/worrying**. | I'm **worried**. |
| It was **embarrassing**. | She was **embarrassed**. |
| This is **disappointing**. | We're **disappointed**. |
| The task is **challenging**. | — |
| It's **shocking**. | I was **shocked**. |
| That's **satisfying**. | I'm **satisfied**. |
| The book is **amazing**. | I'm **amazed**. |

> ⚠️ **"I am boring"** = "eu sou chato/entediante." **"I am bored"** = "estou entediado." Saying *"I'm boring"* in a job interview is a memorable mistake.

**Memory trick:** if a thing gives you the feeling, it ends in **-ing**. If you receive the feeling, you end in **-ed**.

## 10.5 Adjectives + prepositions — memorize as chunks

These combinations are fixed and frequently wrong in learner English.

| Adjective + preposition | Example |
|---|---|
| interested **in** | I'm interested in machine learning. |
| good / bad **at** | She's good at negotiating. |
| responsible **for** | He's responsible for deployments. |
| worried / concerned **about** | I'm worried about the deadline. |
| afraid / scared **of** | I'm afraid of flying. |
| proud **of** | We're proud of the team. |
| aware **of** | Are you aware of the risk? |
| tired **of** *(= farto)* | I'm tired of these meetings. |
| full **of** | The log is full of errors. |
| similar **to** | It's similar to Redis. |
| different **from** *(🇬🇧 also "to", 🇺🇸 also "than")* | This is different from the old one. |
| married **to** | She's married to a lawyer. |
| used **to** | I'm used to it. |
| based **in / on** | We're based in Porto. / It's based on Linux. |
| available **for / to** | I'm available for a call. |
| familiar **with** | Are you familiar with Kafka? |
| satisfied / happy **with** | I'm happy with the result. |
| dependent **on** | It's dependent on the API. |
| capable **of** | It's capable of handling 10k requests. |
| suitable **for** | This isn't suitable for production. |
| keen **on** 🇬🇧 | I'm not keen on that idea. |
| angry **at/with** (person) / **about** (thing) | She's angry about the delay. |

> 🇧🇷 Common wrong transfers: ❌ *interested **on*** → ✅ *in*; ❌ *good **in*** → ✅ *at*; ❌ *married **with*** → ✅ *to*; ❌ *responsible **of*** → ✅ *for*; ❌ *depends **of*** → ✅ *depends **on***.

## 10.6 Other useful adjective facts

- **Adjectives can act as nouns for groups:** *the rich, the unemployed, the elderly* ✍️
- **Compound adjectives are hyphenated before a noun and singular:**
  - a **five-minute** break *(not five minutes)*, a **two-year** contract, a **well-known** company, an **up-to-date** report
  - After the noun, no hyphen: *The break was five minutes long. / The report is up to date.*
- **`-ly` words are usually adverbs, but some are adjectives:** *friendly, lovely, lonely, likely, ugly, silly*. You cannot say *"He spoke friendly"* → ✅ *"He spoke in a friendly way."*
- **Enough goes AFTER the adjective:** ✅ *fast enough*, *good enough* ❌ *enough fast*. But **before a noun**: *enough time*.
- **`too` goes before:** *too expensive, too late*.

---

# 11. Comparatives and Superlatives

## 11.1 The formation rules

| Adjective type | Comparative | Superlative | Examples |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 syllable | + **-er** | **the** + **-est** | fast → faster → the fastest |
| 1 syllable, CVC | double the consonant | | big → bigger → the biggest; hot → hotter |
| ending in `-e` | + **-r / -st** | | large → larger → the largest |
| 2 syllables ending in `-y` | `-y` → **-ier / -iest** | | easy → easier → the easiest; happy → happier |
| 2 syllables (other) | **more / most** | | modern → more modern → the most modern |
| 3+ syllables | **more / most** | | expensive → more expensive → the most expensive |

**Some 2-syllable adjectives take both:** *simple/simpler, quiet/quieter, narrow/narrower, clever/cleverer, friendly/friendlier* — `more simple` is also acceptable.

## 11.2 Irregular forms — memorize

| Adjective | Comparative | Superlative |
|---|---|---|
| good / well | **better** | **the best** |
| bad / badly | **worse** | **the worst** |
| far | **farther / further** | the farthest / **the furthest** |
| little | **less** | the least |
| much / many | **more** | the most |
| old | older / **elder** (family) | the oldest / the eldest |

**`farther` vs `further`:** `farther` = physical distance only; `further` = distance AND abstract ("more"). When in doubt, use **further**: *further information, further discussion, let's discuss it further*.

## 11.3 Structures

### Comparative

```text
A + be/verb + comparative + than + B
```
- Python is **easier than** C++.
- This laptop is **more expensive than** mine.
- She works **harder than** anyone.
- The new version runs **better than** the old one.

**After `than`, both object and subject pronouns are used:**
- ✅ 🗣️ He's taller **than me**. *(normal speech)*
- ✅ ✍️ He's taller **than I am**. *(formal, safest in writing)*
- ⚠️ *than I* alone sounds stiff.

### Superlative

```text
the + superlative + (noun) + in/of + group
```
- It's **the fastest** option **in** the market.
- She's **the most experienced** developer **on** the team.
- That was **the worst** meeting **of** the year.
- ⚠️ **`the` is required**: ❌ *It's best solution* → ✅ *It's **the** best solution*.
- Exception: after a possessive → *my best friend*, *our biggest client*.
- **`in` for places/groups**, **`of` for time periods**: *the best restaurant **in** town*, *the best day **of** my life*.

### Intensifying and softening comparatives

```text
much / a lot / far / significantly + comparative     → much better, far more expensive
a bit / a little / slightly + comparative            → a bit slower, slightly cheaper
even + comparative                                    → even worse
```
- ❌ very better → ✅ **much** better
- ✅ It's **a lot** faster. / It's **slightly** more complex. / That's **even** worse.

## 11.4 `as ... as` — equality

```text
as + adjective/adverb + as
```
- It's **as fast as** the original.
- She's **as experienced as** he is.
- Come **as soon as** possible.
- I earn **as much as** you.
- We have **as many** users **as** last year.

**Negative:**
```text
not as ... as   =   less than
```
- It's **not as expensive as** I thought. *(= cheaper than I expected)*
- The new one **isn't as fast as** the old one.
- ✅ *not so ... as* also exists but is less common.

**Useful fixed comparisons 🗣️:** *as soon as possible, as far as I know, as good as new, as easy as that, as much as I'd like to*.

## 11.5 `less ... than` and `the least`

```text
less + adjective + than   → the opposite of "more ... than"
```
- This solution is **less complex than** the other one.
- It's **less expensive**, but **less reliable** too.
- **the least** = the minimum: *That's **the least** of our problems.*

⚠️ In practice, native speakers prefer **not as ... as** for short adjectives:
- ⚠️ It's less big than mine. → ✅ It's **not as big as** mine.
- ✅ `less` works well with longer adjectives: *less complicated, less expensive, less important*.

## 11.6 `the more ..., the more ...` — the parallel structure

Very useful and very natural.

```text
The + comparative + clause,  the + comparative + clause
```

- **The more** you practice, **the better** you get.
- **The bigger** the team, **the slower** the decisions.
- **The sooner** we start, **the sooner** we finish.
- **The more** I learn, **the less** I understand.
- 🗣️ **The more, the merrier.** *(fixed expression: quanto mais, melhor)*

**Also useful:** *comparative + and + comparative* = progressive change
- It's getting **harder and harder**.
- Prices are getting **more and more expensive**.

## 11.7 Comparing nouns and verbs

```text
more/fewer/less + noun + than
verb + more/less + than
```
- We have **more users than** last year.
- There were **fewer bugs than** expected.
- I work **more than** I should.
- He talks **less than** he used to.
- **twice as much / three times as many:** *We have **twice as many** customers **as** last year.*

## 11.8 Common mistakes

| ❌ | ✅ |
|---|---|
| more better | **better** |
| more easier | **easier** |
| the most best | **the best** |
| It's more big than that. | It's **bigger** than that. |
| She is more tall. | She is **taller**. |
| It's the more expensive. | It's **the most** expensive. |
| I'm better than him in English. | I'm better **at** English than he is. |
| very better | **much** better |
| as fast than | as fast **as** |
| It's best option. | It's **the** best option. |
| the same than | the same **as** |
| different than 🇺🇸 ⚠️ | different **from** *(safest)* |

> 🇧🇷 `mais grande` → `bigger` (not "more big"), `mais bom` → `better`. Portuguese has *melhor/pior*, so the concept exists — the trap is applying `more` to short adjectives.

---

# 12. Adverbs

Adverbs describe **how, when, where, how often, and how much**. Their biggest practical challenge is not form but **position**.

## 12.1 Formation

```text
adjective + -ly   → quick → quickly, careful → carefully, easy → easily, true → truly
```

**Adverbs with the same form as the adjective:** *fast, hard, late, early, straight, right, wrong, high, low, near, daily, weekly*.
- He drives **fast**. / She works **hard**. / They arrived **late**.
- ❌ fastly ❌ hardly (exists but means something else!) ❌ lately (exists but means "recently")

**Irregular:** *good → **well***.

## 12.2 Types and default positions

| Type | Examples | Usual position |
|---|---|---|
| **Manner** | quickly, well, carefully, badly | after the verb/object |
| **Place** | here, there, outside, upstairs | after the verb/object |
| **Time** | now, yesterday, later, soon, tomorrow | end (or start for emphasis) |
| **Frequency** | always, usually, often, never | before the main verb |
| **Degree** | very, really, quite, too, extremely | before the adjective/adverb |
| **Comment** | fortunately, obviously, honestly | start of the sentence |

## 12.3 Frequency adverbs — the position rule

This is the rule worth memorizing exactly:

```text
BEFORE the main verb        →  I always check my email.
AFTER the verb "be"         →  He is always late.
AFTER the first auxiliary   →  I have never seen that. / You should always test.
```

| Structure | Example |
|---|---|
| main verb | She **often works** from home. |
| `be` | She **is often** late. |
| auxiliary + verb | I **have often wondered** about that. |
| negative | I **don't usually** work weekends. |

**Frequency scale:**

```text
100%  always
 90%  usually / normally / generally
 70%  often / frequently
 50%  sometimes
 30%  occasionally
 10%  rarely / seldom / hardly ever
  0%  never
```

**Flexible ones:** *usually, normally, sometimes, often, occasionally* can also go at the beginning or end for emphasis:
- ✅ **Sometimes** I work from home. / I work from home **sometimes**.
- ❌ *Always I work...* / ❌ *I work always...* — **`always` and `never` stay in the middle.**

> 🇧🇷 Portuguese puts frequency adverbs after the verb (*"Eu trabalho sempre em casa"*).
> ```text
> Eu sempre chego cedo.  /  Eu chego sempre cedo.
>         ↓
> ❌ I arrive always early.
>         ↓
> ✅ I always arrive early.
> ```

**Frequency expressions (not single adverbs) go at the END:**
- I check my email **every day** / **twice a week** / **once a month** / **all the time**.
- ❌ I every day check my email.

## 12.4 The high-frequency adverbs, one by one

### `already` — earlier than expected

```text
mid-position (before main verb / after be / after auxiliary)
```
- I'**ve already** sent it.
- She'**s already** here.
- It's **already** 6 p.m.!
- 🗣️ End position for surprise: *"You've finished **already**?"*

### `still` — continuing (not finished)

```text
before the main verb / after "be";  in negatives, BEFORE the negative
```
- I'm **still** waiting.
- He **still** works there.
- It **still** doesn't work. *(→ `still` before `doesn't` shows frustration/surprise)*
- Compare: *It **doesn't** work **yet**.* (neutral) vs *It **still doesn't** work.* (annoyed)

### `yet` — not until now (questions and negatives)

```text
end of the sentence
```
- **Have** you finished **yet**?
- I haven't started **yet**.
- The results aren't in **yet**.
- ✍️ `yet` can also mean "but": *It's simple, **yet** powerful.*

### `just` — a moment ago / only (see [Section 16](#16-high-value-expressions-and-distinctions))

- I'**ve just** arrived. / 🇺🇸 I **just** arrived.
- It's **just** a suggestion.

### `still / already / yet` in one table

| Word | Meaning | Position | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| still | continues | middle | I'm **still** working on it. |
| already | happened, sooner than expected | middle | I've **already** finished. |
| yet | hasn't happened (so far) | end | I haven't finished **yet**. |
| no longer / not anymore | stopped | ✍️ middle / 🗣️ end | He **no longer** works here. / He doesn't work here **anymore**. |

### Degree adverbs

| Adverb | Strength | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| **a bit / a little** | weak | *It's a bit slow.* (softener — very common 🇬🇧) |
| **fairly** | moderate | neutral-positive |
| **quite** | ⚠️ 🇬🇧 "fairly" / 🇺🇸 "very" | *quite good* = "reasonably good" 🇬🇧, "really good" 🇺🇸 |
| **pretty** 🗣️ | quite/very | *It's pretty good.* — very common informal |
| **really** | strong | works with verbs too: *I really like it.* |
| **very** | strong | adjectives/adverbs only |
| **extremely / incredibly** | very strong | |
| **absolutely / totally** | with extreme adjectives | *absolutely amazing*, not *absolutely good* |
| **too** | excessive — negative | *too expensive* = more than acceptable |
| **enough** | sufficient | goes AFTER the adjective: *good enough* |
| **almost / nearly** | not quite | *almost done*, *nearly finished* |
| **hardly / barely** | almost not | *I could hardly hear you.* |

**Strong adjectives don't take `very`:**
- ❌ very excellent / very huge / very perfect
- ✅ **absolutely** excellent / **really** huge / **completely** perfect

## 12.5 Adverb position — the general order at the end of a sentence

```text
VERB + (object) + MANNER + PLACE + TIME
```

- She spoke **clearly** (manner) **in the meeting** (place) **yesterday** (time).
- I worked **hard at the office all week**.
- ❌ She spoke yesterday clearly in the meeting.

**Time can move to the front** for emphasis or flow:
- ✅ **Yesterday** she spoke clearly in the meeting.

**Never put an adverb between the verb and its object:**
- ❌ I speak **fluently** English. / ❌ She likes **very much** coffee.
- ✅ I speak English **fluently**. / ✅ She likes coffee **very much**. / ✅ She **really** likes coffee.

> 🇧🇷 This is one of the most common word-order errors:
> ```text
> Eu falo bem inglês.
>         ↓
> ❌ I speak well English.
>         ↓
> ✅ I speak English well.
> ```

## 12.6 Comment adverbs — useful in professional English

Placed at the start (with a comma) to comment on the whole sentence:

*Fortunately, Unfortunately, Obviously, Clearly, Apparently, Basically, Honestly, Frankly, Personally, Actually, Ideally, Realistically, Hopefully, Surprisingly, Interestingly, Technically*

- **Unfortunately**, we missed the deadline.
- **Apparently**, they changed the API.
- **Ideally**, we'd test this first.
- **Honestly**, I don't think it'll work.
- **Basically**, it's a caching issue.

## 12.7 Adverb vs adjective — the common confusion

| Use an adjective after | Use an adverb after |
|---|---|
| be, seem, look, feel, sound, taste, smell, become, get | action verbs |
| *It looks **good**.* | *It works **well**.* |
| *That sounds **great**.* | *She sang **beautifully**.* |
| *I feel **bad** about it.* | *He behaved **badly**.* |
| *The food tastes **strange**.* | *He looked at me **strangely**.* |

**"I feel good" vs "I feel well":** *good* = happy/positive; *well* = healthy. Both are correct English.

---

# 13. Prepositions

Prepositions are mostly **memorized, not derived**. Learn them inside phrases, not as isolated words.

## 13.1 Time: `in` / `on` / `at` — the size rule

```text
at → precise points        →  at 3 p.m., at noon, at night, at the moment
on → days and dates        →  on Monday, on July 4th, on my birthday
in → longer periods        →  in July, in 2024, in the morning, in winter
```

| at | on | in |
|---|---|---|
| at 7 o'clock | on Monday | in April |
| at midnight / noon | on Friday morning | in 2025 |
| at lunchtime | on 15 March | in the 90s |
| at the weekend 🇬🇧 | on the weekend 🇺🇸 | in the morning/afternoon/evening |
| at Christmas *(period)* | on Christmas Day | in summer |
| at the same time | on time *(pontual)* | in time *(a tempo)* |
| at present | | in a week *(daqui a uma semana)* |

**Zero preposition** with: *next, last, this, every, all, tomorrow, yesterday, today*
- ❌ in next week → ✅ **next week**
- ❌ on last Monday → ✅ **last Monday**
- ❌ in every day → ✅ **every day**

### Other essential time prepositions

| Preposition | Meaning | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| **since** | starting point (with Present Perfect) | since 2020, since Monday, since I joined |
| **for** | duration | for two hours, for a week, for ages |
| **during** | within a period (when?) | during the meeting, during the summer |
| **while** | + clause (conjunction, not preposition) | while I was working |
| **until / till** | up to a point in time | I'll wait until 5. / It's open till late. |
| **by** | not later than (deadline) | Send it **by** Friday. |
| **from ... to/until** | range | from 9 to 5 |
| **within** | inside a period | within 24 hours |
| **after / before** | sequence | after lunch, before the call |
| **in** (future) | after a period from now | I'll be there **in** 10 minutes. |
| **ago** | back from now (goes AFTER) | two years **ago** |

> **`by` vs `until` — this one causes real problems at work.**
> - **by** = the deadline; the action happens at some point before: *"I'll finish it **by** Friday."*
> - **until** = the action continues up to that time: *"I'll work on it **until** Friday."*
> ```text
> Preciso disso até sexta.
>         ↓
> ❌ I need it until Friday.   (= I need it continuously up to Friday)
>         ↓
> ✅ I need it by Friday.
> ```

> **`during` vs `for`:** *during* answers "when", *for* answers "how long."
> - ✅ I was on holiday **for** two weeks **during** the summer.
> - ❌ I slept during two hours. → ✅ I slept **for** two hours.

> **`in` vs `within`:** *in 10 minutes* = after 10 minutes. *within 10 minutes* = at some point in the next 10 minutes.

## 13.2 Place: `in` / `on` / `at`

```text
in → inside a space/area   →  in the office, in Brazil, in a car, in my pocket
on → on a surface / a line →  on the table, on the wall, on the second floor, on a bus
at → a point / a location  →  at the door, at the office, at the bus stop, at work
```

| in | on | at |
|---|---|---|
| in the room | on the desk | at the entrance |
| in Lisbon / in Portugal | on the wall | at the airport |
| in the car / in a taxi | on the bus / train / plane | at home / at work / at school |
| in bed | on the floor / on the roof | at the top / bottom |
| in the picture | on page 12 | at the corner *(street corner)* |
| in the newspaper | on TV / on the radio / on the internet | at the meeting |
| in the queue 🇬🇧 | on the list | at the table *(sitting)* |
| in a book | on a website | at a party / concert |

### `in` vs `at` for buildings — the practical rule

```text
at → thinking of the function/activity   →  I'm at the office. She's at the gym.
in → thinking of the physical inside     →  It's cold in the office.
```
Both are usually correct; `at` is safer for locations.

### Other place prepositions

| Preposition | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **under** | directly below | The cable is under the desk. |
| **below** | lower than (not touching) | Sales are below target. |
| **over** | above / covering / more than | over the bridge, over 100 people |
| **above** | higher than | The shelf above the sink. |
| **between** | two things (or clear individuals) | between the two options |
| **among** | three+ / a group mass | among the team members |
| **next to / beside** | at the side | Sit next to me. |
| **near / close to** | not far | near the station |
| **behind** | at the back | behind the building |
| **in front of** | at the front (facing) | in front of the screen |
| **opposite / across from** | facing on the other side | opposite the bank |
| **inside / outside** | | outside the building |
| **around** | surrounding / approximately | around the table, around 20 people |
| **by** | very close | Sit by the window. |

> ⚠️ **`in front of` ≠ "em frente a"(facing).** *In front of the building* = na frente do prédio (do lado da entrada). To say "across the street from," use **opposite** 🇬🇧 / **across from** 🇺🇸.

## 13.3 Movement

| Preposition | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **to** | destination | I'm going **to** the office. |
| **into** | entering | She walked **into** the room. |
| **onto** | moving to a surface | Drag the file **onto** the desktop. |
| **out of** | exiting | He got **out of** the car. |
| **from** | origin | I'm **from** Brazil. / a message **from** Ana |
| **through** | in one side, out the other | through the tunnel, through the process |
| **across** | from one side to the other (surface) | across the street, across the room |
| **along** | following a line | along the river, along the way |
| **around** | circular / all over | around the corner, around the city |
| **towards** | in the direction of | He walked **towards** me. |
| **up / down** | vertical / along a street | up the stairs, down the street |
| **off** | away from a surface | Take it **off** the table. / get **off** the bus |
| **past** | going by | Drive **past** the bank. |

**Key contrasts:**
- **to** vs **into**: *Go to the room* (destination) / *Go into the room* (crossing the threshold).
- **in** vs **into**: *He's **in** the car* (position) / *He got **into** the car* (movement).
- **across** vs **through**: *across* = a flat surface (street, field) / *through* = a 3D space (tunnel, forest, crowd).

**No preposition with `home`:**
- ❌ I'm going **to** home. → ✅ I'm going **home**.
- ✅ But: *I'm going to my parents' home/house.*

## 13.4 Verb + preposition — the highest-value list

These must be memorized as units. Errors here are extremely common.

| Correct | ❌ Common error (PT interference) |
|---|---|
| depend **on** | depend of |
| listen **to** | listen ∅ |
| wait **for** | wait ∅ / wait for by |
| look **for** *(procurar)* | search for ✅ but ❌ look ∅ |
| ask **for** *(pedir)* | ask ∅ |
| pay **for** *(pagar por algo)* | pay ∅ the coffee |
| think **about/of** | think in |
| dream **about/of** | dream with |
| talk / speak **to/with** | talk with ✅ (both fine) |
| worry **about** | worry with |
| apologize **for** | apologize ∅ |
| belong **to** | belong ∅ |
| agree **with** (person) / **on** (thing) | agree ∅ / agree to ⚠️ |
| arrive **at** (place) / **in** (city) | arrive to / arrive in the office ⚠️ |
| get **to** (a place) | get in the office ⚠️ |
| participate **in** | participate of |
| consist **of** | consist in |
| succeed **in** | succeed ∅ |
| focus **on** | focus in |
| rely **on** | rely in |
| complain **about** | complain of ⚠️ |
| apply **for** (a job) / **to** (a company) | apply ∅ |
| congratulate **on** | congratulate for ⚠️ |
| insist **on** | insist in |
| result **in** (cause) / **from** (effect) | |
| happen **to** | happen with |
| remind **of / about** | remember of |
| care **about** | care with |
| deal **with** | deal ∅ |
| translate **into** | translate for ⚠️ |
| divide **into** | divide in ⚠️ |
| **No preposition after these:** discuss, enter, answer, marry, phone/call, join, approach, reach, lack, resemble | discuss about ❌, enter in ❌, answer to ❌, marry with ❌, call to ❌, join to ❌ |

> 🇧🇷 The "no preposition" line is critical. Portuguese adds prepositions that English forbids:
> ```text
> Vamos discutir sobre o projeto.
>         ↓
> ❌ Let's discuss about the project.
>         ↓
> ✅ Let's discuss the project.  /  ✅ Let's talk about the project.
> ```
> ```text
> Ele entrou na sala.  →  ❌ He entered in the room.  →  ✅ He entered the room. / He went into the room.
> Ela casou com ele.   →  ❌ She married with him.    →  ✅ She married him. / She got married to him.
> Vou ligar para ele.  →  ❌ I'll call to him.        →  ✅ I'll call him.
> ```

## 13.5 The `by / with / for / of / about` cluster

| Preposition | Core meaning | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| **by** | agent, method, deadline, proximity | written **by** Ana, **by** email, **by** car, **by** Friday, **by** the window, **by** accident |
| **with** | accompaniment, tool, feature | coffee **with** milk, cut it **with** a knife, a laptop **with** 32GB |
| **for** | purpose, beneficiary, duration | a tool **for** testing, this is **for** you, **for** two hours |
| **of** | belonging, quantity, material | the end **of** the file, a cup **of** tea, made **of** wood |
| **about** | topic | a book **about** design, worried **about** it |

**`by` vs `with` for method:**
- **by** + general method (no article): *by email, by phone, by car, by hand, by credit card*
- **with** + a specific instrument: *with my phone, with a hammer, with this tool*
- ✅ I'll send it **by email** **with** my phone.

**`made of / from / by / with`:**
- made **of** wood *(material still visible)* / made **from** grapes *(transformed)* / made **by** hand *(method or agent)* / made **with** love

## 13.6 Prepositions at the end of a sentence

This is **normal, correct English** — especially in questions and relative clauses:
- Who are you talking **to**?
- That's the file I was looking **for**.
- What's this **for**?
- It's nothing to worry **about**.

The "rule" against it is a myth. The formal alternative (*to whom...*) is optional and sounds academic.

## 13.7 A survival list of fixed prepositional phrases

```text
on time / in time          on purpose / by accident
in a hurry                 at least / at most
in advance                 by the way
on the phone               in person
at first / at last         in general
on average                 in fact
in charge of               on behalf of ✍️
in touch with              in terms of
on my way                  out of order
at risk                    under pressure
by mistake                 in the meantime
on the other hand          from my point of view
in my opinion              on the whole ✍️
```

## 13.8 Preposition mistakes ranked

**Highest priority (they cause misunderstanding or sound very wrong):**
1. *depend of* → **depend on**
2. *discuss about* → **discuss ∅**
3. *arrive to* → **arrive at/in**
4. *married with* → **married to**
5. *by Friday* vs *until Friday*
6. *in the TV* → **on TV**
7. *go to home* → **go home**
8. *listen ∅ music* → **listen to music**

**Low priority:** *at/on the weekend*, *in/at the office*, *different from/than*. Nobody will misunderstand you.

---

# 14. The Most Important English Connectors

Connectors are what separate "I speak some English" from "I can explain things in English." A learner with 800 words and good connectors communicates better than one with 3,000 words and none.

## 14.1 The basic six

| Connector | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **and** | addition | I checked the logs **and** restarted the service. |
| **but** | contrast | It works, **but** it's slow. |
| **or** | alternative | We can fix it now **or** wait until Monday. |
| **so** | result | The API was down, **so** we rolled back. |
| **because** | reason | We rolled back **because** the API was down. |
| **if** | condition | **If** it fails again, call me. |

**Practical note:** `so` and `because` are two sides of the same coin.
```text
CAUSE, so RESULT   =   RESULT because CAUSE
```
- It was raining, **so** we cancelled. = We cancelled **because** it was raining.

⚠️ **Never use both:** ❌ *Because it was raining, so we cancelled.* — This is a direct translation of *"Como estava chovendo, então..."* and is wrong in English.

## 14.2 Contrast — the family that causes trouble

| Connector | Grammar | Position | Register |
|---|---|---|---|
| **but** | + clause | middle | all |
| **although / even though** | + clause | start or middle | all |
| **though** | + clause; also **end** 🗣️ | flexible | informal-neutral |
| **however** | + full sentence, with comma | start (or middle with semicolons) | formal ✍️ |
| **while / whereas** | + clause (comparison) | start or middle | neutral-formal |
| **despite / in spite of** | + **noun/-ing** (not a clause) | start or middle | formal ✍️ |
| **nevertheless / nonetheless** | + sentence | start | very formal ✍️ |
| **on the other hand** | + sentence | start | neutral |

**The critical grammar difference:**

```text
although / even though / though  + SUBJECT + VERB
despite / in spite of            + NOUN or -ING
however                          . However, + NEW SENTENCE
```

- ✅ **Although** it's expensive, it's worth it.
- ✅ **Despite** the cost, it's worth it.
- ✅ **Despite being** expensive, it's worth it.
- ❌ Despite it's expensive... ❌ Despite of the cost... ❌ Although the cost...

**`however` punctuation** — a very common error:
- ❌ It's expensive, however it's worth it.
- ✅ It's expensive. **However,** it's worth it.
- ✅ It's expensive; **however,** it's worth it. ✍️
- ✅ It's expensive, **but** it's worth it.

**`though` at the end** 🗣️ — extremely common in speech, worth learning:
- "It's expensive." — "It's good, **though**." *(= mas é bom)*
- I like it. It's a bit slow, **though**.

**`while` vs `whereas`:**
- **while** = at the same time OR contrast: *I'll take notes **while** you present.* / ***While** the UI is great, the backend is a mess.*
- **whereas** = pure contrast, more formal: *Our team uses Go, **whereas** theirs uses Rust.*

**`even though` vs `even if`:**
```text
even though + real fact        →  Even though it's raining, we're going. (it IS raining)
even if     + hypothetical     →  Even if it rains, we're going. (maybe it will)
```

## 14.3 Adding information

| Connector | Position | Register | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| **and** | middle | all | fast **and** cheap |
| **also** | mid-position (before main verb / after `be`) | all | We **also** need approval. |
| **too** | end | all | We need approval, **too**. |
| **as well** | end | all | She's coming **as well**. |
| **as well as** | + noun/-ing | neutral | We need design **as well as** code. |
| **besides** | start; also "apart from" | informal | **Besides**, it's too late. |
| **in addition (to)** | start / + noun | formal ✍️ | **In addition**, costs increased. |
| **moreover / furthermore** | start | very formal ✍️ | **Furthermore**, the data is incomplete. |
| **plus** 🗣️ | start | informal | **Plus**, it's cheaper. |
| **not only ... but also** | | neutral-formal | It's **not only** faster **but also** cheaper. |

> ⚠️ **`also` position:** ❌ *Also I think...* at the start is common in learner English and sounds slightly off in writing. Prefer *"I also think..."* or *"In addition,"* or 🗣️ *"Also,"* which IS fine in speech and casual writing.

## 14.4 Result and consequence

| Connector | Register | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **so** | all (most common) | It broke, **so** I fixed it. |
| **therefore** | formal ✍️ | The test failed. **Therefore,** we cannot release. |
| **as a result** | neutral-formal | **As a result,** delivery was delayed. |
| **that's why** | conversational | **That's why** we use Postgres. |
| **which is why** | neutral | The API changed, **which is why** it broke. |
| **consequently / thus / hence** | very formal ✍️ | **Consequently,** the contract was void. |
| **so that** | purpose (not result) | I wrote a script **so that** we don't repeat this. |

## 14.5 Reason

| Connector | Grammar | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **because** | + clause | ...**because** the disk was full. |
| **because of** | + noun | ...**because of** the disk space. |
| **since** | + clause (known reason) | **Since** you're here, can you review this? |
| **as** | + clause ✍️ | **As** the deadline is close, we'll simplify. |
| **due to** ✍️ | + noun | **Due to** technical issues, the call was cancelled. |
| **owing to** ✍️ | + noun | rare, formal |
| **the reason is that** | + clause | **The reason is that** the cache wasn't invalidated. |

⚠️ **`because` vs `because of`:**
- ❌ because of it was late → ✅ **because** it was late
- ❌ because the traffic → ✅ **because of** the traffic

⚠️ **`since` has two meanings** — time and reason. Context disambiguates: *I've been here **since** 9* (time) vs ***Since** you're busy, I'll do it* (reason).

## 14.6 Time connectors

| Connector | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **when** | at that time | Call me **when** you arrive. |
| **while** | during | I'll wait **while** you finish. |
| **as soon as** | immediately after | I'll let you know **as soon as** I hear back. |
| **before / after** | sequence | Check the logs **before** you restart. |
| **until / till** | up to that moment | Wait **until** I confirm. |
| **once** | after (something completes) | **Once** it's deployed, we'll test. |
| **by the time** | at the moment when | **By the time** I arrived, it was over. |
| **as** | at the same moment | **As** I was leaving, he called. |
| **then / after that / next** | sequence in a list | First..., **then**..., **finally**... |
| **meanwhile / in the meantime** | at the same time | **In the meantime,** use the old version. |

> **Remember: no `will` after time connectors.**
> ❌ I'll call you when I will arrive. → ✅ ...**when I arrive**.
> ❌ As soon as he will finish... → ✅ **As soon as he finishes**...

## 14.7 Alternatives, conditions, and exceptions

| Connector | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **or** | alternative | Coffee **or** tea? |
| **otherwise** | if not | Save it, **otherwise** you'll lose it. |
| **instead** | replacing (end/start position) | The meeting was cancelled. We'll email **instead**. |
| **instead of** | + noun/-ing | Use Redis **instead of** Memcached. |
| **unless** | if not | I'll go **unless** it rains. |
| **as long as / provided that** | on condition that | You can use it **as long as** you credit us. |
| **in case** | precaution | Bring a charger **in case** the battery dies. |
| **whether ... or** | two options | I don't know **whether** to fix it **or** rewrite it. |
| **either ... or / neither ... nor** | | **Either** we ship it **or** we delay. |

⚠️ **`in case` ≠ `if`.** *Take an umbrella **in case** it rains* = take it BEFORE, as a precaution. *Take an umbrella **if** it rains* = only take it when it's raining.

⚠️ **`instead` vs `instead of`:** `instead` stands alone; `instead of` needs an object.
- ✅ Don't call. Email me **instead**.
- ✅ Email me **instead of** calling.

## 14.8 Explaining, illustrating, correcting

| Connector | Use | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **for example / for instance** | illustration | Some tools — **for example**, Docker — help a lot. |
| **such as** | + noun list | tools **such as** Docker and Kubernetes |
| **like** 🗣️ | + noun (informal "such as") | tools **like** Docker |
| **that is / i.e.** ✍️ | precise restatement | the deadline, **i.e.** March 3rd |
| **in other words** | restating | **In other words,** we need more time. |
| **that means** | consequence | **That means** we can't launch on Friday. |
| **in fact / actually** | adding a surprising truth or correcting | It's not slow. **In fact,** it's faster. |
| **basically** 🗣️ | simplifying | **Basically,** it's a caching problem. |
| **specifically / in particular** | narrowing | We need help, **specifically** with QA. |
| **overall / in general** | generalizing | **Overall,** it went well. |
| **to be honest / frankly** 🗣️ | opinion marker | **To be honest,** I don't like the design. |
| **anyway** 🗣️ | returning to the point / closing | **Anyway,** let's move on. |
| **by the way** | new topic, minor | **By the way,** did you see the email? |

**`actually` vs `in fact`** — both correct a wrong impression, but:
- **actually** 🗣️ is soft and polite: *"Actually, the meeting is at 3, not 2."*
- **in fact** ✍️ often reinforces: *"It's cheap. In fact, it's free."*
- ⚠️ **`actually` does NOT mean "atualmente"** — see [Section 27](#27-false-friends--false-cognates).

## 14.9 Conversational vs formal connectors

| Idea | 🗣️ Conversational | Neutral | ✍️ Formal |
|---|---|---|---|
| Contrast | but, though | however | nevertheless, nonetheless |
| Result | so, that's why | as a result | therefore, consequently, thus |
| Reason | because, 'cause | since, as | due to, owing to |
| Addition | and, plus, also | in addition | moreover, furthermore |
| Example | like | for example | for instance, such as |
| Restating | I mean, basically | in other words | that is (i.e.) |
| Opinion | I think, I'd say | in my opinion | from my perspective, it appears that |

> **Practical advice:** in emails and meetings, `however`, `therefore`, `moreover` are safe but slightly stiff. Native professionals write *"but," "so," "also."* Don't force formality.

## 14.10 Starting sentences with And / But / So

Textbooks say don't. Reality: native speakers do it constantly, in speech and in modern professional writing.
- ✅ *"We tested it. **But** the results were inconclusive."*
- ✅ *"**So**, where do we go from here?"*

Avoid it only in very formal documents (academic papers, legal text).

---

# 15. Relative Pronouns

Relative clauses let you add information about a noun without starting a new sentence. They are essential for sounding fluent — learners who avoid them produce choppy, childlike English.

## 15.1 The basic set

| Pronoun | Refers to | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **who** | people | The engineer **who** wrote this left the company. |
| **which** | things, animals | The bug **which** caused the outage is fixed. |
| **that** | people or things (defining only) | The person **that** called / the file **that** broke |
| **whose** | possession (people & things) | The client **whose** contract expires today. |
| **where** | places | The office **where** I work is downtown. |
| **when** | times | The day **when** we launched was chaotic. |
| **why** | reasons | The reason **why** it failed is simple. |
| **whom** ✍️ | people (object) — formal, rare | The manager **whom** we hired... |

## 15.2 Defining vs non-defining — the comma rule

This is the only relative-clause rule that changes meaning.

### Defining (no commas) — the information is essential

It tells you **which one**. Remove it and you don't know what's being discussed.

- The developer **who wrote the parser** is on vacation. *(which developer? that one)*
- The file **that you sent** is corrupted.
- Everything **I need** is in the repo.

### Non-defining (with commas) — the information is extra

The noun is already identified. The clause just adds a comment.

- Ana, **who wrote the parser**, is on vacation. *(we already know who Ana is)*
- Our main server, **which was installed in 2019**, needs replacing.
- I spoke to the CTO, **who agreed with us**.

### Why it matters — a real difference in meaning

```text
My brother who lives in Lisbon is a doctor.
→ I have several brothers. The Lisbon one is a doctor.

My brother, who lives in Lisbon, is a doctor.
→ I have one brother. (Extra info: he lives in Lisbon.)
```

```text
The engineers who worked overtime got a bonus.
→ Only the ones who worked overtime.

The engineers, who worked overtime, got a bonus.
→ All of them worked overtime, and all got a bonus.
```

### Three rules that follow from this

1. **`that` can only be used in DEFINING clauses.**
   - ✅ The file **that** you sent... / ❌ Ana, **that** wrote the parser, ... → ✅ Ana, **who** wrote...
2. **Non-defining clauses must use `who` / `which` / `whose` / `where`, with commas.**
3. **In defining clauses, you can DROP the pronoun if it's the object.**
   - ✅ The file (**that**) you sent is corrupted.
   - ✅ The person (**who/that**) I met yesterday...
   - ❌ You cannot drop it if it's the subject: *The file **that** broke the build* — `that` is required.

> **The dropping test:** if a subject + verb follows immediately (*you sent*, *I met*), you can drop the pronoun. If a verb follows immediately (*broke*, *wrote*), you can't.

## 15.3 `who` vs `which` vs `that` — practical guidance

```text
People       → who (or that in defining clauses)
Things       → which (or that in defining clauses)
Defining     → that is the most common in speech
Non-defining → never "that"
```

- ✅ The client **who** called... / ✅ The client **that** called... *(both fine)*
- ✅ The report **which** you sent... / ✅ The report **that** you sent... / ✅ The report you sent...
- 🇺🇸 American style guides prefer **that** for defining and reserve **which** for non-defining. British usage is more relaxed. Both are understood.

## 15.4 `whose`

Shows possession. Works for people **and** things.

- The developer **whose** code broke the build is fixing it.
- A company **whose** products I really like.
- We need a solution **whose** cost is predictable. ⚠️ *(grammatically fine but stiff — better: "a solution with a predictable cost")*

> ⚠️ `whose` ≠ `who's`. **`who's` = who is / who has.**
> - Who's calling? *(who is)* / The man **whose** car was stolen. *(possession)*

## 15.5 `where`, `when`, `why`

```text
where → place       the city where I grew up
when  → time        the year when we launched
why   → reason      the reason why it failed
```

**Practical alternatives** (often more natural):
- the city **where** I grew up = the city **(that) I grew up in** 🗣️
- the day **when** we met = the day **(that) we met**
- the reason **why** = the reason **(that)** / just *the reason*

⚠️ **`where` vs `which`:** use `where` only when the place is a *location*, not a thing.
- ✅ the room **where** we meet / ✅ the room **in which** we meet ✍️
- ❌ the document **where** it's explained → ✅ the document **in which** it's explained ✍️ / **the document that explains it**

## 15.6 `which` for a whole idea

Very useful in professional English: `which` can refer back to an entire clause (always with a comma).

- The build failed, **which** means we can't deploy today.
- She resigned last week, **which** surprised everyone.
- They cut the budget, **which** is why the project stalled.

❌ Don't use `what` here: *The build failed, ~~what~~ means...* — a frequent Portuguese-speaker error, since *"o que"* covers both.

```text
O build falhou, o que significa que...
        ↓
❌ The build failed, what means that...
        ↓
✅ The build failed, which means that...
```

## 15.7 Prepositions in relative clauses

Natural English puts the preposition at the **end**:

- The tool **(that) I told you about**.
- The person **(who) I spoke to**.
- The project **(that) she's working on**.

Formal English moves it to the front and requires `which`/`whom`:
- ✍️ The tool **about which** I told you. / The person **to whom** I spoke.

Use the natural version unless you're writing a legal document.

## 15.8 Common mistakes

| ❌ | ✅ |
|---|---|
| The man which called | The man **who/that** called |
| The book who I read | The book **which/that** I read |
| Ana, that is my manager, ... | Ana, **who** is my manager, ... |
| The house where I live in it | The house **where** I live / the house I live **in** |
| The file that you sent it | The file **(that) you sent** *(no double object)* |
| The reason because it failed | The reason **why** it failed / The reason **(that)** it failed |
| ...which is the problem what worries me | ...**which** worries me |
| People who works here | People who **work** here *(verb agrees with "people")* |
| I have a friend he lives in NY | I have a friend **who** lives in NY |

---

# 16. High-Value Expressions and Distinctions

This is the section to bookmark. These pairs cause more real communication problems than any grammar rule.

## 16.1 `just` vs `only`

**`just` has four common meanings** — context and position tell you which:

| Meaning | Example | Portuguese |
|---|---|---|
| **1. Recently** | I'**ve just** finished. / 🇺🇸 I **just** finished. | acabei de |
| **2. Only / simply** | It's **just** a suggestion. | apenas, só |
| **3. Exactly** | That's **just** what I needed. | exatamente |
| **4. Softener** 🗣️ | I **just** wanted to check in. | (amaciador) |

**`only` = nothing more than.** More restrictive, more emphatic.
- We have **only** two options. / There's **only** one problem.

### They overlap in the "only" sense but differ in tone

- *It's **just** a bug.* → minimizing, casual: "não é grave"
- *It's **only** a bug.* → same idea, slightly more emphatic
- *I **only** work Mondays.* → restriction of fact
- *I **just** work here.* 🗣️ → "I'm not responsible" — a fixed, slightly defensive expression

### Position changes meaning with `only`

```text
Only I sent the report.      → nobody else sent it
I only sent the report.      → I sent it, I didn't do anything else with it
I sent only the report.      → I sent nothing else
I sent the report only to Ana. → to nobody else
```

In speech, native speakers put `only` before the verb and use **stress** to indicate the meaning: *"I only sent it to **ANA**."*

### The `just` softener — very important professionally

In emails and chat, `just` makes requests less demanding:
- *I **just** wanted to follow up on this.*
- *Just checking — did you get my message?*
- ***Just** a quick question.*
- *I'**m just** wondering if...*

⚠️ Overusing it can sound insecure. Native professionals debate this. Use it once per message, not four times.

**Also memorize:** *just in case, just about* (= almost), *just now, just a second/minute* 🗣️, *just like*, *just as good*, *not just X but Y*.

## 16.2 `kind of` vs `sort of`

### Literal meaning (identical, and interchangeable)

```text
kind of / sort of / type of + noun   =   tipo de
```
- What **kind of** database is it?
- It's a **sort of** cache. / It's a **kind of** wrapper around the API.
- ⚠️ **`kinds of` + plural:** *What kinds of problems are you seeing?*
- ❌ *What kind of a database...* — drop the `a` in most cases.

### Informal meaning: "a bit / somewhat" — HIGH FREQUENCY 🗣️

This is the use that dominates real conversation. It **softens** an adjective, verb, or statement.

- It's **kind of** expensive. *(= meio caro)*
- I **kind of** like it. *(= gosto meio que sim)*
- That's **sort of** what I meant. *(= mais ou menos isso)*
- "Did it work?" — "**Kind of.**" *(= mais ou menos)*
- It's **kinda** weird, but it works.

### Why it matters — softening opinions

English speakers rarely state negative opinions bluntly. `kind of` is one of the main hedging tools:

| Blunt | Softened |
|---|---|
| The design is bad. | The design is **kind of** confusing. |
| I disagree. | I **kind of** disagree. |
| That's wrong. | That's **sort of** missing the point. |

### Pronunciation and spelling 🗣️

```text
kind of → "kinda"  /ˈkaɪndə/
sort of → "sorta"  /ˈsɔːrtə/
```

- These spellings appear in chat, subtitles, and informal writing.
- ✍️ **Do not write `kinda`/`sorta` in professional emails.** Write *kind of* or, better, use a precise word: *somewhat, a bit, fairly*.
- 🇺🇸 `kind of` is more common; 🇬🇧 `sort of` is more common. No real difference.

**Related softeners:** *a bit, a little, somewhat, more or less, I guess, pretty much, sort of like, you know, in a way*.

## 16.3 `still` vs `yet` vs `already`

| Word | Meaning | Typical position | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| **still** | continues — hasn't changed | mid-sentence | I'm **still** waiting. |
| **yet** | up to now (not yet happened) | end; questions/negatives | Has it arrived **yet**? / Not **yet**. |
| **already** | happened, earlier than expected | mid-sentence | I've **already** replied. |

**Key contrasts:**

```text
It hasn't arrived yet.        → neutral. It's expected.
It still hasn't arrived.      → impatient. It's late.
It has already arrived.       → surprise: sooner than expected.
```

- "Are you ready?" — "Not **yet**." / "**Already** done!"
- He **still** works there. *(positive, continuing)*
- He doesn't work there **anymore**. *(stopped)*

⚠️ `still` in negatives goes **before** the auxiliary: *It **still isn't** working* (not *"it isn't still working"*).

## 16.4 `already` vs `just`

- **just** = a very short time ago (minutes): *I've **just** sent it.*
- **already** = before now, sooner than expected: *I've **already** sent it.* (= you didn't need to ask)

Context: your boss asks "Can you send the report?"
- "I **just** sent it." → seconds ago
- "I've **already** sent it." → it was done before you asked

## 16.5 `very` vs `really`

| | very | really |
|---|---|---|
| + adjective | ✅ very good | ✅ really good |
| + adverb | ✅ very quickly | ✅ really quickly |
| + verb | ❌ | ✅ **I really like it.** |
| + strong adjective | ❌ very amazing | ✅ really amazing |
| meaning "truly" | ❌ | ✅ It really happened. |

> 🇧🇷 The #1 error: ❌ *"I very like it."* → ✅ *"I **really** like it."* / *"I like it **a lot**."*
> `very` never modifies a verb. Use **really**, **a lot**, or **very much** (formal, after the object).

## 16.6 `too` vs `very`

```text
very = high degree (neutral)
too  = MORE THAN acceptable (negative)
```

- It's **very** expensive. *(but maybe I'll buy it)*
- It's **too** expensive. *(so I won't buy it)*
- ✅ *This coffee is very hot* — good. *This coffee is too hot* — I can't drink it.

**Structures:**
```text
too + adjective + to + verb     →  It's too late to call.
too much / too many + noun      →  too much work / too many meetings
adjective + enough + to         →  It's good enough to ship.
not + adjective + enough        →  It's not fast enough.
```

> 🇧🇷 Portuguese *"muito"* covers both **very** and **too much**. Getting this wrong reverses your meaning:
> ```text
> Esse projeto é muito caro.
>         ↓
> ❌ This project is too expensive.  (= we can't do it)
>         ↓
> ✅ This project is very expensive. (= it costs a lot)
> ```

**Also note `too` = "also"** at the end of a sentence: *I'm coming **too**.*

## 16.7 `so` vs `such`

```text
so   + adjective/adverb            → It's so slow.
so   + much/many/few/little        → so many bugs
such + (a/an) + (adjective) + noun → It's such a slow system.
```

- ✅ She's **so** talented. / She's **such a** talented designer.
- ✅ It was **so** cold. / It was **such** cold weather.
- ✅ There were **so many** people. / It was **such a** crowd.
- ❌ It's so a good idea. / ❌ It's such good. → ✅ It's **such a good idea** / **so good**.

**With result clauses:**
- It was **so** expensive **that** we cancelled.
- It was **such an** expensive project **that** we cancelled.

## 16.8 `say` vs `tell`

```text
say   + something  (+ to + person)     → He said hello. He said it to me.
tell  + person     + something          → He told me the news.
```

**The rule:** `tell` needs a person right after it. `say` doesn't take a person directly.

| ✅ | ❌ |
|---|---|
| He **said** he was late. | He said me he was late. |
| He **told me** he was late. | He told he was late. |
| She **said** "no". | She told "no". |
| What did he **say**? | What did he tell? |
| **Tell** me the truth. | Say me the truth. |
| He **said to me** that... | |

**Fixed expressions with `tell`** (no "say" version): *tell a story, tell a joke, tell the truth, tell a lie, tell the time, tell the difference, tell someone about something*.
**Fixed with `say`:** *say hello/goodbye, say sorry, say a word, say please, say yes/no, say a prayer*.

🗣️ Also: *"Tell you what..."* (= I have an idea), *"You're telling me!"* (= totally agree), *"I told you so."*

## 16.9 `speak` vs `talk`

Mostly interchangeable. Practical differences:

| speak | talk |
|---|---|
| slightly more formal | more conversational |
| languages: *I speak Portuguese* | ❌ I talk Portuguese |
| one direction / to an audience: *She spoke at the conference* | two-way: *We talked for an hour* |
| phone: *Can I speak to Ana?* | *Can I talk to Ana?* (also fine) |
| *speak up* (louder), *speaking of...* | *talk about, talk it over, talk someone into* |

- ✅ Can I **speak/talk to** you for a minute?
- ✅ **Speaking of** deadlines, when is it due?
- ✅ Let's **talk about** it tomorrow. *(more natural than "speak about")*

## 16.10 `make` vs `do`

```text
make = create, produce, cause something to exist
do   = perform an activity, general/unspecified actions
```

| make | do |
|---|---|
| make a decision | do business |
| make a mistake | do homework |
| make money | do research |
| make a plan | do a favor |
| make progress | do the dishes |
| make an effort | do exercise |
| make a suggestion | do a job |
| make a phone call | do your best |
| make an appointment | do damage |
| make sense | do harm |
| make sure | do the shopping 🇬🇧 |
| make a difference | do work |
| make friends | do something/anything/nothing |
| make coffee/dinner | do a test ⚠️ (also "take a test" 🇺🇸) |
| make a reservation | do the paperwork |
| make a change | do an analysis |
| make noise | do a course 🇬🇧 / take a course 🇺🇸 |

> 🇧🇷 Portuguese has one verb, *fazer*, for both. This produces constant errors:
> ```text
> Fazer uma pergunta   →  ❌ make/do a question  →  ✅ ask a question
> Fazer uma reunião    →  ❌ do a meeting        →  ✅ have a meeting
> Fazer aniversário    →  ❌ do birthday         →  ✅ It's my birthday / I'm turning 30
> Fazer um curso       →  ❌ make a course       →  ✅ take a course
> Fazer sentido        →  ❌ do sense            →  ✅ make sense
> Fazer uma prova      →  ❌ make a test         →  ✅ take a test
> Fazer parte de       →  ❌ make part of        →  ✅ be part of
> Fazer o café         →  ✅ make coffee
> Fazer compras        →  ✅ go shopping / do the shopping
> ```
> **Memory hook:** you *make* things that didn't exist before (a decision, a plan, coffee). You *do* activities and tasks (work, homework, the dishes).

## 16.11 `know` vs `meet`

```text
know = have knowledge of / be acquainted with (a state)
meet = encounter for the first time, or have a meeting (an action)
```

- ✅ I'**ve known** her for ten years. *(state, ongoing)*
- ✅ I **met** her ten years ago. *(the first encounter)*
- ✅ Nice to **meet** you. *(first time)* / Nice to **see** you. *(not the first time)*
- ✅ We'**re meeting** at 3. *(a meeting)*

> 🇧🇷 *"Eu conheci ele ano passado"* → ❌ *I knew him last year* → ✅ **I met him last year.**
> *"Eu conheço ele"* → ✅ **I know him.**
> *"Conhece Paris?"* → ❌ *Do you know Paris?* ⚠️ (possible but odd) → ✅ **Have you (ever) been to Paris?**

## 16.12 `see` vs `look` vs `watch`

| Verb | Meaning | Structure | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| **see** | perceive with your eyes (passive) | see + object | I **saw** an error message. |
| **look (at)** | direct your eyes deliberately (static) | look **at** + object | **Look at** this graph. |
| **watch** | look at something over time / that moves | watch + object | I **watched** the demo. |

- ✅ I **see** what you mean. *(= understand)*
- ✅ Can you **look at** this code? *(examine)*
- ✅ We **watched** the presentation / a movie / TV.
- ✅ I **saw** a movie last night. ⚠️ *(also correct — "see a movie" = go to the cinema)*
- ❌ I'm seeing TV. → ✅ I'**m watching** TV.
- ❌ Look this. → ✅ **Look at** this.

**Also:** *see you later, see about it, let's see, watch out! 🗣️ (careful!), look into it (investigate)*.

## 16.13 `hear` vs `listen`

```text
hear   = perceive sound (no intention)     → I heard a noise.
listen = pay attention to sound (intention) → I'm listening to a podcast.
```

- ❌ I'm hearing music. → ✅ I'm **listening to** music.
- ❌ Listen me! → ✅ **Listen to** me!
- ✅ Can you **hear** me? *(on a call — always `hear`, never `listen`)*
- ✅ I **heard** you're leaving. *(= I received the information)*
- ✅ **Listen**, we need to talk. 🗣️ *(attention-getter)*

## 16.14 `bring` vs `take`

```text
bring = movement TOWARD the speaker/listener
take  = movement AWAY from here
```

- **Bring** your laptop to the meeting. *(to where I'll be)*
- **Take** this to Ana's desk. *(away from here)*
- Can you **bring** me a coffee? / I'll **take** the documents with me.
- Also: *bring up* (mention), *take away* (remove), *bring back* (return something here).

> 🇧🇷 Portuguese *trazer/levar* maps almost exactly. The confusion comes from *"Vou levar você"* → ✅ *I'll **take** you* / *I'll **give** you a ride* (not "bring").

## 16.15 `come` vs `go`

Same logic:
```text
come = movement toward the speaker or the listener
go   = movement away
```

- "Dinner's ready!" — "I'm **coming**!" *(toward you — NOT "I'm going")*
- Are you **coming** to the party? *(the speaker will be there)*
- I'm **going** to the office. *(away from here)*
- ✅ Can I **come** with you? *(joining you)*

> 🇧🇷 *"Já vou!"* → ❌ *I'm going!* → ✅ **I'm coming!**

## 16.16 `remember` vs `remind`

```text
remember = the memory is in YOUR head           → I remember his name.
remind   = someone/something CAUSES you to remember → Remind me to call him.
```

- ✅ **Remind** me to send the invoice. *(you cause me to remember)*
- ✅ I don't **remember** her name.
- ✅ This song **reminds me of** my childhood.
- ❌ Remember me to call him. → ✅ **Remind** me to call him.
- ❌ It remembers me of Lisbon. → ✅ It **reminds me of** Lisbon.

> 🇧🇷 *lembrar* covers both — *"me lembra de ligar"* and *"eu lembro"*. Watch this one; it's very frequent.

**Also:** `remember doing` (a memory of the past) vs `remember to do` (not forget): see [Section 22](#22-gerunds-and-infinitives).

## 16.17 `learn` vs `teach`

```text
learn = receive knowledge   → I'm learning Python.
teach = give knowledge      → She teaches Python.
```
- ❌ Can you learn me English? → ✅ Can you **teach** me English?
- ✅ I **learned** it from a colleague. / She **taught** me how to use it.
- ⚠️ *learn* is irregular in 🇬🇧: *learnt* = *learned* (both fine).

## 16.18 `borrow` vs `lend`

```text
borrow = take (temporarily) FROM someone   → Can I borrow your charger?
lend   = give (temporarily) TO someone     → Can you lend me your charger?
```

- ✅ I **borrowed** €50 **from** him. = He **lent** me €50.
- ❌ Can you borrow me your pen? → ✅ Can you **lend** me your pen? / Can I **borrow** your pen?
- *lend* is irregular: lend → **lent** → lent.

> 🇧🇷 *emprestar* means both in Portuguese (*"me empresta"* / *"peguei emprestado"*), which is exactly why this is a top-10 mistake.

## 16.19 `job` vs `work`

| job (countable) | work (uncountable) |
|---|---|
| a position, a role | activity, effort, the place |
| I have a good **job**. | I have a lot of **work**. |
| She got a new **job**. | She's at **work**. |
| It's a hard **job**. | It's hard **work**. |
| ❌ a work | ❌ works ⚠️ |

- ✅ What's your **job**? / What do you do **for work**? / What do you **do**?
- ✅ I'm looking for a **job**. ❌ I'm looking for a work.
- ✅ I have too much **work**. ❌ too many works.
- ✅ **Good job!** 🗣️ *(= parabéns, bom trabalho)* — also *Nice work!*
- Related: **career** (long-term path), **position/role** (formal for job), **task** (one unit of work), **occupation** (form-filling word).

## 16.20 `fun` vs `funny`

```text
fun   = enjoyable, pleasant (divertido)     → The party was fun.
funny = makes you laugh, or strange (engraçado / esquisito)  → He's funny.
```

- ✅ We had **fun**. / It was a **fun** project.
- ✅ That joke was **funny**.
- ⚠️ *"That's funny"* can mean "that's strange": *"That's funny — it worked yesterday."*
- ❌ The party was very funny. *(unless everyone was laughing at it)* → ✅ The party was a lot of **fun** / really **fun**.

## 16.21 `house` vs `home`

```text
house = the building
home  = where you live (emotional/functional), any type of dwelling
```

- ✅ They bought a **house**. / They're **at home**.
- ✅ I work from **home**. / I'm working **at home** today. ❌ I work from house.
- ✅ Go **home** — no `to`, no article. ❌ go to the home
- ✅ Make yourself at **home**. / There's no place like **home**.
- People who live in apartments say **home**, not "house."

## 16.22 `look for` vs `find`

```text
look for = search (the process)     → I'm looking for my keys.
find     = the result (success)     → I found them!
```

- ❌ I'm finding a job. → ✅ I'm **looking for** a job.
- ✅ Did you **find** it? / I've been **looking for** it all morning.
- Related: **search for** (more formal/technical), **look up** (find in a reference: *look up the word*), **find out** (discover information: *I found out he quit*).

## 16.23 `wait` vs `expect` vs `hope`

```text
wait (for)  = stay until something happens (time passing)
expect      = believe something will happen (prediction)
hope        = want something to happen (desire)
```

- ✅ I'm **waiting for** the build to finish. *(sitting here)*
- ✅ I **expect** the build to fail. *(prediction, maybe negative)*
- ✅ I **hope** the build passes. *(desire)*
- ❌ I'm waiting the bus. → ✅ I'm **waiting for** the bus.
- ⚠️ I'm hoping you're fine. → ✅ I **hope** you're doing well. *(`hope` is a state verb — avoid the continuous)*

> 🇧🇷 *esperar* means all three. This is a genuine source of misunderstanding:
> ```text
> Espero que você venha.   → ✅ I hope you come.   (desire)
> Estou esperando você.    → ✅ I'm waiting for you. (time)
> Espero que ele chegue às 3. → ✅ I expect him at 3. (prediction)
> ```
> Also useful: *I'm looking forward to it* (positive anticipation), *I can't wait!* 🗣️ (excitement).

## 16.24 `hope` vs `wish`

```text
hope + present/future (possible)      → I hope you get the job.
wish + past form (impossible/regret)  → I wish I had more time.
```

- ✅ I **hope** it works. *(it might)*
- ✅ I **wish** it worked. *(it doesn't — regret)*
- ✅ I **wish** I could help. *(I can't)*
- ✅ I **wish** I hadn't said that. *(regret about the past)*
- ✅ **I wish you** a happy birthday. *(fixed expression = desire for someone)*
- ❌ I wish you get the job. → ✅ I **hope** you get the job.

## 16.25 `maybe` vs `may be`

```text
maybe  = one word, adverb = "perhaps"  → Maybe he's busy.
may be = two words, verb              → He may be busy.
```
- ✅ **Maybe** we should wait. = We **may be** waiting a while. *(different structures)*
- ❌ May be he's late. → ✅ **Maybe** he's late. / He **may be** late.
- **Position:** `maybe` usually goes at the start 🗣️; `perhaps` is its more formal twin and moves around freely.

## 16.26 `especially` vs `specially`

```text
especially = above all, particularly (most common)
specially  = for one specific purpose (rare)
```
- ✅ I like Italian food, **especially** pasta.
- ✅ It's **especially** important now.
- ✅ This report was **specially** prepared for the board. *(made for that purpose)*
- **Practical advice:** 90% of the time you want **especially**.
- ❌ Specially in the morning → ✅ **Especially** in the morning.

## 16.27 `actually` vs `currently`

**This is the single most common false friend for Portuguese speakers.**

```text
actually  = in fact / really (na verdade)
currently = now, at the present time (atualmente)
```

- ✅ **Actually**, the meeting is at 4, not 3. *(correction)*
- ✅ I'm **currently** working on the migration. *(now)*
- ❌ Actually I work at Google. *(means "in fact," not "these days")*
- ✅ **Currently** / **At the moment** / **These days** I work at Google.

Also: **actual** = real/true (❌ not "atual"). *The actual cost was higher.* For "atual," use **current**: *the current version*.

## 16.28 `eventually` vs `finally` vs `possibly`

```text
eventually = at some point in the future, after a while (por fim, com o tempo)
finally    = at last, after a long wait (finalmente)
possibly   = maybe (eventualmente = "possibly/occasionally" in PT!)
```

- ✅ He **eventually** agreed. *(after some time/resistance)*
- ✅ **Finally**! The build passed. *(relief after waiting)*
- ✅ We might **possibly** need more time.
- ❌ *"Eventually I work on Saturdays"* — this does NOT mean *"eventualmente"*. Say: *"**Occasionally** / **Sometimes** I work on Saturdays."*

> 🇧🇷 **`eventually` is a false friend.** PT *eventualmente* = *occasionally, possibly*. EN *eventually* = *no final, com o tempo*.

## 16.29 `real` vs `really`

```text
real   = adjective (describes a noun)  → a real problem
really = adverb (describes a verb/adjective) → really good, I really think so
```
- ❌ It's real good. 🗣️ *(common in casual American speech, non-standard)*
- ✅ It's **really** good.
- ✅ That's a **real** issue. / Is this **real**?

## 16.30 `good` vs `well`

```text
good = adjective  → a good result; It looks good.
well = adverb     → It works well; She speaks English well.
```

- ❌ She speaks English good. → ✅ ...**well**.
- ✅ The team did **well**. / The team did a **good** job.
- ⚠️ After `be/feel/look/seem`, use **good**: *I feel good. You look good.*
- ⚠️ `well` is also an adjective meaning "healthy": *I don't feel well* = I'm slightly ill.
- 🗣️ "How are you?" — "**Good**, thanks." *(technically "well" is more correct, but "good" is universal)*

## 16.31 `hard` vs `hardly`

**These are almost opposites. This is a real misunderstanding risk.**

```text
hard   = with effort / difficult  → I work hard.
hardly = almost not at all         → I hardly work.
```

- ✅ She studies **hard**. *(a lot of effort)*
- ✅ She **hardly** studies. *(almost never)*
- ✅ I could **hardly** hear you. *(= I almost couldn't hear you)*
- ✅ **hardly ever** = almost never: *I hardly ever check LinkedIn.*
- ✅ **hardly any** = almost none: *There's hardly any time left.*
- Note: `hardly` is already negative — don't add another negative.

## 16.32 `late` vs `lately`

```text
late   = not on time / at a late hour
lately = recently (= "recently", used with Present Perfect)
```
- ✅ I arrived **late**. / He works **late**. *(until a late hour)*
- ✅ I've been busy **lately**. *(= recently)*
- ❌ I've been arriving lately. *(means "recently," not "late")* → ✅ I've been arriving **late**.
- Also: *later* (afterwards), *the latest* (most recent), *the last* (final).

## 16.33 `near` vs `nearly`

```text
near   = close (distance)
nearly = almost (= almost)
```
- ✅ The station is **near** here. / I live **near** the office.
- ✅ I'm **nearly** finished. *(= almost done)*
- ✅ It's **nearly** 6 o'clock.
- ❌ I nearly the office. → ✅ I'm **near** the office.

## 16.34 More distinctions worth knowing

### `travel` vs `trip` vs `journey`
- **travel** = the general activity (uncountable verb/noun): *I love travel(ling).*
- **trip** = one journey with a purpose: *a business trip, a trip to Spain*
- ❌ *I made a travel* → ✅ I **took a trip** / I **went on a trip**.
