- **Have a good trip!** *(= boa viagem)*

### `during` vs `for` vs `while`
- **during + noun** (when): *during the meeting*
- **for + duration** (how long): *for two hours*
- **while + clause**: *while we were meeting*

### `win` vs `earn` vs `gain` vs `beat`
- **win** a game/prize/election. **beat** an opponent: *We **beat** them 3–0.* (❌ *we won them*)
- **earn** money by working: *I **earn** €4,000 a month.* (❌ *I win €4,000*)
- **gain** = increase: *gain experience, gain weight, gain access*.
> 🇧🇷 *ganhar* covers all of these — a very frequent error.

### `lose` vs `miss`
- **lose** = no longer have: *I lost my keys / we lost the game*
- **miss** = fail to catch/attend, or feel the absence: *I **missed** the meeting. I **miss** you. You **missed** the point.*
- ❌ I lost the train. → ✅ I **missed** the train.

### `spend` vs `pass` (time)
- ✅ I **spent** two hours on it. ❌ I passed two hours.
- ✅ Time **passes** quickly. / We **spent** the weekend in Porto.

### `arrive` vs `reach` vs `get`
- **arrive at** a place / **arrive in** a city / country
- **get to** a place 🗣️ (most common): *What time did you **get** here?*
- **reach** + object (no preposition): *We **reached** the office at 9.* / *I couldn't **reach** him.* (contact)
- ❌ arrive to → ✅ arrive **at/in**

### `remember` vs `remind` vs `memorize`
- Covered above; **memorize** = deliberately commit to memory.

### `economic` vs `economical`
- **economic** = relating to the economy: *economic growth*
- **economical** = cheap to run: *an economical car*

### `sensible` vs `sensitive`
- **sensible** = reasonable, practical (❌ not "sensível"): *a sensible decision*
- **sensitive** = easily affected, delicate (= sensível): *sensitive data, a sensitive person*

### `historic` vs `historical`
- **historic** = important in history: *a historic decision*
- **historical** = relating to history: *historical records*

### `stay` vs `be` vs `keep`
- ❌ *Stay calm* is fine, but ❌ *"I stayed happy"* usually → ✅ *I was happy*.
- 🇧🇷 *ficar* is a trap: *ficar bravo* → **get angry**; *ficar em casa* → **stay home**; *ficar pronto* → **be ready**; *ficar sabendo* → **find out**; *fica bom* → **it looks good**.

### `explain` and `answer` — no "to" before the thing
- ✅ **Explain** the problem **to me**. ❌ Explain me the problem.
- ✅ **Answer** the question. ❌ Answer to the question.

### `advice` vs `advise`
- **advice** = noun (uncountable): *good advice*. **advise** = verb: *I advise you to wait.*
- Same pattern: *practice/practise* 🇬🇧, *licence/license* 🇬🇧.

### `affect` vs `effect`
- **affect** = verb (to influence): *This affects performance.*
- **effect** = noun (the result): *It has a big effect.* (✍️ verb "effect change" is rare/formal)

### `then` vs `than`
- **then** = time/sequence: *First A, then B.* **than** = comparison: *bigger than*.
- Native speakers misspell this constantly; don't imitate.

### `its` vs `it's`, `your` vs `you're`, `their/there/they're`
- The most common written English errors, including among natives. Learn them and you'll write better than many.

---

# 17. Phrasal Verbs

## 17.1 What they are and why they matter

A phrasal verb = **verb + particle** (a preposition or adverb), where the combination has a meaning you often can't guess from the parts.

```text
give  +  up   =  quit (not "give in an upward direction")
look  +  after = take care of
run   +  out of = have nothing left
```

**Why they matter:** phrasal verbs are the **default vocabulary of spoken English**. Every phrasal verb usually has a Latin-origin synonym, but the single-word version sounds formal.

| Everyday (phrasal) | Formal (Latin) |
|---|---|
| find out | discover |
| put off | postpone |
| set up | establish, configure |
| carry out | conduct, perform |
| bring up | mention, raise |
| look into | investigate |
| go over | review |
| come up with | devise, generate |
| deal with | handle, address |
| get rid of | eliminate |
| turn down | reject, decline |
| point out | indicate |
| leave out | omit |

> **Practical strategy:** you don't need to *produce* many phrasal verbs — the formal synonyms are perfectly good English. But you must **understand** them, because native speakers use them constantly. Prioritize recognition; add production gradually.

## 17.2 Separable vs inseparable

### Separable — the object can go in the middle

```text
turn on the light   =   turn the light on
```

**BUT: if the object is a pronoun, it MUST go in the middle.**

- ✅ Turn **it** on. ❌ Turn on it.
- ✅ Pick **me** up at 8. ❌ Pick up me.
- ✅ I'll call **you** back. ❌ I'll call back you.
- ✅ Throw **it** away. ❌ Throw away it.

Common separable ones: *turn on/off, pick up, put on, take off, give up ⚠️, fill in/out, look up, throw away, call back, set up, bring up, write down, work out, figure out, turn down, put off, hand in*.

### Inseparable — never split

```text
look after the kids   ❌ look the kids after
```

- ✅ I'm **looking for** it. ❌ I'm looking it for.
- ✅ I ran **into** her. ❌ I ran her into.
- ✅ We're **looking forward to** the launch.

Common inseparable ones: *look for, look after, look forward to, run into, get on/off, come across, deal with, go through, take after, get over, count on, run out of*.

### Three-word phrasal verbs — always inseparable

*look forward to, put up with, get along with, run out of, catch up with, come up with, get rid of, look down on, keep up with, get away with*

- ✅ I can't **put up with** this anymore.
- ✅ We **came up with** a solution.

**Practical rule:** when in doubt, put the object **after** the whole phrasal verb — unless it's a pronoun, in which case try the middle. If it sounds wrong either way, use a single-word synonym.

## 17.3 How to recognize and learn them

- **Watch the particle, not just the verb.** *Turn up* (appear / increase volume), *turn down* (reject / lower), *turn into* (become), *turn out* (result), *turn over* (flip), *turn off* (stop / disgust). Same verb, six meanings.
- **Learn them in a phrase, not alone:** don't memorize *"put off = postpone"*. Memorize *"Let's put the meeting off until next week."*
- **Common particle meanings** (loose patterns, not rules):
  - **up** = completion or increase: *finish up, drink up, speed up, use up*
  - **out** = removing / discovering: *find out, throw out, sort out, run out*
  - **off** = separating / stopping: *take off, call off, put off, log off*
  - **on** = continuing: *carry on, go on, hold on, move on*
  - **down** = reducing / recording: *slow down, turn down, write down, break down*
  - **back** = returning: *call back, get back, come back, pay back*

## 17.4 The high-frequency list (curated)

### The `get` family — the most useful verb in English

| Phrasal verb | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **get up** | leave bed / stand up | I **get up** at 6. |
| **get in** | enter (car, building) / arrive | What time does your flight **get in**? |
| **get out (of)** | leave / exit | **Get out of** the car. |
| **get on** | board (bus/plane) / progress | **Get on** the train at platform 3. |
| **get off** | leave (bus/plane) / finish work | I **get off** at 6. |
| **get back** | return / receive again | I'll **get back** to you. *(essential at work)* |
| **get along (with)** | have a good relationship | I **get along** well with my team. |
| **get over** | recover from | It took a week to **get over** the flu. |
| **get through** | finish / survive / reach by phone | I couldn't **get through** to support. |
| **get rid of** | eliminate | We need to **get rid of** this legacy code. |
| **get by** | manage, survive | I **get by** with basic Spanish. |
| **get around to** | finally do | I never **got around to** fixing it. |
| **get together** | meet socially | Let's **get together** next week. |

### The `look` family

| Phrasal verb | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **look for** | search for | I'm **looking for** the config file. |
| **look at** | examine, direct eyes | Can you **look at** this error? |
| **look after** | take care of | Who's **looking after** the servers? |
| **look forward to** + -ing | anticipate positively | I'**m looking forward to** meeting you. |
| **look into** | investigate | I'll **look into** it. *(top-5 work phrase)* |
| **look up** | find in a reference | **Look** it **up** in the docs. |
| **look out** 🗣️ | be careful | **Look out!** |
| **look like** | resemble / seem | It **looks like** a memory leak. |

> ⚠️ **`look forward to` takes `-ing`**, because `to` is a preposition here, not an infinitive marker:
> ❌ I look forward to meet you. → ✅ I look forward to **meeting** you.
> Also note: *"I'm looking forward to..."* (warmer) vs *"I look forward to..."* ✍️ (formal email closing).

### Everyday actions

| Phrasal verb | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **turn on / off** | start/stop a device | **Turn** the AC **on**. |
| **turn up / down** | increase/decrease; appear/reject | **Turn** the volume **down**. / He **turned down** the offer. |
| **put on / take off** | wear / remove (clothes) | **Put on** your jacket. |
| **take off** | (plane) leave the ground / grow fast | The flight **took off** late. / Sales **took off**. |
| **pick up** | collect / learn casually / answer phone | I'll **pick you up** at 8. |
| **drop off** | deliver / leave someone somewhere | Can you **drop** me **off** at the station? |
| **wake up** | stop sleeping | I **woke up** at 5. |
| **come back / go back** | return | I'll **come back** later. |
| **run out of** | have no more | We'**ve run out of** time. |
| **throw away** | discard | Don't **throw** it **away**. |
| **fill in / out** | complete a form | Please **fill out** this form. |
| **hang on / hold on** 🗣️ | wait | **Hold on** a second. |
| **hang up** | end a call | She **hung up** on me. |
| **check in / out** | hotel, airport, verify | Let me **check** that **out**. |

### Work and problem-solving

| Phrasal verb | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **find out** | discover information | I **found out** they're merging. |
| **figure out** | solve, understand | I can't **figure out** why it fails. |
| **work out** | solve / turn out well / exercise | It **worked out** in the end. |
| **set up** | configure, arrange, establish | Let's **set up** a call. |
| **carry on / go on** | continue | **Carry on** — I'm listening. / What's **going on**? |
| **give up** | quit, stop trying | Don't **give up**. |
| **bring up** | mention, raise a topic | She **brought up** a good point. |
| **come up** | arise (unexpectedly) | Something **came up** — I have to cancel. |
| **come up with** | invent, produce an idea | We **came up with** three options. |
| **put off** | postpone | Let's **put** the release **off**. |
| **call off** | cancel | They **called off** the meeting. |
| **go over / go through** | review / examine in detail | Let's **go over** the numbers. |
| **point out** | indicate, mention | He **pointed out** an error. |
| **sort out** 🇬🇧 | resolve | I'll **sort** it **out**. |
| **deal with** | handle | Who's **dealing with** this ticket? |
| **follow up (on)** | check progress after | I'm **following up on** my email. |
| **catch up (on/with)** | reach the same level / update | Let's **catch up** next week. |
| **back up** | make a copy / support | Did you **back up** the database? |
| **break down** | stop working / analyze in parts | The server **broke down**. / Let's **break** it **down**. |
| **shut down** | turn off completely / close | We're **shutting down** the old system. |
| **roll out** | launch gradually | We'll **roll out** the feature next month. |
| **scale up / down** | increase/decrease capacity | We need to **scale up**. |
| **log in / out** | access a system | I can't **log in**. |
| **sign up (for)** | register | **Sign up** for the newsletter. |
| **reach out (to)** 🇺🇸 | contact | I'll **reach out to** the vendor. |
| **touch base** 🗣️ | briefly contact | Let's **touch base** on Friday. |
| **run by** | check with someone | Let me **run** this **by** my manager. |
| **hand in / turn in** | submit | **Hand in** your report by Friday. |
| **take over** | assume control | She'**s taking over** the project. |
| **lay off** | dismiss (for economic reasons) | They **laid off** 200 people. |
| **end up** | finally be/do (unplanned) | We **ended up** rewriting it. |

### Social and personal

| Phrasal verb | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **grow up** | become an adult | I **grew up** in Recife. |
| **get along with** | have a good relationship | They don't **get along**. |
| **break up (with)** | end a relationship | They **broke up** last year. |
| **run into** | meet by chance | I **ran into** an old colleague. |
| **hang out** 🗣️ | spend time casually | We **hung out** after work. |
| **show up / turn up** | arrive, appear | He didn't **show up**. |
| **calm down** | become calm | **Calm down**, it's fixable. |
| **cheer up** | become happier | **Cheer up!** |
| **put up with** | tolerate | I can't **put up with** the noise. |
| **make up** | invent / reconcile / compensate | He **made up** an excuse. |
| **take after** | resemble (family) | She **takes after** her mother. |
| **look down on / up to** | despise / admire | I **look up to** her. |
| **count on** | rely on | You can **count on** me. |
| **go out** | leave for social reasons | Let's **go out** tonight. |
| **eat out** | eat at a restaurant | We **ate out** last night. |

## 17.5 The "don't overdo it" warning

Learners sometimes force phrasal verbs to sound native and produce odd sentences. If you're unsure:
- ✅ *We will **investigate** the issue.* — perfectly good professional English.
- ✅ *We'll **look into** it.* — more natural, but only if you're confident.

Both are fine. Never sacrifice clarity for the appearance of fluency.

---

# 18. Spoken English

Textbook English and spoken English are different languages in practice. If you have only studied written English, this section explains why native speech sounds like an undifferentiated stream of sound.

**The core principle:** English speakers **compress everything that isn't important**. Content words (nouns, main verbs, adjectives) are pronounced clearly. Everything else (articles, prepositions, auxiliaries, pronouns) gets squeezed.

## 18.1 Contractions in speech — mandatory, not optional

In natural speech, the full forms sound wrong or overly emphatic:

- ⚠️ "I **am** going to the office." → sounds like you're arguing with someone
- ✅ "I'**m** going to the office."

Even beyond the standard written contractions, speech contracts further:

| Written | Spoken |
|---|---|
| that is → that's | *thats* |
| there is → there's | *therez* |
| what is → what's | *whats* |
| who is → who's | *whooz* |
| it will → it'll | *itl* |
| that will → that'll | *thatl* |
| there will → there'll | *therel* |
| I would have → I'd have | *I'd-uv* / *Ida* |
| should have → should've | *shoulda* |
| could have → could've | *coulda* |
| would have → would've | *woulda* |
| must have → must've | *musta* |

> **Listening consequence:** *"I would have told you"* sounds like **"I'da told ya."** If you're waiting to hear four separate words, you will miss the sentence entirely.

## 18.2 The classic reductions

These are the ones every learner should **recognize instantly**:

| Full form | Reduced | Example |
|---|---|---|
| going to | **gonna** | I'm **gonna** check. |
| want to | **wanna** | Do you **wanna** grab lunch? |
| got to / have got to | **gotta** | I **gotta** go. |
| have to | **hafta** | I **hafta** finish this. |
| has to | **hasta** | She **hasta** approve it. |
| kind of | **kinda** | It's **kinda** slow. |
| sort of | **sorta** | I **sorta** agree. |
| let me | **lemme** | **Lemme** check. |
| give me | **gimme** | **Gimme** a sec. |
| out of | **outta** | We're **outta** time. |
| a lot of | **alotta** | There's **alotta** work. |
| don't know | **dunno** | I **dunno**. |
| what do you | **whaddya** | **Whaddya** think? |
| what are you | **whatcha** | **Whatcha** doing? |
| did you | **didja** | **Didja** see that? |
| would you | **wouldja** | **Wouldja** mind? |
| what is up | **what's up / 'sup** | **What's up**? |
| because | **'cause / cuz** | **'Cause** it's broken. |
| them | **'em** | Tell **'em** I'm coming. |
| you | **ya** | See **ya**! |
| and | **'n'** | rock **'n'** roll |
| probably | **prolly** 🗣️ | I'll **prolly** be late. |

### When are these appropriate?

| Context | Use them? |
|---|---|
| Casual conversation with friends | ✅ Yes, natural |
| Team chat (Slack, WhatsApp) | ✅ Yes, common |
| Video calls with colleagues | ✅ Yes, in speech |
| Presentations | ⚠️ Some (*gonna* is fine, *gimme* isn't) |
| Interviews | ⚠️ In speech yes, but keep it moderate |
| Professional emails | ❌ Write the full form |
| Documentation, reports, contracts | ❌ Never |
| Job applications | ❌ Never |

> **Critical distinction:** **`gonna` is a pronunciation, not a word.** Native speakers write "going to" and say "gonna." You should do the same. Writing *"I'm gonna send the proposal"* in a client email looks careless.

> **One grammar trap:** `gonna` only replaces *going to* + **verb**, never *going to* + **place**.
> - ✅ I'm **gonna** call him. *(going to call)*
> - ❌ I'm gonna the office. → ✅ I'm **going to** the office.

## 18.3 Connected speech — why words disappear

Native speakers don't put spaces between words. Four processes explain most of it:

### 1. Linking (consonant → vowel)

The final consonant attaches to the next word's vowel:
```text
an apple        → "a-napple"
turn it off     → "tur-ni-toff"
pick it up      → "pi-ki-tup"
not at all      → "no-da-tall"
an hour and a half → "a-nou-ran-da-half"
```

### 2. Elision (sounds disappear)

Especially **/t/** and **/d/** between consonants:
```text
next day        → "nex day"
last night      → "las night"
sandwich        → "sanwich"
I don't know    → "I dono"
must be         → "muss be"
friendship      → "frenship"
```

### 3. Assimilation (sounds change to blend)

```text
did you     → "didja"     /dɪdʒə/
would you   → "wouldja"   /wʊdʒə/
don't you   → "doncha"    /doʊntʃə/
got you     → "gotcha"    /ɡɑtʃə/
meet you    → "meetcha"
ten minutes → "tem minutes"
```

### 4. Weak forms (unstressed function words become /ə/)

This is the biggest one. **~40 common words have a strong form and a weak form**, and the weak form is what you actually hear:

| Word | Strong | Weak (normal) |
|---|---|---|
| a | /eɪ/ | /ə/ |
| the | /ðiː/ | /ðə/ |
| to | /tuː/ | /tə/ |
| of | /ɒv/ | /əv/ or just /ə/ |
| for | /fɔːr/ | /fər/ |
| and | /ænd/ | /ən/ or /n/ |
| but | /bʌt/ | /bət/ |
| at | /æt/ | /ət/ |
| from | /frɒm/ | /frəm/ |
| can | /kæn/ | /kən/ |
| was | /wɒz/ | /wəz/ |
| were | /wɜːr/ | /wər/ |
| have | /hæv/ | /əv/ |
| has | /hæz/ | /əz/ |
| are | /ɑːr/ | /ər/ |
| you | /juː/ | /jə/ |
| your | /jɔːr/ | /jər/ |
| he / him / her | /hiː/ | /i/ /ɪm/ /ər/ — the **h disappears** |

**Example of everything at once:**

```text
Written:  What do you want to do about it?
Spoken:   "Whaddya wanna do about it?"   /ˈwʌdəjə ˈwɑnə ˈduː əˈbaʊdɪt/

Written:  Did he tell her about the meeting?
Spoken:   "Di-di tell-er about the meeting?"  (the h's vanish)

Written:  I would have called you.
Spoken:   "I-da call-jew."
```

> **This is why you understand written English but not spoken English.** It isn't your vocabulary — it's that the audio doesn't match the spelling you have in your head.

## 18.4 Sentence stress and rhythm

English is **stress-timed**: stressed syllables come at roughly regular intervals, and everything between them gets compressed to fit.

```text
CATS   EAT   FISH.                        (3 beats)
The CATS have EATen the FISH.             (still 3 beats — the extra words are squeezed)
```

**What gets stressed:** nouns, main verbs, adjectives, adverbs, question words, negatives.
**What doesn't:** articles, prepositions, auxiliaries, pronouns, conjunctions.

**Stress changes meaning** — an important and underused tool:

```text
I didn't say he stole the money.  → someone else said it
I DIDN'T say he stole the money.  → I deny saying it
I didn't SAY he stole the money.  → I implied it
I didn't say HE stole the money.  → someone else stole it
I didn't say he STOLE the money.  → he did something else with it
I didn't say he stole THE MONEY.  → he stole something else
```

> 🇧🇷 **Portuguese is syllable-timed** — every syllable gets roughly equal time. Applying Portuguese rhythm to English makes you sound "flat" and, more importantly, makes it **harder for natives to understand you**, because they rely on stress to find the important words.
>
> **Practical fix:** when speaking, deliberately make content words longer and louder, and rush through the small words. This does more for your intelligibility than perfecting individual sounds.

## 18.5 Intonation basics

| Pattern | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Falling ↘ at the end | statement, WH-question, certainty |
| Rising ↗ at the end | yes/no question, uncertainty, "is that OK?" |
| Rising then falling ↗↘ | contrast, surprise |
| Flat | boredom, disinterest (avoid this) |

- *Where's the file?* ↘ *(normal question)*
- *Is the file ready?* ↗
- *You sent it?* ↗ *(statement as question — very common 🗣️)*
- *Sure...* ↗ *(hesitation, doubt)*

**Practical warning:** flat intonation can make you sound rude or uninterested in English, even when you're not. Native English uses more pitch variation than Portuguese.

## 18.6 Fillers, hesitation, and thinking time

Everyone hesitates. Using **English fillers** instead of Portuguese ones (*"é..."*, *"tipo"*, *"né"*) keeps you sounding fluent.

| Filler | Function | Register |
|---|---|---|
| **um / uh / er** | basic hesitation | universal |
| **well...** | starting a considered answer | universal |
| **so...** | starting / transitioning | universal |
| **you know** | seeking agreement | 🗣️ (don't overuse) |
| **I mean** | clarifying/rephrasing | 🗣️ |
| **like** 🗣️ | filler (very American, informal) | avoid in professional contexts |
| **actually** | introducing a correction | universal |
| **let me think** | buying time explicitly | universal |
| **that's a good question** | buying time (professional) | ✅ meetings |
| **how can I put this...** | searching for words | universal |
| **sort of / kind of** | hedging while thinking | 🗣️ |
| **right / OK / anyway** | transitioning | universal |
| **basically** | simplifying | 🗣️ |
| **to be honest** | preparing an opinion | 🗣️ |

**Professional time-buying phrases** — memorize these; they make hesitation sound deliberate:

```text
That's a good question — let me think about that for a second.
Let me rephrase that.
How should I put it...
Give me a moment to find the right word.
I'm not sure how to say this in English, but...
Bear with me for a second.
```

## 18.7 Discourse markers — the "traffic signs" of conversation

These signal what's coming next. Native listeners rely on them heavily.

| Marker | Signals |
|---|---|
| **so** | starting a topic, drawing a conclusion |
| **anyway** | returning to the main topic, or ending |
| **right / OK** | transitioning, checking agreement |
| **actually** | correcting or surprising info |
| **by the way** | side topic |
| **speaking of which** | related topic |
| **the thing is** | introducing a problem |
| **look / listen** | emphasis, mild impatience |
| **to be fair** | balancing an argument |
| **at the end of the day** 🗣️ | summarizing (cliché but common) |
| **long story short** | skipping details |
| **in any case / either way** | it doesn't matter which |
| **that said / having said that** | contradicting yourself politely |
| **as I was saying** | resuming after interruption |
| **well** | mild disagreement or hesitation |
| **oh** | new information / surprise |

**Example in context:**
> "**So**, we tested the new version. **Actually**, it was faster than expected. **That said**, there's a memory issue. **Anyway**, I think we should ship it. **By the way**, did you see the invoice?"

## 18.8 Informal expressions worth understanding

| Expression | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **What's up? / 'Sup?** | How are you? / What's happening? |
| **No worries / No problem** | It's fine / You're welcome |
| **Sure thing** | Yes, of course |
| **My bad** | My mistake (informal apology) |
| **Fair enough** | OK, I accept that |
| **Never mind** | Forget it / don't worry about it |
| **You bet** 🇺🇸 | Definitely / you're welcome |
| **I'm good** | No thanks / I'm fine |
| **Hang on / Hold on** | Wait |
| **Go ahead** | Please proceed |
| **Sounds good** | I agree |
| **Got it** | I understand |
| **Makes sense** | That's logical |
| **No big deal** | It's not important |
| **A heads-up** | An advance warning |
| **ASAP** | As soon as possible |
| **FYI** | For your information |
| **Kind of / Sort of** | Somewhat |
| **Pretty much** | Almost entirely |
| **Big time** 🗣️ | A lot |
| **Awesome / Great / Cool** | Very good |
| **That's a bummer** 🗣️ 🇺🇸 | That's disappointing |
| **Let's touch base** | Let's talk briefly later |
| **Circle back** | Return to a topic later |
| **Ping me** | Message me |
| **On my end** | On my side/computer |
| **Off the top of my head** | Without checking |
| **Down the road** | In the future |

> **Advice on slang:** understand it, use very little. Slang dates quickly, varies by region, and using it incorrectly is more noticeable than not using it. Native-sounding English comes from **collocations and rhythm**, not slang.

## 18.9 Spoken grammar that "breaks the rules"

Real speech contains structures that textbooks call wrong. They're normal.

| Spoken form | "Correct" form | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| "You going?" | Are you going? | dropped auxiliary — very common |
| "Got a minute?" | Have you got a minute? | dropped subject + auxiliary |
| "Sounds good." | That sounds good. | dropped subject |
| "Me and John went." | John and I went. | very common, non-standard |
| "There's three people." | There are three people. | extremely common |
| "Who did you talk to?" | To whom did you speak? | the "correct" form is archaic |
| "If I was you..." | If I were you... | *was* is now widely accepted |
| "Everyone brought their laptop." | ...his or her laptop. | *their* is now standard |
| "Can I get a coffee?" 🇺🇸 | May I have a coffee? | normal in the US |
| "Less bugs" | Fewer bugs | common but criticized |
| "Where you at?" 🗣️ 🇺🇸 | Where are you? | very informal |

**What you should do:** recognize all of these. **Produce** the standard forms in writing, and feel free to use the first few in casual speech.

---

# 19. Listening — Why English Is Hard to Understand

## 19.1 The diagnosis

If you read English comfortably but panic on calls, the problem is almost never vocabulary. It's one or more of these:

| Problem | What's happening |
|---|---|
| **The audio doesn't match the spelling** | You learned words visually; speech reduces and links them (Section 18.3) |
| **Wrong rhythm expectations** | You expect equal syllables; English compresses unstressed ones |
| **Processing speed** | You translate mentally; native speech is faster than translation |
| **Vocabulary gaps in the "small" words** | You know *implement*; you miss *"I'da gotten to it"* |
| **Phrasal verbs and idioms** | You know all the words separately but not the combination |
| **Accents** | You trained on one accent (often American, from courses) |
| **Missing context** | Without visual/contextual clues, gaps become fatal |
| **Anxiety loop** | You miss one word, freeze on it, and miss the next ten |

## 19.2 The most important realization

**You do not need to hear every word.** Native listeners don't either — they reconstruct meaning from stressed content words plus context.

```text
What you hear:  "... sen ... report ... clien ... yesterday ... but ... reply"
What you build: "I sent the report to the client yesterday, but there's no reply."
```

Train yourself to grab **content words** and let the small ones go.

## 19.3 The specific things that break comprehension

### Reduced vowels

Almost every unstressed vowel becomes **/ə/** (the "schwa"). This means many syllables lose their identity:
- *photograph* /ˈfoʊtəɡræf/ → *photography* /fəˈtɑːɡrəfi/ — the same letters sound completely different.
- *comfortable* → "**cumf**-ta-bul" (3 syllables, not 4)
- *vegetable* → "**vej**-ta-bul" / *chocolate* → "**choc**-lit" / *interesting* → "**in**-tres-ting"
- *business* → "**biz**-nis" / *Wednesday* → "**Wenz**-day"

### Word linking makes new "words"

*"an ice cream"* and *"a nice cream"* sound identical. Context decides. Other examples: *"That's tough"* / *"That stuff"*; *"I scream"* / *"ice cream"*.

### Words that sound almost identical

| Pair | Difference |
|---|---|
| can / can't 🇺🇸 | stress and vowel length — see 7.9 |
| he / he'll / heal | length |
| lives (verb) / lives (plural of life) | /ɪ/ vs /aɪ/ |
| than / then | usually both /ðən/ in fast speech — context decides |
| walk / work | vowel |
| beach / bitch, sheet / shit, piece / piss | **long vs short /i/ — worth practicing for safety reasons** |
| thirty / thirteen | stress: **THIR**-ty vs thir-**TEEN** |
| desert / dessert | stress |

### The `-s` and `-ed` endings vanish

In fast speech, *"He works"* and *"He work"* can sound similar; *"I finished"* often sounds like *"I finish."* You have to use **context and time words** to know the tense.

### Numbers, dates, and names

These are objectively hard because context doesn't help:
- 13 vs 30, 14 vs 40, 15 vs 50
- Dates: 🇺🇸 *March 5th / 3/5* vs 🇬🇧 *5 March / 5/3* — **a real source of business errors**
- Phone numbers, addresses, unfamiliar names

**Always confirm numbers:** *"Sorry, was that thirteen — one-three — or thirty?"*

### Accents

| Accent | Notable features |
|---|---|
| 🇺🇸 General American | strong /r/, *t* between vowels sounds like /d/ (*water* → "wader"), flat *a* |
| 🇬🇧 British (RP) | no /r/ after vowels (*car* → "cah"), clearer *t*, different vowels (*bath*, *not*) |
| 🇦🇺 Australian | vowel shifts (*day* sounds like "die") |
| 🇮🇳 Indian | syllable-timed rhythm, different stress — **very common in tech** |
| 🇮🇪 Irish / 🏴 Scottish | strong vowel differences |
| Non-native (huge in business) | varied — often *easier* to understand |

> **Practical note for professional English:** most of your English conversations will probably be with other **non-native speakers**. International English is usually slower, more explicit, and uses fewer idioms. Don't optimize only for Hollywood English.

## 19.4 Practical strategies that actually work

### Strategy 1 — Listen to the same audio multiple times

1. First pass: no subtitles. Get the general idea.
2. Second pass: English subtitles. See what you missed.
3. Third pass: no subtitles again. Now you *hear* it.

The third pass is where the learning happens — you're teaching your ear to map sound to meaning.

### Strategy 2 — Use English subtitles, never Portuguese

Portuguese subtitles switch off your listening entirely. English subtitles connect the sound to the spelling.

### Strategy 3 — Shadowing (highest return per minute)

Play 10–20 seconds of audio and repeat it **out loud** at the same speed, copying the rhythm and intonation — not just the words. This trains listening and speaking simultaneously, and it forces you to produce the reductions you struggle to hear.

### Strategy 4 — Dictation on short clips

Take 30 seconds of audio, write down exactly what you hear, then compare with the transcript. The gaps you find are your specific weaknesses (usually weak forms and linking).

### Strategy 5 — Vary the input deliberately

- Podcasts with transcripts (huge advantage)
- Conference talks in your professional field (you already know the context)
- YouTube at 0.75× speed first, then 1×, then 1.25×
- Different accents on purpose

### Strategy 6 — Front-load context

Before a meeting or video, read the agenda, the title, the description, the slides. Context does half the listening work.

### Strategy 7 — Learn to interrupt gracefully

The skill of asking for repetition is more valuable than understanding everything:

```text
Sorry, could you say that again?
Sorry, I didn't catch that.
Could you repeat the last part?
Sorry, could you speak a bit more slowly?
Do you mean ...?
Just to make sure I understood — you're saying that ...?
Sorry, what does "X" mean?
Could you spell that?
Was that thirteen or thirty?
```

**Never pretend to understand.** In professional contexts, misunderstanding a requirement costs far more than one moment of asking.

### Strategy 8 — Accept partial understanding

Set a realistic target: understanding 70% of a fast native conversation is normal for a good non-native speaker. Meaning survives at 70%.

## 19.5 What NOT to do

- ❌ Don't study phonetic symbols exhaustively. Useful in small doses; not a priority.
- ❌ Don't watch content far above your level with Portuguese subtitles and call it practice.
- ❌ Don't stop at the first unknown word — you'll miss the rest of the sentence.
- ❌ Don't only listen passively (background music). Active listening for 15 minutes beats 3 hours of background noise.

---

# 20. Word Order and Natural English

Grammar mistakes make you sound like a learner. **Word-order mistakes make you sound confusing.** This section is about the difference between "understandable" and "natural."

## 20.1 The master template

```text
SUBJECT + VERB + OBJECT + MANNER + PLACE + TIME
```

- *She explained the problem clearly to the team in the meeting yesterday.*
- *I've been working on this remotely from Lisbon all week.*

**Time can move to the front** (common, especially in writing):
- *Yesterday, she explained the problem to the team.*

**Never split the verb and its object:**
- ❌ I like very much this design. → ✅ I like this design **very much**. / I **really** like this design.
- ❌ She speaks fluently English. → ✅ She speaks English **fluently**.
- ❌ We discussed yesterday the budget. → ✅ We discussed the budget **yesterday**.

## 20.2 Adjectives before nouns

```text
❌ a solution simple        ✅ a simple solution
❌ ideas interesting        ✅ interesting ideas
❌ the file more recent     ✅ the most recent file
```

Exceptions where the adjective follows:
- After `be`/linking verbs: *The solution is simple.*
- Fixed expressions: *something new, anything else, nothing special, the person responsible, the best option available, ten years old*
- Some formal/legal titles: *court martial, attorney general, president elect*

## 20.3 Frequency adverbs

```text
before the main verb  /  after "be"  /  after the first auxiliary
```
- ❌ I go always to the gym. → ✅ I **always go** to the gym.
- ❌ He is late always. → ✅ He **is always** late.
- ❌ I never have been there. → ✅ I **have never** been there. *(after the first auxiliary)*

## 20.4 Indirect objects

```text
verb + PERSON + THING           →  I sent Ana the file.
verb + THING + to/for + PERSON  →  I sent the file to Ana.
```

**Use the second form when the thing is a pronoun:**
- ❌ Send me it. → ✅ **Send it to me.**
- ❌ Give her it. → ✅ **Give it to her.**

**Verbs that require `to`:** *explain, describe, say, suggest, mention, recommend, announce, introduce, report*
- ❌ Explain me the process. → ✅ **Explain the process to me.**
- ❌ He suggested me a solution. → ✅ He **suggested a solution to me**. / He **suggested that I...**
- ❌ Can you describe me the problem? → ✅ Can you **describe the problem to me**?

> 🇧🇷 This is a top-5 error, because Portuguese allows *"me explica o processo"*.

## 20.5 Questions and negatives

Covered in Sections 6 and 7. The word-order essentials:
- Question: **auxiliary before subject** — *Where **do you** live?*
- Indirect question: **statement order** — *Do you know where **he is**?*
- Negative: **not after the first auxiliary** — *I **haven't** seen it.*

## 20.6 Multiple adjectives

```text
Opinion → Size → Age → Shape → Color → Origin → Material → Purpose → NOUN
```
- ✅ a great little Italian restaurant
- ❌ an Italian little great restaurant

In practice: **opinion first, then physical facts.**

## 20.7 Time and place expressions

```text
PLACE before TIME
```
- ✅ I'll meet you **at the office at 3**.
- ❌ I'll meet you at 3 at the office. ⚠️ *(understandable, slightly off)*
- ✅ She moved **to Berlin last year**.
- ✅ *(For emphasis, time can lead:)* **Last year**, she moved to Berlin.

**Order within time expressions: smaller unit first.**
- ✅ at 8 a.m. on Monday / on Monday morning
- ✅ at 6 p.m. on 3 March 2025

## 20.8 "Grammatically fine but unnatural" — and how to fix it

| ⚠️ Understandable but unnatural | ✅ Natural |
|---|---|
| I have 30 years. | I'm 30. |
| I am agree with you. | I agree with you. |
| Please, do the favor of sending me the report. | Could you send me the report? |
| I want that you help me. | I want you to help me. |
| It's necessary that you sign it. | You need to sign it. |
| I have doubts about the project. | I have a few questions about the project. |
| We will make a meeting tomorrow. | We're having a meeting tomorrow. |
| I stayed very happy with the result. | I was really happy with the result. |
| Give me a return. | Let me know. / Get back to me. |
| I pretend to finish it today. | I intend to / I plan to finish it today. |
| Any doubts? | Any questions? |
| I will pass you the information. | I'll send you the information. |
| Let's combine a time. | Let's set a time. / Let's schedule something. |
| Realize the meeting | Hold the meeting / Have the meeting |
| I am with a problem. | I have a problem. |
| Explain me this. | Could you explain this to me? |
| We need to align. ⚠️ | Let's get on the same page. / We need to sync. |
| I didn't understand nothing. | I didn't understand anything. |
| For me is difficult. | It's difficult for me. |
| Is possible to change? | Is it possible to change? |
| How is called this? | What's this called? |
| I'm here since Monday. | I've been here since Monday. |
| Take a decision | Make a decision |
| Do a question | Ask a question |
| Assist to the meeting | Attend the meeting |
| I'm accustomed to work late. | I'm used to working late. |

## 20.9 The three word-order errors that matter most

If you fix only three things:

1. **Adjective before noun** — *a red car*, not *a car red*
2. **Object immediately after the verb** — *I like this design very much*, not *I like very much this design*
3. **Auxiliary before subject in questions** — *Where do you work?*, not *Where you work?*

# 21. English Patterns Worth Memorizing

Fluency comes from **prefabricated chunks**, not from assembling grammar in real time. Memorize the pattern; swap the content.

## 21.1 Wants, needs, intentions

```text
I want to + VERB                 I want to talk about the timeline.
I want you to + VERB             I want you to review this before Friday.
I need to + VERB                 I need to check with my manager.
I need + NOUN                    I need more information.
I'd like to + VERB               I'd like to suggest a different approach.  (polite)
I'd like + NOUN                  I'd like a coffee, please.
I'd rather + VERB (base)         I'd rather do it tomorrow.
I'd rather not + VERB            I'd rather not commit to that yet.
I'm trying to + VERB             I'm trying to reproduce the bug.
I'm planning to + VERB           I'm planning to finish by Thursday.
I'm about to + VERB              I'm about to join the call.  (imminent)
I'm supposed to + VERB           I'm supposed to send it today.  (expected of me)
I was going to + VERB            I was going to call you.  (intention, didn't happen)
I'm looking for + NOUN           I'm looking for the deployment logs.
```

> ⚠️ **`I want that you...`** is a direct translation of *"quero que você..."* and is wrong.
> ✅ **I want you to...** / **I'd like you to...** / **I need you to...**

## 21.2 Habits, experience, familiarity

```text
I used to + VERB                 I used to work in finance.  (past, not now)
I'm used to + NOUN/-ING          I'm used to working remotely.  (accustomed)
I'm getting used to + -ING       I'm getting used to the new tool.
I usually + VERB                 I usually start around 9.
I tend to + VERB                 I tend to overthink things.
I'm interested in + NOUN/-ING    I'm interested in learning more about this.
I'm good at + -ING               I'm good at debugging.
I'm not very good at + -ING      I'm not very good at estimating.
I have experience with/in + NOUN I have experience with Kubernetes.
I've never + PAST PARTICIPLE     I've never used that framework.
I've been + -ING                 I've been working on it all week.
```

## 21.3 Opinions and hedging (extremely useful professionally)

```text
I think (that) + clause          I think we should wait.
I don't think + clause           I don't think it'll work.   ← NOT "I think it won't"
I feel like + clause 🗣️          I feel like we're missing something.
In my opinion, + clause ✍️       In my opinion, the risk is too high.
It seems (to me) that + clause   It seems that the cache isn't clearing.
It looks like + clause/NOUN      It looks like a permissions issue.
It sounds like + clause/NOUN     It sounds like a good plan.
From what I can tell, + clause   From what I can tell, it's a config problem.
As far as I know, + clause       As far as I know, nothing's changed.
As far as I'm concerned, + clause  As far as I'm concerned, it's done.
I'd say + clause                 I'd say it'll take two weeks.
I'm not sure if/whether + clause  I'm not sure if that's the right approach.
I have a feeling (that) + clause  I have a feeling this will break.
Correct me if I'm wrong, but...   Correct me if I'm wrong, but isn't that deprecated?
```

> **`I don't think` is important.** English moves the negative to the first verb:
> ```text
> Acho que não vai funcionar.
>         ↓
> ⚠️ I think it won't work.  (understandable but unnatural)
>         ↓
> ✅ I don't think it'll work.
> ```
> Same with: *I don't believe..., I don't suppose..., I don't expect...*

## 21.4 Causes, reasons, explanations

```text
The reason is that + clause          The reason is that the API changed.
The reason why X is that Y           The reason why it failed is that the token expired.
It's because of + NOUN               It's because of the network latency.
That's why + clause                  That's why we switched providers.
This is due to + NOUN ✍️             This is due to a configuration error.
It depends on + NOUN/-ING            It depends on the payload size.
It has to do with + NOUN             It has to do with how we cache requests.
It comes down to + NOUN              It comes down to cost.
The problem is (that) + clause       The problem is we don't have the data.
The thing is, + clause 🗣️            The thing is, nobody tested it.
What happened was (that) + clause    What happened was the disk filled up.
```

> ⚠️ **`It depends on`** — never *"depends of"*, and when standing alone: *"It depends."* (with `it`).

## 21.5 Uncertainty and incomplete knowledge

```text
I'm not sure (if/whether/what/how)   I'm not sure how long it'll take.
I don't know whether + clause        I don't know whether we should refactor it.
I have no idea                       I have no idea why it's slow.
It's hard to say                     It's hard to say without more data.
I'd have to check                    I'd have to check the logs.
Off the top of my head, ...          Off the top of my head, around 200 users.
Don't quote me on this, but... 🗣️     Don't quote me on this, but I think it's Friday.
I might be wrong, but...             I might be wrong, but that looks like a typo.
Roughly / approximately / about      It's about 3 GB.
Something like + NUMBER              Something like 50 requests per second.
```

## 21.6 Requests, offers, suggestions

```text
Could you + VERB?                    Could you take a look at this?
Would you mind + -ING?               Would you mind sending it again?
Do you think you could + VERB?       Do you think you could review it today?
Is there any chance you could...?    Is there any chance you could join at 4?
Can I + VERB?                        Can I ask you something?
Would you like + NOUN / to + VERB?   Would you like to join us?
Do you want me to + VERB?            Do you want me to set that up?
Shall I + VERB? 🇬🇧                   Shall I send the invite?
Let me + VERB                        Let me check and get back to you.
How about + -ING/NOUN?               How about meeting at 3?
What about + NOUN/-ING?              What about the budget?
Why don't we + VERB?                 Why don't we split this into two tasks?
We could always + VERB               We could always postpone it.
It might be worth + -ING             It might be worth testing it first.
Have you considered + -ING?          Have you considered using a queue?
```

> ⚠️ **`How about` and `What about` take a gerund or a noun, never an infinitive:**
> ❌ How about to meet at 3? → ✅ How about **meeting** at 3?

> ⚠️ **`Why don't we/you` is a suggestion, not a real question:** *"Why don't you take a break?"* = "Você deveria descansar."

## 21.7 Agreeing, disagreeing, reacting

```text
That makes sense.                    I see your point, but...
Exactly. / Absolutely.               I'm not sure I agree.
Good point.                          I see it a bit differently.
That's a fair point.                 I'd argue that...
I couldn't agree more.               That's true, but...
Same here. / Me too. / Me neither.   Actually, I think...
Fair enough.                         Hmm, I'm not convinced.
You're right.                        I hear you, but...
```

## 21.8 Talking about time and plans

```text
I'm free on + DAY / at + TIME        I'm free on Thursday afternoon.
Does + TIME work for you?            Does 3 p.m. work for you?
I'm tied up until + TIME             I'm tied up until 4.
Let's aim for + TIME/DATE            Let's aim for Friday.
It should take about + DURATION      It should take about two hours.
By the end of the day/week           I'll have it by the end of the week.
As soon as + clause                  I'll send it as soon as I'm done.
No later than + TIME                 No later than Wednesday.
I'll get back to you by + TIME       I'll get back to you by tomorrow.
```

## 21.9 Describing problems (professional gold)

```text
There's an issue with + NOUN         There's an issue with the login flow.
X isn't working (properly)           The export isn't working properly.
X keeps + -ING                       The app keeps crashing.
I'm having trouble + -ING            I'm having trouble connecting to the VPN.
I can't seem to + VERB               I can't seem to reproduce it.
It fails when + clause               It fails when the file is larger than 10 MB.
It works on my end 🗣️                It works on my end — can you clear your cache?
It used to work                      It used to work before the update.
It looks like X is causing Y         It looks like the migration is causing the timeout.
We're running into + NOUN            We're running into rate limits.
```

## 21.10 Structural patterns worth memorizing

```text
It's + adjective + to + VERB         It's difficult to estimate.
It takes + TIME + to + VERB          It takes ten minutes to run.
There's no point (in) + -ING         There's no point in optimizing this yet.
It's worth + -ING                    It's worth checking the logs.
The more X, the more Y               The more we automate, the fewer errors we get.
Not only ... but also                It's not only faster but also cheaper.
Both ... and / Either ... or         Either we fix it now or we roll back.
The + comparative                    The sooner, the better.
So + adjective + that                It was so slow that users complained.
Such + a + noun + that               It was such a mess that we started over.
Enough + noun / adjective + enough   We don't have enough time. / It's not stable enough.
too + adjective + to + VERB          It's too risky to deploy on Friday.
by + -ING (method)                   We fixed it by increasing the timeout.
instead of + -ING                    Instead of rewriting it, we patched it.
without + -ING                       He left without saying anything.
after / before + -ING                After deploying, check the metrics.
```

## 21.11 Small phrases that make you sound fluent

```text
Just to be clear, ...                Let me know if that works.
That being said, ...                 Feel free to reach out.
On second thought, ...               I'll keep you posted.
To be fair, ...                      Sounds good to me.
Either way, ...                      Works for me.
At the end of the day, ...           Whatever works for you.
In any case, ...                     Let's play it by ear. 🗣️
As a rule, ...                       Better safe than sorry.
More often than not, ...             It's up to you.
For what it's worth, ...             Your call. 🗣️
```

---

# 22. Gerunds and Infinitives

## 22.1 The three basic patterns

```text
1. verb + to + VERB        →  I want to go.
2. verb + VERB-ing         →  I enjoy working here.
3. preposition + VERB-ing  →  I'm interested in learning.
```

Pattern 3 is a **rule with no exceptions**: after any preposition, use `-ing`.

## 22.2 Verbs followed by `to + verb`

*want, need, decide, hope, plan, promise, agree, offer, refuse, learn, manage, seem, try*, *afford, expect, pretend, fail, arrange, choose, deserve, intend, prepare, wait, tend, happen*

- I **decided to leave** early.
- We **agreed to postpone** the release.
- She **managed to fix** it.
- They **refused to pay**.
- It **seems to be** a network issue.

## 22.3 Verbs followed by `-ing`

*enjoy, avoid, finish, keep, mind, suggest, recommend, consider, practice, imagine, miss, involve, risk, deny, admit, quit, postpone, delay, can't help, look forward to, be worth, spend time*

- I **enjoy working** with this team.
- We should **avoid changing** it now.
- Have you **finished testing**?
- I **don't mind waiting**.
- She **suggested using** a cache.
- This **involves updating** three services.
- I **can't help thinking** we missed something.

> ⚠️ **`suggest` and `recommend` never take `to + verb` with a person:**
> ❌ He suggested me to use Redis.
> ✅ He **suggested using** Redis. / He **suggested that I use** Redis. / He **recommended** Redis.

## 22.4 Verb + object + to + verb

```text
verb + PERSON + to + VERB
```
*want, ask, tell, allow, expect, need, advise, encourage, remind, teach, force, invite, persuade, warn, get*

- I **want you to review** this.
- She **asked me to send** the file.
- They **told us to wait**.
- The system **doesn't allow users to delete** records.
- **Remind me to call** the client.

> ⚠️ **The `want that` error again:**
> ❌ I want that you send it. → ✅ I **want you to send** it.
> ❌ I need that he approves it. → ✅ I **need him to approve** it.

**Special group — no `to` after these:** `make`, `let`, `have` (causative), and `help` (optional)
- ✅ Let me **know**. ❌ Let me to know.
- ✅ That makes me **think**. ❌ makes me to think.
- ✅ I had him **look** at it. *(= I arranged for him to)*
- ✅ Help me **understand** / Help me **to understand** *(both fine)*

## 22.5 Preposition + `-ing`

Because `-ing` is the only verb form that can follow a preposition:

```text
interested in learning        good at solving problems
tired of waiting              instead of rewriting
before starting               after finishing
without asking                by automating
look forward to hearing       used to working
capable of handling           responsible for maintaining
think about changing          keen on trying
```

> ⚠️ **The `to` trap.** In *look forward to*, *be used to*, *object to*, *be committed to*, the `to` is a **preposition**, so it takes `-ing`:
> - ✅ I look forward to **hearing** from you. ❌ to hear
> - ✅ I'm used to **working** late. ❌ to work
> - ✅ We're committed to **improving** the process.
>
> **Test:** can you put a noun after it? *"I look forward to **the meeting**"* → yes → so it's a preposition → use `-ing`.

## 22.6 Verbs where the meaning CHANGES

This is the part that matters for communication.

### `remember` / `forget`

```text
remember TO do   → don't forget (the action comes after remembering)
remember DOING   → a memory of the past
```
- **Remember to lock** the door. *(do it!)*
- I **remember locking** the door. *(I have the memory)*
- I **forgot to send** it. *(I didn't send it)*
- I'll never **forget seeing** that. *(the memory stays)*

### `stop`

```text
stop DOING  → quit the activity
stop TO do  → interrupt in order to do something else
```
- He **stopped smoking**. *(quit)*
- He **stopped to smoke**. *(paused what he was doing, to smoke)*

### `try`

```text
try TO do   → make an effort (it's difficult)
try DOING   → experiment with it (as a possible solution)
```
- I **tried to open** the file, but it's corrupted. *(effort failed)*
- **Try restarting** it. *(experiment — this might work)*

### `regret`

```text
regret DOING     → sorry about the past
regret TO say ✍️ → formal, delivering bad news
```
- I **regret telling** him. / We **regret to inform** you that...

### `mean`

```text
mean TO do   → intend
mean DOING   → involve as a consequence
```
- I **meant to call** you. *(I intended to)*
- Fixing this **means rewriting** the module. *(it involves)*

### `go on`

```text
go on DOING  → continue the same thing
go on TO do  → move to the next thing
```
- She **went on talking** for an hour. / She **went on to become** CTO.

### `need`

```text
need TO do            → I must do it
need DOING 🇬🇧         → passive meaning: it requires being done
```
- I **need to fix** this. / The report **needs updating**. *(= needs to be updated)*

### `like / love / hate / prefer` — no real change

Both forms work: *I like **swimming*** = *I like **to swim***.
- Slight nuance: `-ing` = general enjoyment; `to` = a habit/preference/choice.
- **With `would`, always use `to`:** ✅ I'd like **to go**. ❌ I'd like going.

## 22.7 The `-ing` form as a noun (gerund subject)

```text
VERB-ing + verb  →  the -ing form acts as the subject
```
- **Testing** takes time.
- **Working** from home saves two hours a day.
- **Learning** English is easier with context.

> 🇧🇷 Portuguese uses the infinitive here (*"Trabalhar de casa..."*), so learners write:
> ❌ **To work** from home saves time. ⚠️ *(grammatically possible, sounds formal/odd)*
> ✅ **Working** from home saves time.

## 22.8 Common mistakes summary

| ❌ | ✅ |
|---|---|
| I look forward to hear from you. | I look forward to **hearing** from you. |
| I'm used to work late. | I'm used to **working** late. |
| He suggested me to call. | He **suggested calling** / **suggested that I call**. |
| I want that you help me. | I **want you to help** me. |
| Let me to explain. | **Let me explain.** |
| I enjoy to read. | I **enjoy reading**. |
| I'm interested to learn ⚠️ | I'm **interested in learning** *(both exist; "in + -ing" is safer)* |
| Thanks for help me. | Thanks for **helping** me. |
| Instead of to wait | **Instead of waiting** |
| Before to start | **Before starting** / **Before you start** |
| It's worth to try. | It's **worth trying**. |
| I avoid to use it. | I **avoid using** it. |
| I decided going. | I **decided to go**. |

# 23. Conditionals

Forget the numbering system if it confuses you. There are really only **three practical situations**.

## 23.1 The three you need

### A. Real / always true (zero conditional)

```text
If + PRESENT, + PRESENT
```
Facts, rules, automatic results. `if` = `when`.

- If you **press** this button, it **restarts**.
- If the disk **is** full, the service **crashes**.
- If I **don't sleep**, I **can't** focus.
- Water **boils** if you **heat** it to 100°C.

### B. Real future possibility (first conditional)

```text
If + PRESENT, + WILL / CAN / MIGHT / SHOULD + VERB
```
The most useful conditional in daily life.

- If it **rains**, we'**ll cancel** the event.
- If you **finish** early, you **can** leave.
- If the tests **pass**, I'**ll deploy** it.
- If we **don't fix** this today, we **might miss** the deadline.
- **Call** me if you **need** help. *(imperative works too)*

> ⚠️ **Never `will` in the `if` clause.**
> ```text
> Se você precisar, me ligue.  /  Se ele vier, avisa.
>         ↓
> ❌ If you will need... / If he will come...
>         ↓
> ✅ If you need... / If he comes...
> ```
> Portuguese uses the future subjunctive here (*se precisar*, *se vier*), which strongly suggests a future form. English uses the present. This is one of the highest-frequency errors for Portuguese speakers.

### C. Unreal / hypothetical present (second conditional)

```text
If + PAST, + WOULD / COULD / MIGHT + VERB
```
Imaginary situations, advice, and politeness. The past form doesn't mean past time — it signals **unreality**.

- If I **had** more time, I'**d rewrite** it. *(I don't have time)*
- If I **were** you, I'**d wait**. *(classic advice pattern)*
- What **would** you **do** if this **happened** in production?
- If we **hired** two more people, we **could** ship faster.
- It **would be** great if you **could** join.

**Note on `were`:** *If I **were** you* is the traditional form. *If I **was** you* is very common in speech and now widely accepted. Use `were` in writing.

## 23.2 The past one (third conditional) — USEFUL for regret

```text
If + HAD + PAST PARTICIPLE, + WOULD HAVE + PAST PARTICIPLE
```
Something that didn't happen, and its imaginary consequence.

- If we **had tested** it, we **wouldn't have broken** production.
- If I **had known**, I **would have told** you.
- She **would have passed** if she **had studied**.

🗣️ Pronounced: *"If we'd tested it, we wouldn'ta broken production."*

**When you need it:** talking about mistakes, post-mortems, regrets. Common in professional retrospectives.

**Simpler alternatives** that convey the same idea:
- *We **should have** tested it.*
- *The problem was that we didn't test it.*

## 23.3 Mixed conditional — LATER

Past cause → present result:
```text
If + HAD + PP, + WOULD + VERB (now)
```
- If I **had studied** engineering, I **would be** an engineer now.
- If we **hadn't migrated**, we **wouldn't have** this problem today.

Recognize it; you don't need to drill it.

## 23.4 `if` alternatives

| Word | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **unless** | if not | I'll deploy **unless** you object. |
| **as long as** | only if / provided | You can use it **as long as** you cite the source. |
| **provided (that)** ✍️ | formal "as long as" | ...**provided that** all tests pass. |
| **in case** | as a precaution (before) | Bring the charger **in case** the battery dies. |
| **even if** | it doesn't matter | I'll go **even if** it rains. |
| **whether or not** | in both cases | We'll ship **whether or not** it's perfect. |
| **suppose / what if** | hypothesis | **What if** the API goes down? |
| **otherwise** | if not (after the fact) | Save it, **otherwise** you'll lose your work. |

> ⚠️ **`unless` already means "if not"** — don't add another negative.
> ❌ Unless you don't finish... → ✅ **Unless you finish**, we can't ship.
>
> ⚠️ **`in case` ≠ `if`:**
> - *I'll take an umbrella **in case** it rains.* → I take it now, as insurance.
> - *I'll take an umbrella **if** it rains.* → only when it's already raining.

## 23.5 Practical conditional expressions

```text
If necessary, ...                  If possible, ...
If needed, ...                     If not, ...
If so, ...                          If in doubt, ...
If all goes well, ...               If everything works as expected, ...
If that's the case, ...             If it were up to me, ...
Let me know if ...                  Just in case, ...
```

- *I'll be there at 3. **If not**, I'll message you.*
- *Did you get my email? **If so**, please confirm.*
- *We can extend the deadline **if necessary**.*

## 23.6 Common mistakes

| ❌ | ✅ |
|---|---|
| If I will have time, I will call. | **If I have** time, I'll call. |
| If I would know, I would tell you. | **If I knew**, I'd tell you. |
| If he would have called, ... | **If he had called**, ... |
| Unless you don't hurry... | **Unless you hurry**... |
| I would have liked if you told me. | I would have liked it **if you had told** me. |
| In case of you need help | **If you need** help / **In case you need** help |
| If I was you ⚠️ | **If I were you** *(safer in writing)* |

---

# 24. Passive Voice

## 24.1 What it is and why it exists

```text
Active:  Someone + VERB + object     →  The team deployed the fix.
Passive: Object + be + PAST PARTICIPLE (+ by someone) →  The fix was deployed (by the team).
```

The passive moves the **result** to the front and makes the **agent** optional.

**Use it when:**
1. The agent is **unknown**: *My laptop **was stolen**.*
2. The agent is **obvious**: *He **was arrested**.* (by the police)
3. The agent is **unimportant**: *The server **is restarted** every night.*
4. You want to **focus on the process/result**: *The data **is encrypted** at rest.*
5. You want to **avoid blaming** someone: *A mistake **was made**.* ⚠️ *(famously evasive)*
6. **Formal/impersonal register**: *Applications **must be submitted** by Friday.*

## 24.2 The forms

| Tense | Passive structure | Example |
|---|---|---|
| Present Simple | am/is/are + PP | The reports **are generated** daily. |
| Present Continuous | am/is/are being + PP | The system **is being updated**. |
| Past Simple | was/were + PP | The issue **was resolved** yesterday. |
| Past Continuous | was/were being + PP | The site **was being maintained**. |
| Present Perfect | have/has been + PP | The changes **have been approved**. |
| Past Perfect | had been + PP | The file **had been deleted**. |
| Future | will be + PP | You **will be notified**. |
| `going to` | am/is/are going to be + PP | It'**s going to be released** on Monday. |
| Modal | modal + be + PP | It **must be tested** first. |
| Infinitive | to be + PP | It needs **to be reviewed**. |
| Gerund | being + PP | I hate **being interrupted**. |

## 24.3 Where the passive is genuinely dominant

### Technical documentation and software

- The request **is validated** before processing.
- Passwords **are hashed** using bcrypt.
- This method **is deprecated** and will **be removed** in v3.
- The container **is restarted** automatically if it crashes.
- Data **is stored** in an encrypted volume.
- Access **is granted** based on role.

### Business and formal communication

- Your request **has been received**.
- The meeting **has been rescheduled** to Thursday.
- You **will be contacted** within 48 hours.
- The invoice **was sent** last week.
- The decision **was made** by the board.
- Changes **are not permitted** after submission.

### Processes and instructions

- The application **is reviewed** by two engineers.
- Payments **are processed** on the 1st of each month.
- Once approved, the PR **is merged** automatically.

### News and reporting

- Three people **were injured** in the accident.
- The CEO **was appointed** in 2022.
- It **is believed** that the outage **was caused** by a config error.

## 24.4 Useful passive constructions

**Impersonal reporting** (formal, common in academic and news writing):
```text
It is said that ...        It is believed that ...
It is estimated that ...   It is expected that ...
It is known that ...       It has been reported that ...
```
- *It **is estimated that** 40% of users are affected.*

**`get` passive** 🗣️ — informal, focuses on the event:
- I **got promoted**. / She **got fired**. / My car **got stolen**. / It **got deleted** somehow.

**Passive with two objects:**
```text
They gave me a bonus.  →  I was given a bonus.  /  A bonus was given to me.
```
The first version (person first) is more natural.

**"have something done"** — arranging for someone else to do it:
- I **had my laptop repaired**. *(a technician did it)*
- We **had the contract reviewed** by legal.
- I need to **get my visa renewed**.

## 24.5 When NOT to use the passive

⚠️ **Overusing the passive is the #1 problem in non-native professional writing.** It makes text vague, long, and evasive.

| ⚠️ Passive-heavy | ✅ Clearer |
|---|---|
| It was decided by the team that the release would be postponed. | The team **decided to postpone** the release. |
| The report was written by me. | **I wrote** the report. |
| An investigation will be conducted. | **We'll investigate.** |
| It is requested that the form be completed. | **Please complete** the form. |
| The bug was found to be caused by a race condition. | **A race condition caused** the bug. |
| Mistakes were made. | **We made a mistake.** |

**Rule of thumb:** if you know who did it and there's no reason to hide it, use the active voice. It's shorter and stronger.

## 24.6 Common mistakes

| ❌ | ✅ |
|---|---|
| The file was delete. | The file **was deleted**. *(past participle)* |
| It was happened yesterday. | It **happened** yesterday. *(`happen` has no passive)* |
| I was born in 1990 ✅ | correct — always passive |
| The meeting was cancel. | The meeting **was cancelled**. |
| It is need to be fixed. | It **needs to be fixed**. |
| The problem was solved by me yesterday ⚠️ | **I solved** the problem yesterday. |
| He was died. | He **died**. |
| The bug is fixing. | The bug **is being fixed**. |

**Verbs with no passive form** (intransitive): *happen, occur, arrive, come, go, die, sleep, exist, seem, appear, belong, rise*.

---

# 25. Reported Speech

## 25.1 The practical approach

Textbooks make this complicated. In real usage, you need **three verbs** and **one rule**.

### The three verbs

```text
say   + (that) + clause              He said (that) he was busy.
tell  + PERSON + (that) + clause     He told me (that) he was busy.
ask   + (PERSON) + if/WH + clause    She asked if I was available.
```

### The one rule: shift the tense back (usually)

| Direct | Reported |
|---|---|
| "I **am** busy." | He said he **was** busy. |
| "I **work** here." | She said she **worked** there. |
| "I **saw** it." | He said he **had seen** it. |
| "I'**ve finished**." | She said she **had finished**. |
| "I **will** call." | He said he **would** call. |
| "I **can** help." | She said she **could** help. |
| "I **may** be late." | He said he **might** be late. |

**Also shift:** *I → he/she*, *my → his/her*, *here → there*, *this → that*, *now → then*, *today → that day*, *tomorrow → the next day*, *yesterday → the day before*.

## 25.2 The exception that matters most

**Don't shift the tense if the statement is still true, or if it was said recently.** Native speakers do this constantly.

- ✅ She said she **lives** in Porto. *(she still does)*
- ✅ He just told me the server **is** down. *(it still is)*
- ✅ They said the meeting **is** at 3. *(it hasn't happened yet)*

Both forms are correct. In everyday conversation, **not shifting is very common** when the information is current.

## 25.3 Reported questions

```text
Yes/no question  →  ask + if/whether + STATEMENT ORDER
WH question      →  ask + WH-word + STATEMENT ORDER
```

- "Are you free?" → She asked **if I was free**.
- "Where is the file?" → He asked **where the file was**.
- "Did you send it?" → They asked **whether I had sent it**.
- "What time does it start?" → I asked **what time it started**.

> ⚠️ **No question inversion and no `do/does/did` in reported questions.**
> ❌ He asked where **was the file**. → ✅ He asked where **the file was**.
> ❌ She asked if **did I finish**. → ✅ She asked if **I had finished**.

## 25.4 Reported commands and requests

```text
tell / ask + PERSON + to + VERB
```
- "Send the report." → He **told me to send** the report.
- "Please wait." → She **asked me to wait**.
- "Don't touch that." → He **told me not to touch** it.

## 25.5 Useful reporting verbs (better than always using "said")

| Verb | Pattern | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **explain** | explain (to sb) that | He **explained that** the API had changed. |
| **mention** | mention that | She **mentioned that** they're hiring. |
| **suggest** | suggest + -ing / that + clause | He **suggested using** a queue. |
| **agree** | agree to / that | They **agreed to** extend the deadline. |
| **promise** | promise to / that | He **promised to** review it. |
| **admit** | admit + -ing / that | She **admitted** making a mistake. |
| **complain** | complain that/about | Users **complained that** it was slow. |
| **warn** | warn sb (not) to | He **warned us not to** deploy on Friday. |
| **confirm** | confirm that | They **confirmed that** the payment went through. |
| **point out** | point out that | She **pointed out that** we're over budget. |
| **claim** | claim that | The vendor **claims that** it scales to 1M users. |
| **add** | add that | He **added that** the budget was fixed. |

## 25.6 Practical workplace reporting

```text
According to + PERSON/SOURCE          According to Ana, the deploy is postponed.
X says (that) ...                     Marketing says the launch is on hold.
I heard (that) ...                    I heard they're restructuring.
Apparently, ...                       Apparently, the client cancelled.
Word is ... 🗣️                        Word is they're hiring.
As mentioned in the email, ...        As mentioned in the email, we need approval.
Just to relay what X said: ...        Just to relay what Ana said: the API is frozen.
```

## 25.7 Common mistakes

| ❌ | ✅ |
|---|---|
| He said me that... | He **told me** that... / He **said** that... |
| She told that she was late. | She **told me** that... / She **said** that... |
| He asked me what time is it. | He asked me **what time it was**. |
| She said me to wait. | She **told me to wait**. / She **asked me to wait**. |
| He said "yes" that he would come. | He **said** he would come. |
| They asked if did I need help. | They asked **if I needed help**. |

---

# 26. Common Mistakes Made by Portuguese Speakers

Organized by **severity**: mistakes that change meaning come first.

## 26.1 Tier 1 — Mistakes that cause real misunderstanding

### 1. `very` vs `too`

```text
Esse notebook é muito caro.
        ↓
❌ This laptop is too expensive.     → "so we can't buy it"
        ↓
✅ This laptop is very expensive.    → "it costs a lot"
```

### 2. `must not` vs `don't have to`

```text
Você não precisa vir.
        ↓
❌ You mustn't come.       → "it's forbidden"
        ↓
✅ You don't have to come.  → "it's optional"
```

### 3. `by` vs `until`

```text
Preciso disso até sexta.
        ↓
❌ I need it until Friday.  → "I need to keep it up to Friday"
        ↓
✅ I need it by Friday.     → "the deadline is Friday"
```

### 4. `hard` vs `hardly`

```text
Ele trabalha muito.
        ↓
❌ He hardly works.   → "he almost never works"
        ↓
✅ He works hard.
```

### 5. `borrow` vs `lend`

```text
Me empresta seu carregador?
        ↓
❌ Can you borrow me your charger?
        ↓
✅ Can you lend me your charger? / Can I borrow your charger?
```

### 6. `remember` vs `remind`

```text
Me lembra de ligar pro cliente.
        ↓
❌ Remember me to call the client.
        ↓
✅ Remind me to call the client.
```

### 7. Negative question answers

```text
"Você não enviou?"  — "Não, eu enviei."
        ↓
❌ "Didn't you send it?" — "No, I sent it."   → confusing/contradictory
        ↓
✅ "Yes, I did — I sent it this morning."
```

### 8. `push` vs `pull`

```text
Puxe a porta.
        ↓
❌ Push the door.   → the opposite action
        ↓
✅ Pull the door.
```
*(`push` = empurrar, `pull` = puxar. See [Section 27](#27-false-friends--false-cognates).)*

### 9. Dates 🇺🇸 vs 🇬🇧

```text
03/04/2025
🇺🇸 = March 4       🇬🇧 = 3 April
```
**Always write the month in letters in international communication:** *4 March 2025* or *March 4, 2025*.

### 10. `actually`

```text
Atualmente eu trabalho remoto.
        ↓
❌ Actually I work remotely.   → "in fact" (sounds like a correction)
        ↓
✅ Currently I work remotely. / These days I work remotely.
```

## 26.2 Tier 2 — Mistakes that sound clearly foreign

### Word order

| Portuguese idea | ❌ Literal | ✅ Natural |
|---|---|---|
| um carro vermelho | a car red | a red car |
| Eu falo bem inglês. | I speak well English. | I speak English well. |
| Eu gosto muito disso. | I like very much this. | I really like this. / I like this a lot. |
| Onde você trabalha? | Where you work? | Where do you work? |
| Eu sempre chego cedo. | I arrive always early. | I always arrive early. |
| Me explica isso. | Explain me this. | Explain this to me. / Could you explain this? |
| Para mim é difícil. | For me is difficult. | It's difficult for me. |
| É possível mudar? | Is possible to change? | Is it possible to change? |
| duas reuniões importantes | two importants meetings | two important meetings |

### Articles

| Portuguese | ❌ | ✅ |
|---|---|---|
| Eu gosto de música. | I like the music. | I like music. |
| O Brasil é grande. | The Brazil is big. | Brazil is big. |
| A vida é curta. | The life is short. | Life is short. |
| Ela é médica. | She is doctor. | She is a doctor. |
| Vou para o trabalho. | I go to the work. | I'm going to work. |
| Vou para casa. | I go to home. | I'm going home. |
| Eu odeio reuniões. | I hate the meetings. | I hate meetings. |
| Ele quebrou o braço. | He broke the arm. | He broke his arm. |

### Prepositions

| Portuguese | ❌ | ✅ |
|---|---|---|
| Depende de você. | It depends of you. | It depends on you. |
| Vamos discutir sobre isso. | Let's discuss about it. | Let's discuss it. / Let's talk about it. |
| Cheguei no escritório. | I arrived to the office. | I arrived at the office. / I got to the office. |
| Casou com ela. | He married with her. | He married her. / He got married to her. |
| Na TV | in the TV | on TV |
| Estou pensando em mudar. | I'm thinking in changing. | I'm thinking about changing. |
| Ouvir música | listen music | listen to music |
| Esperar o ônibus | wait the bus | wait for the bus |
| Responsável por isso | responsible of that | responsible for that |
| Interessado em... | interested on... | interested in... |
| Sonhei com você. | I dreamed with you. | I dreamed about you. |
| Bom em matemática | good in math | good at math |
| Entrar na sala | enter in the room | enter the room / go into the room |
| Ligar para ele | call to him | call him |
| Participar de | participate of | participate in / take part in |
| Focar em | focus in | focus on |

### Verb tenses

| Portuguese | ❌ | ✅ |
|---|---|---|
| Trabalho aqui há 5 anos. | I work here since 5 years. | I've worked here for 5 years. |
| Moro aqui desde 2019. | I live here since 2019. | I've lived here since 2019. |
| Quando eu chegar, te ligo. | When I will arrive, I'll call. | When I arrive, I'll call you. |
| Se você precisar... | If you will need... | If you need... |
| Ontem eu terminei. | Yesterday I have finished. | Yesterday I finished. |
| Você foi? | Did you went? | Did you go? |
| Ele não trabalha aqui. | He doesn't works here. | He doesn't work here. |
| Ele trabalha aqui. | He work here. | He works here. |
| Estou precisando de ajuda. | I'm needing help. | I need help. |
| Estou achando que... | I'm thinking that... | I think... |
| Estou com fome. | I'm with hunger. | I'm hungry. |
| Tenho 35 anos. | I have 35 years. | I'm 35. |
| Tem um problema. | Have a problem. | There's a problem. |
| Faz muito tempo. | Makes a long time. | It's been a long time. |
| Nasci em 1990. | I borned in 1990. | I was born in 1990. |

### `do` / `make` / `have` / `take`

| Portuguese | ❌ | ✅ |
|---|---|---|
| fazer uma pergunta | make a question | ask a question |
| fazer uma reunião | do a meeting | have a meeting |
| fazer sentido | do sense | make sense |
| fazer um curso | make a course | take a course |
| fazer uma prova | make a test | take a test |
| fazer parte de | make part of | be part of |
| tomar uma decisão | take a decision ⚠️ | make a decision |
| tirar uma foto | take a photo ✅ | take a photo |
| dar uma olhada | give a look | take a look / have a look |
| ter razão | have reason | be right |
| ter medo | have fear | be afraid / be scared |
| ter pressa | have hurry | be in a hurry |
| ter sorte | have luck ⚠️ | be lucky |
| fazer aniversário | make birthday | It's my birthday / I'm turning 30 |

### Pronouns

| Portuguese | ❌ | ✅ |
|---|---|---|
| Está chovendo. | Is raining. | It's raining. |
| Comprei um laptop. Ele é rápido. | It's a laptop. **He** is fast. | **It**'s fast. |
| Eu me chamo Ana. | I call myself Ana. | My name is Ana. / I'm Ana. |
| Nos vemos amanhã. | We see ourselves tomorrow. | See you tomorrow. |
| Eu e ele fomos. | Me and him went. ⚠️ | He and I went. |
| Um amigo meu | a friend of me | a friend of mine |
| Esse é meu. | This is my. | This is mine. |
| O meu carro | the my car | my car |

### Plurals and countability

| ❌ | ✅ |
|---|---|
| informations | information |
| advices | advice |
| The news are... | The news is... |
| many works | a lot of work |
| two peoples | two people |
| furnitures | furniture |
| feedbacks | feedback |
| How much people? | How many people? |
| I have many money. | I have a lot of money. |
| persons ⚠️ | people |

### Adjectives

| ❌ | ✅ |
|---|---|
| I'm boring. | I'm bored. |
| The class was very interested. | The class was very interesting. |
| I'm very interesting in this. | I'm very interested in this. |
| two differents options | two different options |
| more better | better |
| I'm agree. | I agree. |
| Is very good the food. | The food is very good. |

### Literal translations of Portuguese expressions

| Portuguese expression | ❌ Literal | ✅ Natural English |
|---|---|---|
| Nossa! | Our! | Wow! / Oh my God! |
| Pois é. | Then is. | Yeah / Right / Exactly. |
| Tudo bem? | All good? | How's it going? / You OK? |
| Beleza! | Beauty! | Cool / Great / Sounds good. |
| Me dá um retorno. | Give me a return. | Let me know. / Get back to me. |
| Vamos combinar. | Let's combine. | Let's arrange something. / Let's set a time. |
| Fica à vontade. | Stay at will. | Make yourself at home. / Feel free. |
| Deu certo. | Gave right. | It worked (out). |
| Não deu. | Didn't give. | It didn't work out. / I couldn't. |
| Tô ligado. | I'm connected. | I get it. / I know. |
| Que pena! | What pity! | That's too bad. / What a shame. |
| Com certeza. | With certainty. | Definitely. / For sure. |
| Puxa vida! | Pull life! | Oh man! / Wow. |
| Estou de boa. | I'm of good. | I'm good. / I'm fine. |
| Tanto faz. | So much makes. | Whatever. / It doesn't matter. |
| Tô fora. | I'm outside. | I'm out. / Count me out. |
| Já era. | Already was. | It's gone. / Too late. |
| Vai dar certo. | Will give right. | It'll work out. |
| Não faz mal. | Doesn't make bad. | It's OK. / No worries. |
| Que horror! | What horror! | That's awful! |
| Bom proveito. | Good use. | Enjoy your meal. / Enjoy! |
| Como assim? | How like this? | What do you mean? |
| Sei lá. | Know there. | I don't know. / No idea. |
| Bater um papo | hit a chat | have a chat |
| Marcar uma reunião | mark a meeting | schedule/set up a meeting |
| Estou por fora. | I'm outside. | I'm out of the loop. |
| Passar um e-mail | pass an email | send an email |
| Pegar no pé | take on the foot | give someone a hard time |
| Quebrar um galho | break a branch | help out / do a favor |

## 26.3 Tier 3 — Pronunciation errors worth fixing

These are the ones that most affect intelligibility:

| Issue | Problem | Fix |
|---|---|---|
| **Final `-e`** | *"like-e"*, *"night-e"* | English words end abruptly. No extra vowel. |
| **`-ed` endings** | *"work-ed"* (3 syllables) | Only after t/d is it a syllable: *wanted* ✅ *worked* = "workt" |
| **`th`** | *think* → "sink"/"fink" | Tongue between the teeth. /θ/ *think*, /ð/ *this* |
| **`h`** | dropping it: *"I ave"* | English pronounces initial /h/: **h**ave, **h**ouse, **h**ome |
