| **`r` initial** | *right* → "hight" | English /r/ is not the Portuguese *rr* |
| **short vs long /i/** | *sheet/shit*, *beach/bitch*, *live/leave* | Length + tension matter — and can be embarrassing |
| **`-tion`** | *"informa-see-on"* | = **/ʃən/** — "informa-shun" |
| **word stress** | *ho-TEL* ✅ but *DE-velop-ment* ❌ | Stress the right syllable: de-**VEL**-op-ment, **COM**-fort-a-ble |
| **initial `s` + consonant** | *"e-school"*, *"e-Spain"* | No vowel before: **school**, **Spain**, **stress** |
| **`v` vs `b`** | *very/berry* | English /v/ uses the teeth on the lip |
| **`-age`** | *manage* → "man-ah-ge" | = /ɪdʒ/ — "MAN-ij", "MESS-ij", "PACK-ij" |

> **Priority:** word stress and the `-e`/`-ed` endings do more damage to comprehension than the `th` sound. Nobody misunderstands "sink" for "think" in context, but wrong stress on a key word ruins the sentence.

## 26.4 The mistakes that DON'T matter much

Don't waste energy on these:
- `a/an/the` in edge cases
- `at the weekend` vs `on the weekend`
- `different from/than/to`
- Perfect `-th` pronunciation
- `who` vs `whom`
- `less` vs `fewer` in speech
- American vs British spelling (just be consistent)
- Slight accent

**Fluency and clarity beat accuracy.** A speaker who talks confidently with small errors communicates better than one who pauses to conjugate perfectly.

---

# 27. False Friends / False Cognates

## 27.1 The critical ten

These appear constantly and cause real confusion.

| English word | Actually means | PT speakers think it means | Correct English for the PT meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| **actually** | na verdade, de fato | atualmente | **currently, nowadays, these days** |
| **eventually** | no final, com o tempo | eventualmente (às vezes) | **occasionally, possibly, sometimes** |
| **pretend** | fingir | pretender | **intend, plan to** |
| **realize** | perceber, dar-se conta | realizar (fazer) | **carry out, hold, perform, accomplish** |
| **parents** | pais (mãe e pai) | parentes | **relatives** |
| **push / pull** | empurrar / puxar | (invertidos) | — |
| **assist** | ajudar | assistir (ver) | **watch, attend** |
| **library** | biblioteca | livraria | **bookstore, bookshop** |
| **college** | faculdade/universidade 🇺🇸 | colégio (ensino médio) | **high school** |
| **fabric** | tecido | fábrica | **factory, plant** |

**Examples:**

- ❌ *I pretend to finish it today.* → ✅ *I **intend/plan** to finish it today.*
- ❌ *We realized a meeting.* → ✅ *We **held** a meeting.*
- ❌ *I assisted the presentation.* → ✅ *I **attended** the presentation.*
- ❌ *My parents live in Recife.* ⚠️ means "my mother and father" — if you mean cousins/uncles, say ***relatives***.
- ❌ *I bought it at the library.* → ✅ *at the **bookstore***.

## 27.2 The full working list

| English | Real meaning | Looks like (PT) | For the PT meaning, say |
|---|---|---|---|
| **actual / actually** | real / in fact | atual / atualmente | current / currently |
| **agenda** | pauta, programação | agenda (caderno) | diary, planner, calendar |
| **anticipate** | prever, esperar | antecipar (adiantar) | bring forward, move up |
| **application** | candidatura, pedido; app | aplicação (uso) | use, deployment |
| **apply** | candidatar-se, aplicar-se | aplicar (investir) | invest |
| **appointment** | consulta, compromisso marcado | apontamento | note |
| **argument** | discussão, briga; argumento | argumento (razão) | point, reasoning ✅ *(both exist)* |
| **assist** | ajudar | assistir | watch, attend |
| **attend** | comparecer a | atender | answer (the phone), help (a customer) |
| **balcony** | varanda, sacada | balcão | counter |
| **cafeteria** | refeitório | cafeteria (café) | coffee shop, café |
| **casualty** | vítima | casualidade | coincidence |
| **college** | faculdade 🇺🇸 | colégio | high school |
| **collar** | gola, coleira | colar (joia) | necklace |
| **comprehensive** | abrangente, completo | compreensivo | understanding |
| **compromise** | acordo, concessão | compromisso | commitment, appointment |
| **confident** | confiante | confidente | confidant |
| **convenient** | prático, conveniente (coisas) | conveniente (pessoa) | — |
| **costume** | fantasia | costume (hábito) | habit, custom |
| **data** | dados | data (calendário) | date |
| **deception** | engano, fraude | decepção | disappointment |
| **defendant** | réu | defensor | defender, advocate |
| **discussion** | conversa, debate | discussão (briga) | argument, fight |
| **editor** | redator-chefe | editor (publisher) | publisher |
| **educated** | instruído | educado (polido) | polite, well-mannered |
| **enroll** | matricular-se | enrolar | wrap; stall 🗣️ |
| **eventually** | no final | eventualmente | occasionally, possibly |
| **exquisite** | requintado | esquisito | strange, weird, odd |
| **fabric** | tecido | fábrica | factory |
| **fastidious** | meticuloso | fastidioso (chato) | tedious, annoying |
| **grip** | agarrar, aderência | gripe | flu |
| **idiom** | expressão idiomática | idioma | language |
| **ingenuity** | engenhosidade | ingenuidade | naivety |
| **injury** | lesão, ferimento | injúria | insult |
| **intend** | pretender | entender | understand |
| **large** | grande | largo | wide, broad |
| **lecture** | palestra, aula | leitura | reading |
| **legend** | lenda; legenda de mapa | legenda (filme) | subtitles |
| **library** | biblioteca | livraria | bookstore |
| **lunch** | almoço | lanche | snack |
| **mayor** | prefeito | maior | bigger, older |
| **notice** | perceber; aviso | notícia | news |
| **novel** | romance (livro) | novela | soap opera |
| **office** | escritório | ofício | trade, craft; official letter |
| **parents** | pais | parentes | relatives |
| **particular** | específico; exigente | particular (privado) | private |
| **pasta** | massa (comida) | pasta (arquivo/folder) | folder; briefcase |
| **policy** | política (diretriz) | polícia | police |
| **prejudice** | preconceito | prejuízo | loss, damage |
| **preservative** | conservante | preservativo | condom |
| **pretend** | fingir | pretender | intend, plan |
| **procure** | adquirir, obter | procurar | look for, search |
| **pull / push** | puxar / empurrar | (invertidos) | — |
| **realize** | perceber | realizar | carry out, hold, achieve |
| **record** | registro; gravar | recordar | remember, recall |
| **resume** | retomar, continuar | resumir | summarize |
| **résumé** 🇺🇸 | currículo | resumo | summary; (CV 🇬🇧) |
| **retire** | aposentar-se | retirar | withdraw, remove |
| **scholar** | acadêmico, estudioso | escolar | school (adj.) |
| **sensible** | sensato | sensível | sensitive |
| **sensitive** | sensível, delicado | — | ✅ |
| **support** | apoiar, sustentar | suportar (aguentar) | put up with, tolerate, stand |
| **sympathetic** | solidário, compreensivo | simpático | nice, friendly, pleasant |
| **tax** | imposto | taxa | fee, rate |
| **terrific** | ótimo, excelente | terrível | terrible, awful |
| **vegetables** | legumes/verduras | vegetais (plantas) | plants |

## 27.3 The ones that cause the worst accidents

| Word | Why it matters |
|---|---|
| **preservative** | *"Does it contain preservatives?"* is about food. Do not use it for *preservativo*. |
| **exquisite** | *"Your idea is exquisite"* is a compliment (refined), not "esquisita." |
| **terrific** | *"That's terrific!"* = excellent. *terrible* = the bad one. |
| **support** | *"I can't support this noise"* → say *"I can't **stand/put up with** this noise."* |
| **push/pull** | Doors, and in Git: *push* = enviar, *pull* = puxar/baixar. |
| **discussion** | *"We had a discussion"* is neutral in English. Say *argument* for a fight. |
| **sympathetic** | *"He's very sympathetic"* = he's compassionate, not "he's nice." Use **nice/friendly**. |
| **educated** | *"He's not educated"* = he lacks schooling, not "he's rude." Use **rude/impolite**. |
| **compromise** | In business, *"we reached a compromise"* = both sides conceded. For *compromisso*, say **commitment**. |

## 27.4 True cognates you can trust

Not everything is a trap. These transfer directly and are worth using confidently:

*important/importante, possible/possível, necessary/necessário, different/diferente, problem/problema, project/projeto, system/sistema, information/informação (⚠️ uncountable), decision/decisão, experience/experiência, professional/profissional, technology/tecnologia, communication/comunicação, responsible/responsável, opportunity/oportunidade, development/desenvolvimento, environment/ambiente, document/documento, process/processo, complete/completo, final/final, natural/natural, general/geral, special/especial, personal/pessoal, social/social, total/total, normal/normal*

**Useful heuristic:** Latin-origin words usually transfer well in **formal/technical** registers. The traps concentrate in **everyday** vocabulary.

---

# 28. English for Being Understood

## 28.1 The core insight

**Perfect grammar is not the goal. Being understood is.**

Most learners spend their energy on the wrong things. Here's the actual hierarchy of what affects comprehension:

```text
1. Word order                    ████████████  huge
2. Correct main verb & vocabulary ███████████   huge
3. Sentence stress & rhythm       █████████     large
4. Clear subject                  ████████      large
5. Correct tense (when time matters) ██████     medium
6. Key prepositions               █████         medium
7. Pronunciation of key words     █████         medium
8. Articles                       ██            small
9. Perfect verb agreement (-s)    ██            small
10. Idiomatic phrasal verbs       █             small
```

## 28.2 Mistakes that are usually harmless

These sound non-native but **do not stop communication**:

| Mistake | Why it's OK |
|---|---|
| *He work here.* | Meaning is 100% clear |
| *I like the music.* | Article error, meaning intact |
| *I'm here since Monday.* | Time is clear from *since* |
| *at the weekend / on the weekend* | Both understood |
| *I have 30 years.* | Odd, but understood in context |
| *Two informations* | Clear, just wrong |
| *Different than* | Universally understood |
| Slight accent | Almost never a problem |
| Missing contractions | Just sounds formal |

## 28.3 Mistakes that DO change meaning

Fix these first:

| Mistake | Risk |
|---|---|
| *too* instead of *very* | Reverses the message |
| *mustn't* instead of *don't have to* | Reverses obligation |
| *until* instead of *by* | Wrong deadline |
| *hardly* instead of *hard* | Opposite meaning |
| *borrow/lend* swapped | Who gives what to whom |
| *bring/take* swapped | Direction confusion |
| *remind/remember* swapped | Who does the remembering |
| *he/she* for objects | Confusing (but recoverable) |
| Wrong number (13/30) | Business errors |
| Wrong date format | Missed deadlines |
| Double negative | Reverses meaning |
| Wrong word order in questions | Slows comprehension |
| False friends (*pretend, realize, actually*) | Wrong meaning entirely |

## 28.4 Strategies for maximum clarity

### 1. Short sentences

```text
⚠️ I would like to inform you that regarding the issue that we discussed in our meeting
   last week, which was related to the performance of the system, we have identified
   that the problem is being caused by a query that is not optimized.

✅ I want to update you on the performance issue from last week's meeting.
   We found the cause: an unoptimized query.
```

Short sentences are not "simple English." They are **good English**. Native professional writers use them.

### 2. Put the main point first

English readers expect the conclusion at the beginning, not at the end (the opposite of some Portuguese writing traditions).

```text
⚠️ After analyzing the logs, checking the metrics, and running several tests,
   we concluded that we should postpone the release.

✅ We should postpone the release. Here's why: [logs, metrics, tests].
```

### 3. Use the vocabulary you're sure about

Don't reach for an impressive word you might use incorrectly.
- ⚠️ *"We need to ameliorate the situation."*
- ✅ *"We need to improve this."*

### 4. Signal your structure

```text
There are three issues. First, ... Second, ... Finally, ...
Let me explain the problem, then the options.
Two things: one, ...; two, ...
```

### 5. Confirm and repeat critical information

```text
So just to confirm: Friday at 3 p.m., your office. Correct?
That's thirteen — one, three — not thirty.
Let me repeat that back to you: you want X, not Y.
```

### 6. Avoid ambiguity traps

| Ambiguous | Clear |
|---|---|
| Next Friday ⚠️ *(this week or next?)* | Friday the 14th |
| It's due tomorrow morning ⚠️ | It's due by 10 a.m. tomorrow |
| We'll ship soon | We'll ship by end of month |
| It's almost done | About 80% done — two days left |
| I'll do it later | I'll do it this afternoon |
| Let me know | Please reply by Wednesday |
| A couple of days ⚠️ | Two days |
| ASAP ⚠️ *(everyone interprets it differently)* | By 5 p.m. today |

> **`Next Friday` genuinely confuses native speakers too.** Always add the date.

### 7. Avoid unnecessary complexity

You don't need the Third Conditional or the Future Perfect in a meeting. Use simple structures with correct vocabulary.
- ⚠️ *"Had we been informed earlier, we would have been able to prevent it."*
- ✅ *"If you'd told us earlier, we could have prevented it."*
- ✅ *"We didn't know, so we couldn't prevent it."*

### 8. Speak with rhythm, not speed

Fast, flat English is harder to understand than slow, well-stressed English. Stress the content words. Pause at commas.

```text
We NEED to POSTpone the RElease  |  beCAUSE the TESTS are FAILing.
```

### 9. When you can't find a word — describe it

Don't freeze. Native speakers do this too.
```text
It's like a ... but for ...
I don't know the word — it's the thing you use to ...
What do you call it when ...?
The word escapes me, but it means ...
```

### 10. Check understanding proactively

```text
Does that make sense?
Am I being clear?
Let me know if I should explain that differently.
Sorry — is my English clear enough?
```

## 28.5 The pragmatic politeness problem

**This is more important than grammar in professional English.** Direct translations from Portuguese can sound rude even when they're grammatically perfect.

| Grammatically correct but ⚠️ rude | ✅ Natural professional English |
|---|---|
| Send me the report. | Could you send me the report when you get a chance? |
| I need this today. | I'd need this today if possible — is that doable? |
| You are wrong. | I think there might be a misunderstanding. |
| I don't agree. | I see it a bit differently. |
| That's not possible. | That might be difficult. Let me check what we can do. |
| Answer me. | Just following up on my message below. |
| Why didn't you do it? | Is there anything blocking this? |
| No. | I'm afraid that won't work for us. |
| Your code has bugs. | I found a couple of issues — mind taking a look? |
| Do it again. | Could you take another pass at this? |

**The mechanism:** English softens through **modals** (*could, would, might*), **hedges** (*a bit, maybe, I think, kind of*), and **questions** instead of statements. Portuguese softens through tone and diminutives, which don't survive translation.

> ⚠️ **The opposite risk:** over-softening. *"I was wondering if it might perhaps be possible for you to possibly consider..."* is worse than being direct. One softener is enough.

---

# 29. Writing Clearly in English

## 29.1 The core rules

### 1. One idea per sentence

```text
⚠️ We tested the new API and it worked well but there were some issues with the
   authentication which we think are related to the token expiration that was
   changed last week by the security team.

✅ We tested the new API and it worked well. However, we found authentication
   issues. They seem related to the token expiration change from last week.
```

**Target:** 15–20 words per sentence on average. Vary the length.

### 2. Paragraph structure

```text
Topic sentence (the main point)
  ↓
Supporting details
  ↓
Consequence or conclusion
```

**One idea per paragraph.** In business writing, 2–4 sentences per paragraph. Break up walls of text.

### 3. Front-load the important information

```text
⚠️ I hope this email finds you well. As you may know, we have been working on the
   migration project for several months now, and after many discussions with the
   team... [3 paragraphs] ...so we need to postpone the launch.

✅ We need to postpone the launch by two weeks.
   [Then explain why.]
```

### 4. Prefer active voice

- ⚠️ *The decision was made to cancel the project.*
- ✅ *We decided to cancel the project.*

### 5. Cut unnecessary words

| ⚠️ Wordy | ✅ Concise |
|---|---|
| due to the fact that | because |
| in order to | to |
| at this point in time | now |
| in the event that | if |
| has the ability to | can |
| make a decision | decide *(when brevity matters)* |
| it is necessary that you | you need to |
| I would like to inform you that | *(just say it)* |
| please be advised that | *(just say it)* |
| in my personal opinion | I think |
| completely eliminate | eliminate |
| absolutely essential | essential |
| each and every | every |
| the reason is because | the reason is / because |
| for the purpose of | for / to |
| a large number of | many |
| at the present time | now |
| in the near future | soon |

### 6. Don't translate Portuguese sentence rhythm

Portuguese business writing favors long, formal, elaborate sentences. English favors short and direct. Translating your Portuguese style produces text that reads as heavy and unclear.

| ⚠️ Translated Portuguese style | ✅ English style |
|---|---|
| Venho por meio deste solicitar... | I'm writing to ask... / Could you...? |
| Conforme conversado anteriormente... | As discussed, ... |
| Aguardo seu retorno. | Looking forward to hearing from you. / Let me know. |
| Desde já agradeço. | Thanks in advance. |
| Segue em anexo. | Here's the file. / Please find attached. ✍️ |
| Estou à disposição para eventuais dúvidas. | Let me know if you have any questions. |
| Sem mais para o momento. | *(omit entirely)* |
| Prezado Senhor, | Dear Mr. Silva, / Hi John, |
| Atenciosamente, | Best regards, / Best, |

## 29.2 Punctuation that matters

### Commas — the four rules you need

**1. Before a coordinating conjunction joining two full sentences:**
- *We tested it**,** but it failed.*
- *(Not needed for short ones: "I tried and failed.")*

**2. After an introductory element:**
- *After the meeting**,** we updated the docs.*
- *However**,** the results were unclear.*
- *If you agree**,** I'll send it today.*

**3. Around non-essential information:**
- *Ana**,** who joined last month**,** is leading the project.*
- *The report**,** which I sent yesterday**,** has an error.*

**4. In lists:**
- *We need design**,** development**,** and testing.*
- 🇺🇸 The final comma (Oxford comma) is standard in American English; optional in British. **Use it — it prevents ambiguity.**

**Comma errors to avoid:**
- ❌ **Comma splice:** *It's late, let's continue tomorrow.* → ✅ Use a period, semicolon, or *so*: *It's late**.** Let's continue tomorrow.*
- ❌ *However, it's expensive, however, it's worth it.* → see [Section 14](#14-the-most-important-english-connectors).
- ❌ Comma between subject and verb: *The report I sent yesterday**,** has an error.*
- ⚠️ In English, **`that` clauses take no comma**: *I think **that** it's fine.* (Portuguese often uses one.)

### Apostrophes

```text
Possession:      the client's request  /  the clients' requests (plural)
Contraction:     it's = it is  /  don't = do not
NEVER for plurals: ❌ PDF's, API's, the 90's  →  ✅ PDFs, APIs, the 90s
```

**The `its`/`it's` rule again:** if you can say "it is," use `it's`. Otherwise `its`.

### Other punctuation

| Mark | Use |
|---|---|
| **Period (.)** | End of a statement. Use more of them. |
| **Semicolon (;)** | Joins two related sentences ✍️. *It failed; we don't know why.* Use sparingly. |
| **Colon (:)** | Introduces a list or explanation. *We need three things: X, Y, and Z.* |
| **Dash (—)** | Adds emphasis or an aside 🗣️✍️. *It works — mostly.* Very useful in modern writing. |
| **Parentheses ( )** | Extra info of lower importance. |
| **Exclamation (!)** | Use rarely in professional writing. One per email at most. |
| **Quotation marks** | 🇺🇸 periods/commas go inside: *"like this."* 🇬🇧 usually outside. |

### Capitalization

```text
ALWAYS capitalize:
- The first word of a sentence
- I (always, everywhere)
- Names: Ana, Google, Lisbon
- Countries, nationalities, languages: Brazil, Brazilian, Portuguese
- Days and months: Monday, March
- Titles before names: Dr. Silva, President Lula
- Acronyms: API, HTTP, CEO

DON'T capitalize:
- Seasons: summer, winter
- General job titles: "she's a manager"
- Subjects (except languages): math, biology, English
- After a colon (usually)
- Random words for emphasis
```

> 🇧🇷 **Portuguese doesn't capitalize languages, nationalities, months, or days. English does.**
> ❌ I speak portuguese and english. → ✅ I speak **Portuguese** and **English**.
> ❌ See you on monday in march. → ✅ ...on **Monday** in **March**.
> ❌ i think so. → ✅ **I** think so.

## 29.3 Contractions in writing

| Context | Contractions? |
|---|---|
| Chat, Slack, WhatsApp | ✅ Yes |
| Everyday work email | ✅ Yes — sounds natural |
| Client email | ✅ Usually yes |
| Blog posts, marketing | ✅ Yes |
| Technical documentation | ⚠️ Mixed; many style guides allow them |
| Formal reports, proposals | ⚠️ Prefer full forms |
| Legal, academic, contracts | ❌ No |

**Never write:** *gonna, wanna, gotta, kinda, lemme, cuz* in any professional context.

## 29.4 Formal vs informal writing — a practical comparison

**The same message, three registers:**

**Informal (Slack to a colleague):**
> Hey — quick one. The deploy's failing on staging. Looks like a config issue. Can you take a look when you get a sec? Thanks!

**Neutral professional (email to a colleague/manager):**
> Hi Marco,
>
> The staging deploy is failing — it looks like a configuration issue with the new environment variables.
>
> Could you take a look when you have a moment? I'm blocked on this for the release.
>
> Thanks,
> Ana

**Formal (email to an external client):**
> Dear Mr. Ferreira,
>
> I am writing to inform you of a delay in the staging deployment. Our team has identified a configuration issue, which we are currently investigating.
>
> We expect to resolve this within 24 hours and will keep you updated on our progress.
>
> Please let me know if you have any questions.
>
> Kind regards,
> Ana Silva

> **Which to use?** In most international workplaces, the **neutral** version is correct 90% of the time. Over-formality reads as distant or non-native.

## 29.5 Unnatural → Natural: a writing gallery

| ⚠️ Unnatural | ✅ Natural |
|---|---|
| I am writing to you in order to request... | I'm writing to ask... |
| Please, find attached the document. | Here's the document. / Please find the document attached. |
| I remain at your entire disposal. | Let me know if you need anything else. |
| Kindly revert to me. ⚠️ (Indian English) | Please get back to me. |
| We would like to inform you that the meeting is cancelled. | The meeting is cancelled. |
| Do you have doubts? | Do you have any questions? |
| I will pass you the information. | I'll send you the information. |
| Any doubt, contact me. | If you have any questions, let me know. |
| I await your feedback. | Looking forward to your thoughts. |
| It is of extreme importance that... | It's really important that... / We need... |
| In case of doubts, do not hesitate to contact me. ⚠️ | Let me know if you have questions. |
| Thanking you in advance, | Thanks in advance, |
| I come through this email to... | I'm writing to... |
| Follows below the summary. | Here's a summary: |
| We will realize the meeting on Monday. | We'll hold the meeting on Monday. / The meeting is on Monday. |
| Sorry for the inconvenient. | Sorry for the inconvenience. |
| I hope you are well. ✅ | ✅ correct and standard |

## 29.6 A quick self-editing checklist

Before sending anything important:

```text
□ Is the main point in the first two sentences?
□ Can any sentence be split into two?
□ Did I use active voice where possible?
□ Are there words I can delete?
□ Is every "the" needed? Is any missing?
□ Did I capitalize I, languages, days, months, names?
□ its / it's — correct?
□ Are the dates unambiguous? (Write the month in letters)
□ Are the numbers correct and clear?
□ Does it sound like something a native would write, or like a translation?
□ Is the tone right — not too blunt, not overly formal?
□ Did I ask clearly for what I need, with a deadline?
```

---

# 30. Professional English

## 30.1 Email

### Structure

```text
Subject: [Specific and actionable]
Greeting
Purpose (1 sentence)
Details (short paragraphs)
Clear ask + deadline
Closing
```

### Subject lines

| ⚠️ Weak | ✅ Strong |
|---|---|
| Question | Question about the Q3 budget |
| Meeting | Meeting request: Thursday 3 p.m.? |
| Update | Sprint 14 update — 2 blockers |
| Urgent!! | Action needed by Friday: contract review |
| Hi | Following up: API access request |

### Greetings and closings

| Register | Greeting | Closing |
|---|---|---|
| Formal ✍️ | Dear Mr./Ms. Silva, / Dear Sir or Madam, | Kind regards, / Sincerely, / Yours faithfully, 🇬🇧 |
| Professional | Hi John, / Hello Ana, / Hi all, | Best regards, / Best, / Thanks, |
| Casual | Hey Marco, / Hi team, | Cheers, 🇬🇧 / Thanks! / — |

⚠️ *Dear John,* is fine but slightly formal in 🇺🇸. **"Hi [Name],"** is the safe default in international business.
⚠️ Never: *Dear friend, / Hello Mr. John, / Dears,* ⚠️ *Hi guys* (avoid in mixed/formal groups — use *Hi everyone / Hi team*).

### Opening lines

```text
I hope you're doing well.               (standard, safe)
Hope you had a good weekend.            (warmer)
Thanks for your quick reply.            (responsive)
Following up on my previous email.      (chasing)
As discussed in today's call, ...        (referencing)
Quick question about ...                 (short email)
I'm reaching out about ...               (first contact) 🇺🇸
It was great meeting you at ...          (after networking)
```

### Making requests

```text
Could you send me ... by [date]?
Would you be able to review this before Friday?
When you get a chance, could you ...?
I'd appreciate it if you could ...
Could you please confirm whether ...?
Do you have any updates on ...?
Just checking in on the status of ...
Would it be possible to ...?
```

### Common email phrases

| Purpose | Phrase |
|---|---|
| Attaching | Please find the document attached. / I've attached the report. / Here's the file. |
| Referring | As mentioned, ... / Per our conversation, ... / Regarding your question about ... |
| Adding | Also, ... / One more thing: ... / In addition, ... |
| Apologizing | Sorry for the delay. / Apologies for the confusion. / Sorry about that. |
| Bad news | Unfortunately, ... / I'm afraid ... / I wanted to let you know that ... |
| Good news | Good news: ... / I'm happy to report that ... |
| Chasing | Just following up on this. / Any update on this? / Gentle reminder: ... |
| Escalating | I want to flag a risk. / This is becoming urgent. / We need a decision by X. |
| Deferring | Let me look into it and get back to you. / I'll check and confirm. |
| Closing | Let me know if you have any questions. / Looking forward to hearing from you. / Thanks in advance. |
| CC'ing | I'm copying Ana, who's leading this. / Adding Marco to the thread. |
| Forwarding | Forwarding for visibility. / FYI. / Passing this along. |

### Email tone: three levels of the same request

```text
Direct:     Send me the numbers today.
Standard:   Could you send me the numbers by end of day?
Soft:       Would you be able to get me the numbers today? Let me know if that's tight.
```

## 30.2 Meetings

### Starting

```text
Thanks everyone for joining.
Let's get started. / Shall we get started?
Can everyone hear me? / Can you see my screen?
Quick round of intros?
The agenda today is: first ..., then ..., and finally ...
We have 30 minutes, so let's keep it focused.
```

### Participating

| Function | Phrases |
|---|---|
| Taking the floor | Can I jump in here? / If I could add something... / Just a quick point — |
| Interrupting politely | Sorry to interrupt, but... / Sorry, can I just clarify something? |
| Yielding | Sorry, go ahead. / You first. / No, please continue. |
| Returning to a topic | Coming back to what Ana said... / Going back to the earlier point... |
| Moving on | Let's move on to the next item. / Shall we move on? |
| Parking a topic | Let's take that offline. / Can we park that for now? / Let's follow up on that separately. |
| Checking time | We're running low on time. / We have five minutes left. |
| Summarizing | So to summarize: ... / The key takeaways are... / So we've agreed that... |
| Assigning | Who can take this? / I'll take that. / Can you own this one? |
| Ending | Thanks everyone. / I'll send out the notes. / Let's reconvene next week. |

### Video call specifics

```text
You're on mute. / Sorry, I was on mute.
Can you hear me now?
You're breaking up. / You're cutting out.
Sorry, there's a lag.
Let me share my screen.
Can you see my screen? / Can everyone see this?
I'll drop the link in the chat.
Sorry, I have to hop off at the top of the hour.
Let's take this offline.
Can we do a quick round-robin?
```

## 30.3 Asking for clarification — the most valuable skill

```text
Sorry, could you repeat that?
Sorry, I didn't catch that last part.
Could you say that again, a bit more slowly?
What do you mean by ...?
Could you clarify what you mean by "X"?
Could you give me an example?
Just to clarify — are you saying that ...?
Let me make sure I understand: you want us to ..., correct?
So if I understand correctly, ...
Sorry, I'm not following. Could you walk me through it?
Can you break that down for me?
When you say "X", do you mean A or B?
I want to make sure we're on the same page.
Sorry, what does "X" stand for? (acronyms)
Could you spell that?
```

> **Professional advice:** asking for clarification is a sign of competence, not weakness. Senior people do it constantly. Silence followed by a wrong deliverable is far worse.

## 30.4 Agreeing, disagreeing, and giving opinions

### Giving an opinion

```text
Neutral:   I think ... / In my view, ... / From my perspective, ...
Tentative: I'd say ... / It seems to me that ... / My sense is that ...
Strong:    I'm convinced that ... / I strongly believe ...
Hedged:    I might be missing something, but ... / Correct me if I'm wrong, but ...
```

### Agreeing

```text
I agree. / Absolutely. / Exactly. / Good point.
That makes sense. / I'm with you on that.
That's exactly what I was thinking.
I couldn't agree more. (strong)
Fair point. / Fair enough.
I'm on board with that.
```

### Disagreeing politely — the formula

```text
[Acknowledge] + [Soften] + [Your view] + [Justify]
```

| Level | Phrase |
|---|---|
| Very soft | I see your point, but I wonder if... |
| Soft | I'm not sure I agree. Here's why... |
| Neutral | I see it a bit differently. |
| Direct but polite | I'd push back on that a little. |
| Alternative framing | That's one option. Another approach could be... |
| Concern-based | My only concern with that is... |
| Data-based | Based on what we saw last quarter, I'd argue... |
| Devil's advocate | Just to play devil's advocate: what if...? |
| Direct ⚠️ | I disagree. *(usable, but be sure of the relationship)* |

**Avoid:** *"You're wrong."* / *"That's not true."* / *"No."* alone — these land much harder in English than in Portuguese.

### Managing conflict in writing

```text
I think there may be a misunderstanding.
I want to make sure we're aligned on this.
Perhaps I wasn't clear in my previous email — let me rephrase.
I'd like to revisit the timeline we agreed on.
Can we get on a quick call to sort this out?
```

## 30.5 Explaining problems and giving status updates

### Status update template

```text
Status: [on track / at risk / blocked]
Done: ...
In progress: ...
Blockers: ...
Next steps: ...
Need from you: ...
```

**Example:**
> **Status:** At risk.
> We've completed the API integration and the data migration script. The load testing is in progress.
> **Blocker:** We're still waiting on production credentials from IT.
> **Next:** Once we have access, testing takes two days.
> **Need from you:** Could you escalate the credentials request?

### Useful phrases

```text
We're on track for [date].
We're slightly behind, but we should still make the deadline.
We've hit a blocker with ...
There's a risk that ...
I want to flag an issue early.
The root cause was ...
As a workaround, we've ...
To prevent this in the future, we'll ...
I'd like to push the deadline to [date] — here's why.
It's about 70% done.
I'll have a clearer estimate by tomorrow.
```

## 30.6 Confirming information

```text
Just to confirm: [restate the facts].
So we're agreed that ...
To recap: ...
Can you confirm receipt of this?
Please confirm by Friday.
Confirming our meeting on Thursday at 3 p.m. CET.
I'll take that as a yes.
Sending you the notes so we're aligned.
Let me put that in writing so there's no confusion.
```

> **Always confirm in writing** after important verbal agreements. This is standard professional practice, and it removes any risk from language misunderstandings.

## 30.7 Presentations

```text
Opening:      Today I'll cover three things: ..., ..., and ...
              By the end, you'll know how to ...
Transitions:  Let's start with ... / Moving on to ... / That brings me to ...
              Now let's look at ... / With that in mind, ...
Emphasizing:  The key point here is ... / What matters most is ...
              I want to highlight ...
Data:         As you can see from this chart, ...
              The numbers show that ...
              This represents a 20% increase over last quarter.
Handling Q&A: That's a great question. / Let me come back to that.
              I don't have that number offhand — I'll follow up.
              Does that answer your question?
Closing:      To sum up, ... / The three takeaways are ...
              Thanks — happy to take any questions.
```

## 30.8 Technical discussions

```text
Describing:   It works like this: ... / Under the hood, it ...
              The way it's designed, ...
Cause:        The root cause is ... / It's caused by ... / It stems from ...
Reproducing:  I can reproduce it consistently. / It's intermittent.
              It only happens when ...
Trade-offs:   The trade-off is ... / On the plus side ... / The downside is ...
              It's a trade-off between speed and reliability.
Proposing:    What if we ...? / One option is to ... / I'd suggest ...
              Have we considered ...?
Estimating:   Roughly two days. / It's hard to say without digging in.
              Best case ... worst case ...
Pushing back: That would work, but it adds a lot of complexity.
              I'd be careful with that approach because ...
              That's technically possible, but I'm not sure it's worth it.
Uncertainty:  I'd have to check. / Let me verify that.
              Off the top of my head, ...
Deferring:    Let's not over-engineer this for now.
              We can revisit it if it becomes a problem.
```

## 30.9 Chat and instant messaging

Chat is closer to speech than to email.

| Norm | Example |
|---|---|
| Short messages | *"quick q — is staging down?"* |
| Contractions and lowercase are fine | *"can't repro it on my end"* |
| Common abbreviations | FYI, ASAP, EOD (end of day), OOO (out of office), PTO (paid time off), LGTM (looks good to me), IMO, TL;DR, WFH, EOW, ETA, PR, 1:1 |
| Emoji reactions replace "OK" | 👍 ✅ |
| Threads for follow-ups | *"Let's thread this"* |
| Avoid | multiple separate messages instead of one; "Hi" alone with no content |

**Common chat phrases:**
```text
Got a sec?                        Sorry, just seeing this.
Quick question —                  On it.
Any update on X?                  Will do.
Heads up: ...                     Makes sense, thanks.
Can you ping me when you're free?  I'll circle back on this.
Sorry for the delay!              Nice work! / Great catch.
No rush.                          LGTM 🚀
I'm heads-down on X right now.     Let's sync on this tomorrow.
```

## 30.10 Documentation

Documentation English is a specific register: **imperative, present tense, precise.**

```text
✅ Run the migration script.               (imperative)
✅ The API returns a 404 if the ID is invalid.   (present simple)
✅ This field is required.                 (passive is fine here)
✅ Note: This feature is deprecated.
⚠️ You should probably run the script...   (too vague)
❌ We will be running the script...        (wrong register)
```

**Standard signal words:** *Note:, Warning:, Important:, Prerequisites:, Example:, Deprecated:, See also:*

**Standard verbs:** *configure, install, run, enable, disable, define, specify, return, accept, require, support, override, deploy, validate*

---

# 31. Useful Conversation Expressions

## 31.1 Starting conversations

| Situation | Expressions |
|---|---|
| Greeting | Hi! / Hey! / Good morning. / Morning! 🗣️ |
| How are you | How are you? / How's it going? / How've you been? / How are things? / You alright? 🇬🇧 |
| Response | Good, thanks — you? / Not bad. / Pretty good. / Can't complain. 🗣️ / Busy, but good. |
| Meeting someone | Nice to meet you. / Great to finally meet you. / I've heard a lot about you. |
| Seeing again | Good to see you again. / It's been a while! / Long time no see! 🗣️ |
| Small talk openers | How was your weekend? / Any plans for the weekend? / How's the project going? / Crazy weather, right? |
| Work opener | How's your week going? / Busy day? / How's the new role treating you? |
| Approaching someone | Sorry to bother you — do you have a minute? / Is now a good time? |

> ⚠️ **"How are you?" is often a greeting, not a real question.** The expected answer is short and positive. Don't give a detailed health report.

## 31.2 Keeping the conversation going

```text
Really? / Oh, wow. / That's interesting.
Tell me more about that.
How did that go?
What happened next?
And then what?
That makes sense.
I know what you mean.
Same here. / I've been there.
That must have been [hard / great / frustrating].
What about you?
So what do you think about ...?
```

**Follow-up questions are the key skill.** English conversation expects you to ask back:
- "How was your weekend?" — "Good! I went hiking. **How about you?**"

## 31.3 Asking someone to repeat

| Politeness | Expression |
|---|---|
| Most polite | I'm sorry, could you repeat that, please? |
| Standard | Sorry, could you say that again? |
| Standard | Sorry, I didn't catch that. |
| Standard | Sorry, one more time? |
| Casual 🗣️ | Sorry, what? / Come again? / Say that again? |
| Very casual ⚠️ | Huh? / What? *(can sound rude)* |
| Specific | Sorry, the last part? / Sorry, did you say Tuesday or Thursday? |
| Speed | Could you slow down a bit? Sorry, my English is better when it's slower. |
| Volume | Sorry, could you speak up a little? |
| Connection | Sorry, you cut out. Could you repeat that? |

> ⚠️ Avoid *"Repeat, please."* — it sounds like a command. Use *"Could you repeat that?"*

## 31.4 Saying you didn't understand

```text
Sorry, I don't follow.
I'm not sure I understand.
Sorry, you lost me.
I didn't get that last part.
Sorry, that went over my head. 🗣️
I understood the first part, but not the rest.
Sorry, what does "X" mean?
I'm not familiar with that term.
Could you explain that in a different way?
Sorry — English isn't my first language. Could you rephrase that?
```

> **The last one is completely acceptable and often appreciated.** People adjust immediately.

## 31.5 Asking for clarification

```text
What do you mean exactly?
Could you give me an example?
Just so I'm clear — you're saying ...?
Do you mean X or Y?
Are you asking me to ..., or ...?
Sorry, when you say "soon", what kind of timeline are we talking about?
Can you be more specific?
Could you walk me through it?
Let me make sure I got that right: ...
```

## 31.6 Buying time

```text
That's a good question.
Let me think about that for a second.
Hmm, good point — give me a moment.
Let me see...
How should I put this...
Well, it depends.
That's a tricky one.
I'd have to think about it.
Off the top of my head, ...
Can I get back to you on that?
```

## 31.7 Correcting yourself

```text
Sorry, I meant ...
Actually, let me rephrase that.
What I meant to say was ...
Sorry, that came out wrong.
Let me start over.
Or rather, ...
Sorry, I misspoke — it's Thursday, not Tuesday.
Scratch that. 🗣️
Wait, no — ...
```

## 31.8 Expressing uncertainty

```text
I think so, but I'm not 100% sure.
I'm not certain, but ...
As far as I know, ...
I could be wrong, but ...
Don't quote me on this, but ... 🗣️
I'd have to double-check.
Probably. / Maybe. / It's possible.
I have no idea.
Your guess is as good as mine. 🗣️
I'm not the right person to ask — try Ana.
```

## 31.9 Agreeing

```text
Absolutely. / Exactly. / Definitely.
That's true. / Good point. / Fair enough.
I agree. / I couldn't agree more.
Same here. / Me too. / Me neither. (negative)
That's what I was thinking.
Makes sense. / Sounds good.
You're right. / Right.
100%. 🗣️ / Totally. 🗣️ / For sure. 🗣️
```

## 31.10 Disagreeing politely

```text
I'm not sure I agree.
I see what you mean, but ...
That's a fair point, but ...
I'd look at it differently.
Hmm, I'm not convinced.
I think there might be another way to look at it.
I hear you, but ... 🗣️
That's one way to see it.
Actually, I think ...
With respect, I'd disagree. ✍️
```

## 31.11 Giving an opinion

```text
I think ... / I feel like ... 🗣️
In my opinion, ... ✍️
If you ask me, ... 🗣️
Personally, I'd ...
From my point of view, ...
I'd say ...
It seems to me that ...
My take is ... 🗣️
Honestly? I think ...
```

## 31.12 Changing the subject

```text
By the way, ...
Speaking of which, ...
That reminds me — ...
Anyway, ...
Changing the subject for a second, ...
Before I forget, ...
On a different note, ...
Oh, one more thing —
```

## 31.13 Ending a conversation

```text
Anyway, I should get going.
I'd better let you go.
It was great talking to you.
Let's catch up soon.
I'll let you get back to work.
Sorry, I have another meeting — talk soon?
Nice seeing you!
Take care. / Have a good one. 🗣️ / See you around.
Let's keep in touch.
I'll follow up by email.
```

**Endings on calls:**
```text
OK, I think that covers everything.
Great — thanks everyone.
Talk to you next week.
Have a good weekend!
Bye for now.
```

## 31.14 Reacting to news

| News | Reaction |
|---|---|
| Good | That's great! / Congratulations! / Nice one! / Well deserved! / Awesome! |
| Neutral | Oh, OK. / Good to know. / Interesting. / Got it. |
| Bad | Oh no. / I'm sorry to hear that. / That's rough. 🗣️ / That's a shame. |
| Surprising | Really? / No way! 🗣️ / You're kidding! / Seriously? |
| Sympathetic | That sounds tough. / Let me know if I can help. |

## 31.15 Everyday politeness

```text
Please / Thank you / Thanks / Thanks a lot / Thanks so much
You're welcome / No problem / No worries / Sure / Anytime / My pleasure
Excuse me (getting attention or passing by)
Sorry (apologizing OR "excuse me" 🇬🇧)
After you.
Go ahead.
Do you mind if I ...?
Sorry to bother you.
I appreciate it.
That's very kind of you.
```

> ⚠️ **`Excuse me` vs `Sorry`:**
> - **Excuse me** = before an action (getting attention, passing through, interrupting)
> - **Sorry** = after an action (apologizing), and in 🇬🇧 also for "excuse me"
>
> ⚠️ **English uses `please` and `thank you` far more than Portuguese.** Under-using them sounds abrupt. When in doubt, add one.

---

# 32. What Native Speakers Actually Say

## 32.1 The gap between textbook and reality

Textbooks teach a formal, complete, careful English that native speakers rarely use in conversation. Both are correct — but only one sounds natural.

| Textbook English | What people actually say |
|---|---|
| How are you? | How's it going? / How are you doing? / How've you been? |
| I am fine, thank you. And you? | Good, thanks — you? / Not bad. / Pretty good. |
| What are you doing? | What are you up to? / What're you working on? |
| What is your job? | What do you do? |
| I do not understand. | I don't get it. / You lost me. / Sorry, I'm not following. |
| Could you repeat, please? | Sorry, what was that? / Say that again? |
| It is not a problem. | No worries. / No problem. / It's all good. |
| You are welcome. | Sure. / No problem. / Anytime. / Of course. |
| I would like to... | I'd love to... / I want to... / Can I...? |
| Excuse me, where is the toilet? 🇬🇧 | Where's the bathroom? 🇺🇸 / Where's the loo? 🇬🇧 🗣️ |
| That is very good. | That's great. / Nice! / Awesome. |
| I am very tired. | I'm exhausted. / I'm wiped out. 🗣️ |
| It is very expensive. | It's pricey. / That's a lot. |
| I must go now. | I gotta go. / I should get going. |
| Do you want to have lunch? | Wanna grab lunch? |
| I agree with you. | Totally. / Exactly. / For sure. |
| Goodbye. | See you! / Later! / Take care. / Bye! |
| What is your opinion? | What do you think? |
| I am not certain. | I'm not sure. / Not sure. |
| Would you like a drink? | Want something to drink? |
| I will telephone you. | I'll call you. / I'll give you a ring. 🇬🇧 |
| The weather is nice. | Nice day, isn't it? |
| Please wait a moment. | Hang on a sec. / One sec. / Just a moment. |
| It is possible that... | Might be... / Could be... |
| I am in agreement. | I'm on board. / Sounds good to me. |
| I did not sleep well. | I barely slept. / I had a rough night. |
| Let us begin. | Let's get started. / Shall we? |

## 32.2 The "what are you doing" family

These are all common, with different nuances:

| Question | Meaning | Register |
|---|---|---|
| What are you doing? | right now, literally | neutral |
| What are you up to? | right now OR generally, informal | 🗣️ friendly |
| What are you working on? | professional context | neutral |
| What have you been up to? | since we last talked | 🗣️ |
| What's going on? | what's happening (situation or greeting) | 🗣️ |
| What's up? / 'Sup? | greeting, not a real question | 🗣️ casual |
| What's new? | any news since last time | 🗣️ |
| Anything exciting happening? | small talk | 🗣️ |

> ⚠️ **"What's up?" is usually a greeting.** The expected reply is *"Not much, you?"* or *"Hey!"* — not a description of your day.

## 32.3 What people actually say at work

| Formal | Natural workplace English |
|---|---|
| I will contact you. | I'll reach out. / I'll ping you. |
| I will investigate this matter. | I'll look into it. |
| Please provide me with the information. | Can you send me the details? |
| We must postpone the meeting. | Let's push the meeting. / Can we move it? |
| I would like to schedule a meeting. | Can we set up a call? / Are you free Thursday? |
| I have completed the task. | It's done. / I've wrapped it up. |
| I do not have the availability. | I'm swamped. / My plate's full. 🗣️ |
| I will consider your proposal. | Let me think about it. / Let me sit on it. |
| Please inform me of any updates. | Keep me posted. / Let me know how it goes. |
| I acknowledge receipt. | Got it. / Received, thanks. |
| That is not within my responsibilities. | That's not really my area — try Ana. |
| I concur with your assessment. | I agree. / Good call. |
| We should discuss this at a later time. | Let's take this offline. / Let's circle back. |
| Please clarify your requirements. | What exactly do you need? |
| The project is progressing satisfactorily. | We're on track. |
| I require additional time. | I need a bit more time. |
| The system is not functioning. | It's down. / It's broken. / It's not working. |

## 32.4 Common informal expressions — understand, use selectively

| Expression | Meaning | Safe to use? |
|---|---|---|
| I'm swamped / slammed | very busy | ✅ workplace |
| My plate's full | too much work | ✅ workplace |
| Heads-up | advance warning | ✅ workplace |
| Circle back | return to a topic | ✅ workplace |
| Touch base | briefly check in | ✅ workplace |
| Ballpark figure | rough estimate | ✅ workplace |
| Low-hanging fruit | easy wins | ✅ workplace |
| Move the needle | make real impact | ⚠️ corporate cliché |
| Deep dive | detailed analysis | ✅ workplace |
| Bandwidth | available time/capacity | ✅ workplace |
| Take it with a grain of salt | be skeptical | ✅ general |
| It's a long shot | unlikely to work | ✅ general |
| Back to square one | start over | ✅ general |
| Get the ball rolling | start | ✅ general |
| On the same page | in agreement | ✅ general |
| Cut corners | do it cheaply/badly | ✅ general |
| Bite the bullet | accept something unpleasant | ✅ general |
| Play it by ear | decide as we go | ✅ general |
| It's not rocket science | it's not that hard | 🗣️ casual |
| A no-brainer | an obvious decision | 🗣️ casual |
| Piece of cake | very easy | 🗣️ casual |
| Under the weather | slightly ill | ✅ general |
| Hit the ground running | start fast and effectively | ✅ workplace |
| Wrap up | finish | ✅ general |
| Reach out | contact | ✅ workplace 🇺🇸 |
| Ping | message | ✅ tech |
| Sync | align, meet briefly | ✅ tech/workplace |

## 32.5 Should you use slang?

**No, mostly.** Here's a realistic policy:

| Category | Recommendation |
|---|---|
| Contractions (*I'm, don't, it's*) | ✅ **Always use** — this is not slang |
| Reductions (*gonna, wanna*) | ✅ Understand; use in casual speech; never write |
| Common phrasal verbs | ✅ Use freely |
| Business idioms (*touch base, heads-up*) | ✅ Use — they're expected at work |
| General idioms (*piece of cake*) | ⚠️ Occasionally — don't force them |
| Regional slang (*innit, y'all, mate, bro*) | ❌ Avoid unless you live there |
| Current internet slang | ❌ Avoid — it dates instantly |
| Swearing | ❌ Avoid — the intensity is hard to calibrate in a foreign language |

> **The real marker of fluency is not slang.** It's rhythm, collocations, and appropriate register. A speaker who says *"I'd like to raise a concern about the timeline"* with good stress sounds far more competent than one who says *"that ain't gonna fly, bro."*

## 32.6 American vs British — only what matters

| Category | 🇺🇸 American | 🇬🇧 British |
|---|---|---|
| **Vocabulary you'll meet daily** | apartment, elevator, vacation, truck, gas, cell phone, soccer, fall, subway, cookie, french fries, line (queue), schedule, movie, garbage, sidewalk | flat, lift, holiday, lorry, petrol, mobile, football, autumn, underground/tube, biscuit, chips, queue, timetable, film, rubbish, pavement |
| **Spelling** | color, center, organize, traveled, analyze, defense, catalog, program | colour, centre, organise, travelled, analyse, defence, catalogue, programme |
| **Past forms** | learned, dreamed, gotten | learnt, dreamt, got |
| **Dates** | March 4, 2025 (3/4/25) | 4 March 2025 (4/3/25) |
| **Present Perfect** | *Did you eat yet?* | *Have you eaten yet?* |
| **have got** | less common | very common |
| **at/on the weekend** | on the weekend | at the weekend |
| **Floors** | first floor = ground level | ground floor, then first floor |
| **Time** | *quarter after five* | *quarter past five* |
| **Politeness** | more effusive (*awesome, great job*) | more understated (*not bad, quite good*) |

> **Practical advice:** pick one and be consistent, especially in writing. Nobody cares which. The 🇺🇸 variant is more common in tech and international business; 🇬🇧 in Europe and academia. **Never mix spellings within one document.**

> ⚠️ **British understatement can be genuinely confusing:** *"That's quite interesting"* may mean "I'm not convinced." *"I might suggest..."* may mean "do this." *"With the greatest respect..."* usually precedes a strong disagreement. Take British professional feedback about 20% more seriously than it sounds.

---

# 33. Vocabulary Strategy

## 33.1 The core principle

**Learn words in context, in phrases, in patterns — not as isolated translations.**

```text
❌ decision = decisão
✅ make a decision / a tough decision / the decision to hire / it's your decision
```

An isolated word gives you the meaning but not the ability to *use* it. A phrase gives you both.

## 33.2 Why `make a decision` beats `decision`

If you learn only the noun, you will produce:
- ❌ *take a decision* (Portuguese *tomar*)
- ❌ *do a decision*
- ❌ *have a decision*

Two of those are wrong, and the third is very formal 🇬🇧. But if you learn **make a decision** as a unit, you can never get it wrong. Multiply this by 500 collocations and you sound native.

**Chunks give you:**
1. Correct grammar automatically
2. Correct prepositions automatically
3. Faster speech (retrieved as one unit, not assembled)
4. Natural-sounding output

## 33.3 How much vocabulary do you actually need?

| Words | What it gives you |
|---|---|
| 1,000 most frequent | ~85% of spoken English |
| 2,000 | ~90% of general text |
| 3,000 | ~95% — comfortable everyday reading |
| 5,000 | most non-technical content |
| 8,000–10,000 | comfortable with novels, films, native conversation |

**The practical takeaway:** the first 2,000 words are worth more than the next 8,000. Master them completely — every collocation, every phrasal verb, every idiom built on them — before chasing rare vocabulary.

> **Deep vs wide:** knowing 100 uses of `get`, `take`, `put`, `make`, `do`, `have`, `go`, `come`, `run`, `set` is more useful than knowing 500 rare nouns.

## 33.4 Word families and derivation

Learning one word gives you five if you learn the family:

| Verb | Noun | Person | Adjective | Adverb |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| decide | decision | decision-maker | decisive | decisively |
| succeed | success | — | successful | successfully |
| develop | development | developer | developing/developed | — |
| manage | management | manager | manageable | — |
| create | creation/creativity | creator | creative | creatively |
| produce | production/product | producer | productive | — |
| analyze | analysis | analyst | analytical | analytically |
| compete | competition | competitor | competitive | competitively |
| depend | dependence | dependent | dependable | — |
| inform | information | informant | informative | — |
| explain | explanation | — | explanatory | — |
| solve | solution | — | solvable | — |
| perform | performance | performer | — | — |
| require | requirement | — | required | — |
| improve | improvement | — | improved | — |
| maintain | maintenance | — | maintainable | — |

> ⚠️ **Watch the stress shifts within a family** — they trip up learners badly:
> *com-**PETE*** → *com-pe-**TI**-tion*; ***AN**-a-lyze* → *a-**NAL**-y-sis*; ***PHO**-to-graph* → *pho-**TOG**-ra-phy*; *e-**CON**-o-my* → *e-co-**NOM**-ic*.
> **General pattern:** words ending in **-tion, -ic, -ity, -ical** put the stress on the syllable **immediately before** the ending: *in-for-**MA**-tion*, *spe-**CIF**-ic*, *pos-si-**BIL**-i-ty*, *tech-**NI**-cal*.

## 33.5 Prefixes and suffixes worth knowing

### Prefixes

| Prefix | Meaning | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| **un-** | not / reverse | unclear, unusual, undo, unfair |
| **in- / im- / il- / ir-** | not | incorrect, impossible, illegal, irregular |
| **dis-** | not / opposite | disagree, disconnect, dishonest |
| **non-** | not | non-technical, non-profit, non-standard |
| **re-** | again | rewrite, restart, reconsider |
| **mis-** | wrongly | misunderstand, misuse, misconfigure |
| **over-** | too much | overload, overestimate, overwrite |
| **under-** | too little | underestimate, underperform |
| **pre-** | before | preview, prepare, preprocess |
| **post-** | after | postpone, postmortem |
| **sub-** | under | subset, subfolder, submit |
| **auto-** | self | automatic, autonomous |
| **multi-** | many | multitask, multilingual |
| **inter-** | between | international, interface |
| **out-** | beyond | outperform, outsource, outdated |
| **de-** | reverse | deactivate, decode, deploy |

### Suffixes

| Suffix | Makes | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| **-tion / -sion** | noun (process) | creation, decision, permission |
| **-ment** | noun (result) | improvement, agreement, requirement |
| **-ness** | noun (quality) | happiness, awareness, readiness |
| **-ity** | noun (state) | ability, security, complexity |
| **-er / -or** | noun (person/thing) | developer, editor, server |
| **-ist** | noun (person) | specialist, analyst |
| **-able / -ible** | adjective (possible) | readable, flexible, scalable |
| **-ful** | adjective (full of) | useful, helpful, careful |
| **-less** | adjective (without) | useless, wireless, endless |
| **-ive** | adjective | effective, productive |
| **-al** | adjective | technical, personal |
| **-ly** | adverb | quickly, probably |
| **-ize / -ise** | verb | organize, optimize, prioritize |
| **-en** | verb (make) | shorten, strengthen, widen |

**Practical use:** these let you *guess* meanings and *build* words you've never seen. *"Unmaintainable"* = un + maintain + able.

## 33.6 A practical learning method

### 1. Capture in context

Don't write down *"reluctant = relutante."* Write down:
> *"She was **reluctant to** commit to the deadline."*

Store: the sentence, the collocation (*reluctant to + verb*), and where you found it.

### 2. Prioritize ruthlessly

Ask: **"Will I use or hear this in the next month?"** If no, skip it. You do not need *"perspicacious"*.

### 3. Use spaced repetition, but for phrases

Flashcards work — with sentences, not words. Front: *"I'm ___ to commit to that deadline."* Back: *reluctant*.

### 4. Produce it within 48 hours

A word you've only read is passive. Write it in a sentence, use it in a message, say it out loud. That's what moves it into active vocabulary.

### 5. Build topic clusters

Learn vocabulary by domain, not alphabetically:
- **Meetings:** agenda, minutes, action item, follow-up, stakeholder, deadline, blocker, alignment
- **Problems:** issue, bug, glitch, outage, root cause, workaround, fix, patch, mitigate
- **Money:** budget, cost, expense, revenue, invoice, quote, margin, forecast
- **Time:** deadline, timeline, milestone, schedule, ETA, lead time, turnaround

### 6. Learn the "small" words deeply

The highest-frequency words carry the most weight. Spend real time on: *get, take, make, do, have, go, come, put, set, run, keep, turn, look, bring, hold, work, call, leave, break, pick*.

### 7. Read and listen in your professional domain

You already know the concepts, so you can infer vocabulary. A Brazilian engineer reading English engineering blogs learns faster than one reading literature.

## 33.7 Active vs passive vocabulary

```text
Passive = you understand it when you see/hear it   (large)
Active  = you can produce it when speaking/writing  (small)
```

Everyone's passive vocabulary is 3–5× their active one. That's normal.

**To move words from passive to active:**
- Use the word deliberately three times in a week
- Force yourself to avoid your default word (*good, nice, thing, do*) and reach for a specific one
- Rewrite something you wrote, replacing generic words

**Words worth upgrading from:**

| Generic | Specific alternatives |
|---|---|
| good | effective, solid, reliable, useful, strong, promising |
| bad | poor, flawed, unreliable, problematic, weak |
| big | significant, substantial, major, considerable |
| thing | issue, factor, aspect, element, point, item |
| do | handle, carry out, perform, implement, address |
| get | obtain, receive, acquire, reach, achieve |
| very | extremely, particularly, remarkably, highly |
| a lot of | numerous, considerable, substantial |
| said | mentioned, explained, pointed out, noted, argued |

> ⚠️ **Don't overdo this.** Simple words are usually better. The point is having options, not replacing every word with a longer one.

# 34. Collocations

## 34.1 What they are

A **collocation** is a combination of words that native speakers habitually use together. They're not governed by logic — only by convention.

```text
✅ strong coffee        ❌ powerful coffee
✅ heavy rain           ❌ strong rain
✅ make a mistake       ❌ do a mistake
✅ take a photo         ❌ make a photo
✅ fast food            ❌ quick food
✅ a big decision       ❌ a large decision
```

**Collocations are where "correct" English becomes "natural" English.** They're also where Portuguese interference is strongest, because Portuguese has its own set.

## 34.2 Verb + noun (the highest-value group)

### `make`
```text
make a decision          make a mistake           make money
make an effort           make progress            make a difference
make sense               make sure                make a suggestion
make an appointment      make a phone call        make a plan
make a change            make friends             make a profit
make an offer            make an exception        make room
make a complaint         make an impression       make a mess
make coffee/dinner       make a reservation       make an announcement
```

### `do`
```text
do business              do research              do homework
do the dishes            do a favor               do exercise
do your best             do damage                do harm
do the shopping          do the paperwork         do an analysis
do a good job            do nothing/something     do the math
```

### `take`
```text
take a break             take a look              take a photo
take a seat              take responsibility      take advantage of
take a risk              take notes               take place (= happen)
take time                take a decision ⚠️🇬🇧     take steps
take care of             take part in             take an exam/test 🇺🇸
take medicine            take a taxi/bus          take into account
take the lead            take action              take a chance
```

### `have`
```text
have a problem           have an idea             have a meeting
have lunch/dinner        have a look              have a break 🇬🇧
have a chat              have fun                 have an impact
have access to           have experience          have a good time
have a baby              have a shower 🇬🇧         have doubts ⚠️ → have questions
have an argument         have the right to        have trouble (doing)
```

### `get`
```text
get ready                get better               get married
get lost                 get a job                get in touch
get started              get involved             get rid of
get the point            get permission           get feedback
get an email             get home                 get angry/upset/tired
get access               get approval             get results
```

### `give`
```text
give advice              give feedback            give a presentation
give a hand (= help)     give someone a call      give an example
give permission          give a discount          give it a try
give notice              give someone a ride 🇺🇸   give priority to
```

### `pay / keep / save / spend`
```text
pay attention            pay a visit              pay a fine
pay the bill             pay in cash              pay by card
keep in mind             keep in touch            keep a promise
keep calm                keep a secret            keep track of
keep an eye on           keep up with             keep someone posted
save time                save money               save a file
save energy              save a seat
spend time               spend money              spend the weekend
```

### `break / run / hold / raise / meet / reach / draw / set`
```text
break a promise          break the law            break the news
break a record           break the rules
run a business           run a test               run late
run out of               run a risk               run an experiment
hold a meeting           hold a position          hold your breath
raise a question         raise concerns           raise money
raise awareness          raise a family
meet a deadline          meet requirements        meet expectations
meet someone halfway
reach an agreement       reach a conclusion       reach a decision
reach out to             reach a target
draw a conclusion        draw attention to        draw a line
set a deadline           set a goal               set an example
set up a meeting         set priorities           set the tone
```

## 34.5 Business and professional collocations

```text
meet a deadline              miss a deadline           extend a deadline
hit a target                 exceed expectations       fall short of
launch a product             roll out a feature        ship a release
close a deal                 win a contract            lose a client
raise an issue               address a concern         resolve a conflict
schedule a meeting           attend a meeting          chair a meeting
take minutes                 follow up on              circle back to
allocate resources           cut costs                 stay within budget
gain experience              build a relationship      develop skills
give notice                  hand in your resignation  get promoted

provide support              deliver value             add value
make a case for              build consensus           reach alignment
```

## 34.6 Technical / software collocations

```text
deploy to production         push a commit             merge a branch
open/close a ticket          fix a bug                 introduce a bug
run tests                    write tests               break the build
review code                  refactor code             ship a feature
scale up / scale out         optimize performance      reduce latency
handle errors                throw an exception        catch an error

roll back a release          cut a release             tag a version

```

## 34.7 Everyday life collocations

```text
catch a bus / train / flight      miss a bus / flight
take a shower 🇺🇸 / have a shower 🇬🇧
make the bed                      set an alarm
do the laundry                    take out the trash
run errands                       grab a coffee
get some sleep                    stay up late
have a lie-in 🇬🇧 / sleep in 🇺🇸
book a table / a flight / a hotel
check in / check out              pack your bags
withdraw money                    pay in installments
go for a walk / a run / a drink
watch a movie                     listen to a podcast
```

## 34.8 Collocations Portuguese speakers get wrong

| Portuguese | ❌ Literal | ✅ Correct collocation |
|---|---|---|
| tomar uma decisão | take a decision ⚠️ | **make** a decision 🇺🇸 |
| fazer uma pergunta | make a question | **ask** a question |
| dar uma olhada | give a look | **take/have** a look |
| tirar uma foto | take out a photo | **take** a photo |
| perder o ônibus | lose the bus | **miss** the bus |
| assistir a um filme | assist a movie | **watch** a movie |
| prestar atenção | pay attention ✅ | pay attention |
| fazer uma reunião | do a meeting | **have/hold** a meeting |
| ter razão | have reason | **be** right |
| ganhar dinheiro | win money | **make/earn** money |
| ficar doente | stay sick | **get** sick |
| pegar um resfriado | catch a cold ✅ | catch a cold |
| dar certo | give right | **work out** |
| chegar a um acordo | arrive at an agreement | **reach** an agreement |
| cumprir um prazo | comply a deadline | **meet** a deadline |
| correr risco | run risk ✅ | run a risk / take a risk |
| dar aula | give class | **teach** a class |
| fazer uma prova | make a test | **take** a test |
| tomar café da manhã | take breakfast | **have** breakfast |
| marcar uma consulta | mark an appointment | **make/book** an appointment |
| dar um telefonema | give a telephone | **make** a call |
| tirar férias | take vacations | **take** a vacation / **take time off** |
| abrir uma exceção | open an exception | **make** an exception |
| levantar uma questão | lift a question | **raise** a question / **bring up** a point |
| contar uma piada | count a joke | **tell** a joke |
| bater um recorde | hit a record | **break** a record |
| pagar uma conta | pay an account | **pay** a bill |
| cometer um erro | commit an error ⚠️ | **make** a mistake |
| dar uma sugestão | give a suggestion ⚠️ | **make** a suggestion |
| ter uma dúvida | have a doubt | **have** a question |

> **`have a doubt` is a top-3 error in professional English.** In English, *doubt* means genuine disbelief (*"I doubt it will work"*). For *"tenho uma dúvida"*, say **"I have a question"** or **"I'm not clear on..."**

## 34.9 How to learn collocations

1. **Notice them.** When reading, highlight verb+noun pairs, not single words.
2. **Record them as units.** Your notes should contain *"meet a deadline"*, never *"deadline = prazo"*.
3. **Use a collocation dictionary** (Oxford Collocations Dictionary is free online) when writing something important.
4. **Search-engine test:** put the phrase in quotes and see how many results it gets. *"make a decision"* → millions. *"do a decision"* → almost none.
5. **Prefer high-frequency verbs.** If you're unsure between *"conduct an analysis"* and *"do an analysis"* — both work. But *"analyze it"* is simplest and always correct.

# 35. Quick-Reference Tables

## 35.1 Essential tenses

| Tense | Structure | Use | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| Present Simple | V / V-s | habits, facts, states, opinions | She **works** here. |
| Present Continuous | am/is/are + V-ing | now, temporary, arrangements | She'**s working** now. |
| Past Simple | V-ed / irregular | finished past | She **worked** yesterday. |
| Past Continuous | was/were + V-ing | in progress in the past | She **was working** at 6. |
| Present Perfect | have/has + PP | past connected to now | She'**s worked** here for 5 years. |
| Pres. Perf. Cont. | have/has been + V-ing | ongoing duration | She'**s been working** all day. |
| Past Perfect | had + PP | before another past event | She'**d worked** there before. |
| Future `will` | will + V | decision now, prediction | She'**ll work** on it. |
| Future `going to` | am/is/are going to + V | plan, evidence | She'**s going to work** on it. |
| Future Continuous | will be + V-ing | in progress in the future | She'**ll be working** at 9. |

## 35.2 Modal verbs

| Modal | Function | Example | Past form |
|---|---|---|---|
| can | ability, permission, request | **Can** you help? | could / was able to |
| could | polite request, possibility, past ability | **Could** you help? | could have + PP |
| may | formal permission, possibility | **May** I? | might have + PP |
| might | possibility | It **might** rain. | might have + PP |
| must | obligation (rule), deduction | You **must** sign. | had to / must have + PP |
| have to | obligation (external) | I **have to** go. | had to |
| should | advice, expectation | You **should** rest. | should have + PP |
| would | hypothetical, polite request | I **would** wait. | would have + PP |
| will | future, promise | I'**ll** call. | would |
| shall 🇬🇧 | offer/suggestion | **Shall** we? | — |
| need to | necessity | You **need to** check. | needed to |
| be able to | ability (all tenses) | I'**ll be able to** join. | was able to |

**Certainty scale:** *must (100%) > should (90%) > may/might/could (50%) > can't (0%)*

## 35.3 Pronouns

| Subject | Object | Poss. adjective | Poss. pronoun | Reflexive |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| I | me | my | mine | myself |
| you | you | your | yours | yourself/yourselves |
| he | him | his | his | himself |
| she | her | her | hers | herself |
| it | it | its | — | itself |
| we | us | our | ours | ourselves |
| they | them | their | theirs | themselves |

**Indefinites:**

| | people | things | places |
|---|---|---|---|
| some- (positive) | someone/somebody | something | somewhere |
| any- (question/negative) | anyone/anybody | anything | anywhere |
| no- (negative) | no one/nobody | nothing | nowhere |
| every- (all) | everyone/everybody | everything | everywhere |

## 35.4 Question words

| Word | Asks about | Example |
|---|---|---|
| what | thing / action | What do you do? |
| where | place | Where is it? |
| when | time | When did it start? |
| who | person (subject) | Who called? |
| whose | possession | Whose is this? |
| which | choice from a set | Which one? |
| why | reason | Why not? |
| how | manner / method | How does it work? |
| how much | amount / price | How much is it? |
| how many | number | How many people? |
| how long | duration | How long does it take? |
| how often | frequency | How often do you go? |
| how far | distance | How far is it? |
| how old | age | How old is it? |
| what time | clock time | What time is the call? |
| what kind of | type | What kind of issue? |

**Structure:** `WH + AUX + SUBJECT + VERB?` — except subject questions (*Who called?*).

## 35.5 Prepositions of time and place

| | at | on | in |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Time** | clock times, night, noon, the moment | days, dates, specific mornings | months, years, seasons, morning/afternoon/evening |
| **Place** | points, locations, addresses with number | surfaces, floors, streets, transport (bus/train) | enclosed spaces, cities, countries, cars |

**Other essentials:**

| Preposition | Time | Place/Movement |
|---|---|---|
| since | starting point | — |
| for | duration | — |
| during | within a period | — |
| until | up to a moment | — |
| by | deadline | near |
| from...to | range | origin → destination |
| to | — | destination |
| into / onto | — | entering / onto a surface |
| through / across | — | in-and-out / side-to-side |
| under / over | — | below / above |
| between / among | — | two / many |

## 35.6 Comparatives and superlatives

| Type | Comparative | Superlative | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 syllable | -er | the -est | fast → faster → the fastest |
| 1 syl. CVC | double + -er | the -est | big → bigger → the biggest |
| ends in -y | -ier | the -iest | easy → easier → the easiest |
| 2+ syllables | more | the most | useful → more useful → the most useful |
| good | better | the best | |
| bad | worse | the worst | |
| far | further/farther | the furthest | |
| much/many | more | the most | |
| little | less | the least | |

**Structures:** `X is + comparative + than Y` / `X is the + superlative + in/of ...` / `as ... as` / `not as ... as` / `the more ..., the more ...`

## 35.7 Common connectors

| Function | Casual 🗣️ | Neutral | Formal ✍️ |
|---|---|---|---|
| Addition | and, plus, also | in addition | moreover, furthermore |
| Contrast | but, though | however, while | nevertheless, whereas |
| Result | so, that's why | as a result | therefore, consequently |
| Reason | because, since | as, due to | owing to |
| Example | like | for example | for instance, such as |
| Condition | if | unless, as long as | provided that |
| Time | when, after, then | as soon as, once | subsequently |
| Restating | I mean | in other words | that is (i.e.) |
| Concession | still, anyway | even so | notwithstanding |

**Grammar reminders:** *although/even though + clause*; *despite/in spite of + noun/-ing*; *however* starts a new sentence with a comma.

## 35.8 Commonly confused words

| Pair | Quick rule |
|---|---|
| just / only | *just* = recently, simply, exactly / *only* = nothing more |
| still / yet / already | continuing / not-yet (end) / done sooner than expected |
| very / really | *very* + adj/adv only; *really* also + verb |
| too / very | *too* = excessive (negative) / *very* = high degree |
| so / such | *so* + adj / *such* + (a) + adj + noun |
| say / tell | *tell* + person / *say* + something |
| speak / talk | *speak* = languages, formal; *talk* = conversation |
| make / do | *make* = create; *do* = perform an activity |
| know / meet | *know* = state; *meet* = first encounter |
| see / look / watch | perceive / direct eyes at / follow over time |
| hear / listen | perceive / pay attention to |
| bring / take | toward me / away from me |
| come / go | toward the listener / away |
| remember / remind | in my head / someone causes me to remember |
| learn / teach | receive / give knowledge |
| borrow / lend | take from / give to |
| job / work | countable position / uncountable activity |
| fun / funny | enjoyable / makes you laugh |
| house / home | building / where you live |
| look for / find | search / result |
| wait / expect / hope | stay for / predict / desire |
| hope / wish | possible / impossible-regret |
| maybe / may be | perhaps / verb phrase |
| especially / specially | above all / for a specific purpose |
| actually / currently | in fact / now |
| eventually / finally | in the end, over time / at last |
| real / really | adjective / adverb |
| good / well | adjective / adverb |
| hard / hardly | with effort / almost not |
| late / lately | not on time / recently |
| near / nearly | close / almost |
| its / it's | possessive / it is |
| than / then | comparison / time |
| affect / effect | verb / noun |
| lose / miss | no longer have / fail to catch |
| win / earn / beat | prize / money / opponent |
| fewer / less | countable / uncountable |
| among / between | many / two |
| by / until | deadline / continuing to |
| during / for | when / how long |

> **Reminder:** with separable verbs, a **pronoun object must go in the middle**: *turn **it** off*, *pick **her** up*.

## 35.10 Sentence patterns

```text
I want to + V                  I want to check something.
I need to + V                  I need to leave early.
I'd like to + V                I'd like to suggest an alternative.
I'd rather + V                 I'd rather wait.
I'm trying to + V              I'm trying to understand the issue.
I'm looking for + N            I'm looking for the config file.
I'm used to + N/-ing           I'm used to working remotely.
I used to + V                  I used to work in sales.
I'm interested in + N/-ing     I'm interested in learning more.
It depends on + N              It depends on the budget.
I'm not sure if + clause       I'm not sure if that's possible.
I don't know whether + clause  I don't know whether to wait.
The reason is that + clause    The reason is that we're understaffed.
It looks like + clause         It looks like a config issue.
It sounds like + clause        It sounds like a good plan.
I think + clause               I think we should wait.
I don't think + clause         I don't think it'll work.
Would you mind + -ing?         Would you mind repeating that?
Could you + V?                 Could you send me the link?
Would you like + N / to V?     Would you like to join?
How about + -ing?              How about meeting at 3?
What about + N/-ing?           What about the deadline?
Why don't we + V?              Why don't we split this?
It's + adj + to + V            It's hard to say.
It takes + time + to + V       It takes two days to process.
There's no point in + -ing     There's no point in rushing.
It's worth + -ing              It's worth checking.
The more X, the more Y         The more we test, the fewer bugs we ship.
too + adj + to + V             It's too late to change it.
adj + enough + to + V          It's stable enough to release.
by + -ing                      We fixed it by restarting the service.
instead of + -ing              Instead of rewriting it, we patched it.
```
