| hear | heard | heard | | understand | understood | understood |
| keep | kept | kept | | wear | wore | worn |
| know | knew | known | | win | won | won |
| lead | led | led | | write | wrote | written |

---

# 36. If You Only Remember 50 Things About English

The highest-value rules from this entire document. If you internalize these, you will communicate clearly and sound competent.

### Structure

**1.** English is **Subject + Verb + Object**, and that order is not negotiable. *I fixed the bug*, never *The bug I fixed*.

**2.** **Always include the subject.** ❌ *Is raining* → ✅ ***It*** *is raining*. English invents subjects (`it`, `there`) when there isn't one.

**3.** **`There is / there are`** for existence, not `have`. ❌ *Have a problem* → ✅ ***There's*** *a problem*.

**4.** **Adjectives go before nouns** and never take `-s`. *Two **important** meetings*, not *two importants meetings*.

**5.** **Never separate a verb from its object.** ❌ *I like very much this* → ✅ *I really like this* / *I like this very much*.

**6.** **Adverbs of frequency go before the main verb, after `be`.** *I **always** check* / *He **is always** late*.

**7.** **Time and place go at the end,** place before time: *I'll meet you **at the office at 3**.*

### Verbs and auxiliaries

**8.** **Questions and negatives need an auxiliary.** No auxiliary in the sentence? Add `do/does/did`. *Where **do** you work?*

**9.** **The auxiliary carries the tense; the main verb goes to base form.** ❌ *Did you went?* → ✅ *Did you **go**?*

**10.** **Don't forget the third-person `-s`.** *He **works** here* — small, meaningless, and immediately noticed.

**11.** **Answer with the auxiliary, not the verb.** *"Do you like it?"* — *"Yes, I **do**."*

**12.** **A specific finished time forces Past Simple.** *Yesterday I **finished** it*, never *I have finished it yesterday*.

**13.** **`for` + duration, `since` + starting point, with Present Perfect.** *I'**ve worked** here **for** five years* — not *I work here since five years*.

**14.** **No `will` after `if, when, as soon as, before, after, until`.** *When I **arrive**, I'll call you.*

**15.** **`used to + verb`** (past habit) ≠ **`be used to + -ing`** (accustomed). *I **used to work** late* / *I'**m used to working** late*.

**16.** **State verbs don't take `-ing`.** ❌ *I'm needing / I'm knowing* → ✅ *I **need** / I **know***.

**17.** **Modals take the base verb: no `to`, no `-s`.** *She **can help***, not *She cans to help*.

**18.** **`mustn't` (forbidden) ≠ `don't have to` (optional).** These are opposites — get it right.

**19.** **`should have + PP`** for regret: *I **should have** tested it first.*

### Words that change meaning

**20.** **`too` ≠ `very`.** *Too expensive* = we can't afford it. *Very expensive* = it costs a lot.

**21.** **`by` = deadline, `until` = continuing.** *I need it **by** Friday* ≠ *I need it **until** Friday*.

**22.** **`hard` = with effort, `hardly` = almost not.** *He works **hard*** vs *He **hardly** works*.

**23.** **`borrow` = take from, `lend` = give to.** *Can you **lend** me your charger?*

**24.** **`remind` = cause someone to remember, `remember` = have it in your head.** ***Remind** me to call him.*

**25.** **`actually` = na verdade, not "atualmente."** For "atualmente," say **currently**.

**26.** **`pretend` = fingir, `realize` = perceber.** For "pretender," say **intend/plan**. For "realizar," say **hold/carry out**.

**27.** **`make` = create, `do` = perform.** *Make a decision, make a mistake, do business, do research.*

**28.** **Ask a question, take a look, have a meeting.** Never *make a question*, *give a look*, or *do a meeting*.

**29.** **`I have a question`, not `I have a doubt`.**

**30.** **`tell` needs a person; `say` doesn't.** *He **told me*** / *He **said** that...*

**31.** **`-ing` describes the thing, `-ed` describes the person.** *The meeting is **boring**; I am **bored**.*

**32.** **`really` modifies verbs; `very` doesn't.** ❌ *I very like it* → ✅ *I **really** like it*.

### Small words with big impact

**33.** **No article for general statements.** *I like **music***, *Life is short*, ***Brazil** is big* — not *the music*, *the life*, *the Brazil*.

**34.** **You need `a/an` before professions.** *She's **a** doctor.*

**35.** **`go home`, `go to work`, `at home`** — no article, and no `to` before `home`.

**36.** **`information`, `advice`, `news`, `feedback`, `work`, `furniture` are uncountable.** No plural, no `a`.

**37.** **`how many` for countable, `how much` for uncountable.** *How **many** people?* / *How **much** time?*

**38.** **One negative per clause.** ❌ *I didn't see nobody* → ✅ *I didn't see **anyone***.

**39.** **Prepositions are memorized, not translated:** *depend **on**, listen **to**, wait **for**, interested **in**, good **at**, married **to**, arrive **at/in***.

**40.** **These verbs take no preposition:** *discuss, enter, answer, marry, call, join, attend*. ❌ *discuss about*.

**41.** **`its` = possessive, `it's` = it is.** The apostrophe never means possession here.

### Communication

**42.** **In indirect questions, use statement order.** *Do you know where **he is**?* — not *where is he*.

**43.** **Softeners are grammar, not decoration.** *Could you...* is not optional politeness in English — the direct imperative sounds harsh.

**44.** **Disagree indirectly:** *"I see your point, but..."* not *"You're wrong."*

**45.** **Contractions are normal English.** *I'm, don't, it's, we'll* — in speech and in most writing. Avoiding them sounds stiff.

**46.** **Understand `gonna`, `wanna`, `kinda`, `dunno` — but write the full forms.** They are pronunciations, not words.

**47.** **Native speech compresses everything unstressed.** You won't hear every word, and you don't need to. Grab the content words.

**48.** **Asking for repetition is a skill, not a failure.** *"Sorry, could you say that again?"* is better than guessing.

**49.** **Short sentences win.** In writing and speaking, one idea per sentence beats one paragraph per sentence.

**50.** **Learn phrases, not words.** *"Make a decision," "meet a deadline," "look into it," "get back to you"* — chunks give you grammar, prepositions, and naturalness for free.

---

## Final note

Fluency is not the absence of mistakes. It is the ability to say what you mean, be understood, and keep the conversation moving.

The learner who says *"I have a doubt about the deadline — can we discuss about it?"* with confidence and good rhythm communicates better than the one who constructs a grammatically perfect sentence after eight seconds of silence.

Fix the mistakes that change meaning. Learn the chunks that native speakers actually use. Listen far more than you think you need to. Everything else is detail.

**Now go use it.**
